# Sources

## Sources used for rev0709

Current notes:

- `840-preemption-defaults-for-ideal-governments-of-each-scope-local-headroom-metro-primacy-federal-bargain-locks-national-floors-and-treaty-bounded-override.md`

These sources informed the new preemption-defaults note in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Ideal governments by scope should use different preemption defaults: micro-local voice without tiny veto sovereignty, municipal headroom above common floors, metropolitan primacy inside real shared systems, constitutionally real regional autonomy, national floor-setting with a higher burden for ceilings or field occupation, and explicit treaty-bound override above the state

- International IDEA. *The Division of Powers in Federations* and *Federalism* — especially the insistence that federated units enjoy constitutionally protected autonomy and that division of powers is part self-government and part shared rule, which supports treating regional override as a bargain question rather than ambient central privilege.
- OECD. *Assigning responsibilities across levels of government* — especially the emphasis that most responsibilities are shared across levels, that clarity of roles matters more than chasing one abstract ideal level, and that defence or macro-stabilization are among the rare generally central competences, which supports differentiated override design rather than one undifferentiated supremacy rule.
- OECD. *Multi-Level Regulatory Governance* — especially the insistence that roles and responsibilities should be clearly defined to avoid overlap and duplication, which supports explicit preemption and system-primacy rules rather than drift by guidance, platform, or administrative habit.
- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 8, which allows administrative supervision only according to law, normally for legality and constitutional principles, and requires proportionality of intervention, which supports distinguishing legality supervision from general upper-tier managerial entitlement.
- OHCHR. *Cities, local and regional governments and human rights* — especially the statement that government obligations under international human-rights law extend to all levels and branches, which supports keeping national and treaty-level rights floors real without treating local and regional implementation as constitutionally optional.
- United Nations. *Chapter XVI: Article 103 — Charter of the United Nations* and *Vienna Convention on the Law of Treaties (1969)* — especially the treatment of Charter obligations as prevailing in conflict and treaties as binding in good faith without internal-law excuse, which supports making continental or global override depend on explicit treaty commitment and named legal effect rather than upper-tier atmosphere.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic preemption-defaults note derived from them and from the archive’s existing work on subsidiarity, silent override, concurrent competences, metropolitan primacy, local self-government, and treaty effect, translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0708

Current notes:

- `839-amendment-defaults-for-ideal-governments-of-each-scope-local-bylaws-municipal-charters-metro-double-keys-federal-entrenchment-and-treaty-ratification.md`

These sources informed the new amendment-defaults note in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Ideal governments by scope should use different amendment defaults: cheap public rule change below the municipality, true municipal charter lanes with consultation for boundary or status change, double-key metro and federal amendment where shared rule is real, national repairable entrenchment, and treaty-bound upper-tier amendment by explicit text and ratification

- International IDEA. *Constitutional Amendment Procedures* — especially the treatment of amendment formulas as combinations of legislative supermajorities, referendums, double-decision rules, and reference to constituent territorial units, which supports differentiating amendment thickness by settlement class rather than pretending one procedure fits every change.
- Venice Commission / Council of Europe. *Report on Constitutional Amendment* — especially the warning against both excessively flexible and excessively rigid amendment procedures, which supports designing national and subnational amendment lanes that block convenience rewrites without making lawful repair impossible.
- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 4.6 on timely consultation of local authorities on matters that concern them directly and Article 5 on prior consultation of local communities for changes in local authority boundaries, which supports giving municipalities real consultation and boundary-protection discipline rather than treating them as remappable administrative furniture.
- International IDEA. *Federalism* — especially the insistence that constituent units need workable structures of government through which their powers are exercised, which supports giving real regional or state self-government an entrenched amendment lane instead of leaving it fully at the mercy of ordinary central law.
- United Nations. *Chapter XVIII: Articles 108 and 109 — Charter of the United Nations* — especially the treatment of Charter amendment and review as explicit text-change and ratification processes, which supports keeping upper-tier settlement change formal, named, and consent-based rather than ambient.
- United Nations. *Vienna Convention on the Law of Treaties (1969)* — especially Articles 39 and 40 on amendment of treaties and the participation rights of all contracting states in proposals to amend multilateral treaties, which supports treating continental and global amendment as explicit treaty work rather than secretariat drift, donor leverage, or executive side deal.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic amendment-defaults note derived from them and from the archive’s existing work on constitutional change, territorial reorganization, ratification, federalism, and treaty review, translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0707

Current notes:

- `838-guarantee-defaults-for-ideal-governments-of-each-scope-local-dignity-municipal-nondiscrimination-regional-implementation-national-equal-citizenship-and-treaty-backstops.md`

These sources informed the new guarantee-defaults note in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Ideal governments by scope should use different guarantee defaults: local dignity and witness, municipal non-discrimination and fair-service duties, territorial implementation guarantees where systems are real, national equal-citizenship floors, and treaty-bound backstops rather than rights vapor or world-guarantor fantasy

- OHCHR. *Cities, local and regional governments and human rights* — especially the insistence that government obligations under international human rights law extend to all levels and branches and that local governments are at the forefront of bringing human rights to all, which supports giving local and municipal tiers real implementation duties instead of treating rights as a purely national abstraction.
- Congress of Local and Regional Authorities / Council of Europe. *Human Rights at local and regional levels* — especially the treatment of rooting human rights at the local level as part of good governance and the call for better sharing of responsibilities across local, regional, national, and international authorities, which supports a multi-level guarantee ladder rather than a single-center myth.
- International IDEA. *A Practical Guide to Constitution Building* — especially the pairing of **Building a Culture of Human Rights** with **Decentralized Forms of Government**, which supports treating rights guarantees and territorial design as one constitutional problem rather than two disconnected literatures.
- International IDEA. *Federalism* — especially the warning that subnational design can protect diversity while a strong center may still be needed to protect locally concentrated minorities and ensure equal protection of the law, which supports assigning the thickest equal-citizenship floor mainly to national level even where subnational implementation is real.
- OHCHR. *Principles relating to the Status of National Institutions (The Paris Principles)* — especially the insistence that a national institution should have as broad a mandate as possible, set in constitutional or legislative text, which supports keeping the strongest rights-monitoring and harmonization infrastructure at national scale even in a multi-level polity.
- OHCHR. *Treaty Bodies* and *Complaints about human rights violations* — especially the treatment of treaty bodies as committees of independent experts that monitor implementation of the core treaties, plus the existence of individual communications, state-to-state complaints, and inquiries, which supports understanding global rights guarantee mainly as monitoring, complaints, and backstop architecture rather than direct everyday administration.
- Council of Europe / ECHR. *The European Convention on Human Rights* and related overview material — especially the treatment of the Convention as making rights binding and securing them to everyone within a state’s jurisdiction, which supports using continental or plurilateral rights systems as real but subsidiary backstops once states have genuinely bound themselves.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic guarantee-defaults note derived from them and from the archive’s existing work on rights institutions, remedy stacks, local democracy, federalism, equality, and treaty backstops, translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0706

Current notes:

- `837-adjudication-defaults-for-ideal-governments-of-each-scope-local-pre-legal-entry-municipal-administrative-justice-regional-ordinary-courts-national-apex-review-and-treaty-tribunals.md`

These sources informed the new adjudication-defaults note in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Ideal governments by scope should use different adjudication defaults: legal entry below the municipality, municipal administrative-justice front doors, metropolitan and regional court routes, national apex review, and treaty-bound tribunals rather than either tiny sovereign courts or apex hoarding

- OHCHR / United Nations. *Basic Principles on the Independence of the Judiciary* — especially the insistence that judicial independence must be guaranteed by the state, that courts decide matters free from improper interference, and that ordinary courts using established procedures should not be displaced by ad hoc tribunals, which supports rejecting micro-local sovereign-court cosplay and municipal self-judging.
- United Nations and the Rule of Law. *Access to Justice* — especially the treatment of access to justice as a basic principle of the rule of law and as the practical condition for hearing voice and exercising rights, which supports putting reachable first-review and entry lanes closer to ordinary people rather than forcing everything upward by distance and cost.
- International IDEA. *A Practical Guide to Constitution Building: The Design of the Judicial Branch* — especially the insistence that judicial design choices must be made in light of broader constitutional structure and safeguards, which supports treating court location and review powers as part of the governance stack rather than as background machinery.
- International IDEA. *Courts in Federal Countries* and *Federalism* — especially the treatment of integrated, dual, and specialist constitutional-review models plus the warning that federal divisions of competence can increase the judiciary’s political role, which supports judging federal court design by clarity of jurisdiction, competence settlement, and rights access rather than by model loyalty.
- OECD. *Administrative justice as the interface between people and institutions* — especially the emphasis that everyday interactions with public services are where most people actually encounter the state, which supports making municipal administrative justice a real front door rather than leaving daily-state disputes to executive self-review or distant constitutional drama.
- CEPEJ / Council of Europe. *Judicial maps to support access to justice within a quality judicial system* — especially the insistence that court geography must balance proximity to users, quality, competence, and cost, which supports locating ordinary first-instance and appellate structures where they remain both reachable and institutionally serious.
- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 11 on the right of local authorities to recourse to a judicial remedy, which supports visible legality and competence-review routes for municipalities and regions rather than pure ministry grace.
- International Court of Justice. *How the Court Works* and the United Nations Charter, Chapter XIV — especially the treatment of the ICJ as a principal judicial organ for legal disputes between states and advisory opinions, which supports keeping global adjudication sparse, real, and treaty-bound rather than either absent or inflated into a court of everything.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic adjudication-defaults note derived from them and from the archive’s existing work on remedy stacks, judicial design, local self-government, dispute routing, and cross-boundary review, translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0704

Current notes:

- `835-emergency-defaults-for-ideal-governments-of-each-scope-local-readiness-municipal-incident-command-regional-civil-protection-national-derogation-discipline-and-treaty-alerting.md`

These sources informed the new emergency-defaults note in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Ideal governments by scope should use different emergency defaults: local readiness and mutual aid, municipal incident command, metropolitan shared-system disruption management, regional civil protection, national derogation discipline, and treaty-bound upper-tier alerting rather than one panic-state fetish

- International IDEA. *Emergency Powers* — especially the insistence that exceptional powers must remain legally structured and bounded, which supports keeping the thickest formal emergency lane mainly at national scale under strict discipline rather than spreading exception sovereignty downward by fear.
- Venice Commission / Council of Europe. *Compilation on states of emergency* and related standards materials — especially the insistence that emergency powers remain temporary, necessary, proportionate, reviewable by parliament and courts, and compatible with the rule of law, which supports building declaration, renewal, and handback brakes directly into the national emergency lane.
- OECD. *Risk governance* and *The Changing Face of Strategic Crisis Management* — especially the emphasis that complex crises are transboundary and require co-ordination across parts of government, which supports distinguishing municipal, metropolitan, regional, and national crisis roles instead of pretending one tier can honestly command every disruption.
- OECD. *The territorial impact of COVID-19: Managing the crisis across levels of government* — especially the evidence that crisis management and recovery are territorially uneven and require coherent multi-level design, which supports metropolitan and regional emergency roles once shared urban and territorial systems are real.
- UNDRR. *Local government powers for disaster risk reduction* — especially the treatment of local governments as key actors for planning, resilience, and disaster management while showing that some capacities remain shared or limited, which supports local readiness and municipal operational roles without collapsing every emergency lane into local sovereignty.
- WHO. *International Health Regulations (2005)* — especially the distinction between state implementation duties and WHO’s global early-warning and co-ordination functions, which supports treaty-bound upper-tier alerting and response support rather than a general global emergency executive.
- United Nations. *United Nations Charter (full text)* and Chapter VII materials — especially the chartered basis for collective action at upper tiers, which supports keeping continental and global emergency powers explicit, delegated, and exceptional rather than ambient.
- OCHA / UNDAC materials — especially the treatment of international disaster response as a co-ordination and surge-support system, which supports upper-tier pooled assistance and operational support without implying a standing world government of crisis life.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic emergency-defaults note derived from them and from the archive’s existing work on emergency homes, mode packets, emergency powers, stress tests, intervention ladders, and cross-boundary continuity, translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0703

Current notes:

- `834-information-defaults-for-ideal-governments-of-each-scope-local-ledgers-municipal-record-homes-metro-observatories-national-statistics-and-treaty-registries.md`

These sources informed the new information-defaults note in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Ideal governments by scope should use different information defaults: local ledgers, municipal record homes, metropolitan observatories, national statistics spines, and treaty-bound registries rather than one dashboard fetish

- United Nations Statistics Division. *The Fundamental Principles of Official Statistics* — especially the treatment of official statistics as an indispensable element in the information system of a democratic society, which supports keeping authoritative public facts inside method-bound, impartial, and accessible lanes rather than political messaging.
- OECD Local Data Portal. *Methodology and sources* — especially the insistence that subnational governments face challenges requiring data-driven decisions and that local indicators need explicit methods, sources, and territorial definitions, which supports metropolitan and regional observatories with publication discipline rather than improvised dashboards.
- OECD. *Government at a Glance 2025 — Transparency of public information* — especially the emphasis on proactive disclosure of clear, complete, timely, reliable, and relevant public-sector data and information, which supports treating publication calendars, access rights, and standard datasets as part of the constitutional information kit.
- Council of Europe. *Transparency and open government* — especially the treatment of access to information, open data, and records management as prerequisites for participation, accountability, and better local and regional decision-making, which supports a full municipal records and publication constitution.
- Council of Europe Convention on Access to Official Documents monitoring materials — especially the insistence that authorities should manage documents efficiently so they are easily accessible, which supports named record homes rather than retrospective document scavenging.
- UN-Habitat. *Urban Observatories* and *A Guide to Setting up an Urban Observatory* — especially the treatment of the urban observatory model as a way to strengthen city-level data monitoring, collection, and analysis, which supports metropolitan information institutions once shared systems become real.
- World Bank. *The Government Analytics Handbook* — especially the emphasis that governments already generate rich administrative records and can repurpose them to diagnose and improve public administration, which supports building usable municipal, regional, and national data infrastructures out of real operational records rather than presentation theater.

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic information-defaults note derived from them and from the archive’s existing work on local democracy, open government, official statistics, urban observatories, publication discipline, and cross-boundary information chains, translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0702

Current notes:

- `833-coercion-defaults-for-ideal-governments-of-each-scope-local-witness-municipal-civil-enforcement-regional-police-platforms-national-force-monopoly-and-treaty-bound-restraint.md`

These sources informed the new coercion-defaults note in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Ideal governments by scope should use different coercion defaults: local witness and referral, municipal civil enforcement, bounded metropolitan coercion only where shared systems require it, typed regional police and emergency platforms, a strongly bounded national force monopoly, and treaty-bound upper-tier restraint

- International IDEA. *Local Democracy* — especially the treatment of local democracy as the decentralization of power and responsibility to democratic institutions at village, municipal, city, town, district, and regional levels, which supports keeping genuinely micro-local bodies inside witness and stewardship lanes rather than treating every level as a full sovereign force holder.
- International IDEA. *Federalism* — especially the treatment of divided powers and overlapping constitutional levels, which supports assigning police, regulatory, and emergency platforms by real constitutional competence rather than by prestige symmetry.
- International IDEA. *Emergency Powers* — especially the insistence that emergency concentration may be necessary yet must remain temporally bounded and legally structured, which supports reserving the heaviest exceptional coercive powers mainly to the national tier under strict handback discipline.
- OECD. *Assigning responsibilities across levels of government* — especially the distinction among local, regional, and national responsibility patterns and the importance of clear assignment across levels, which supports separating municipal civil-enforcement lanes from regional and national coercive platforms.
- OECD. *Governing the City* — especially the evidence that fragmented metropolitan systems fail when institutions do not match functional urban reality, which supports bounded metropolitan enforcement and emergency-coordination powers where shared urban systems genuinely require them.
- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the treatment of regions as strategic territorial co-ordination and platform governments, which supports typed regional police, disaster, and regulatory roles where the constitutional order honestly places them.
- OHCHR / United Nations. *Basic Principles on the Use of Force and Firearms by Law Enforcement Officials* — especially the insistence that force may be used only when strictly necessary and to the extent required, which supports treating policing and force as exceptional, law-bound lanes rather than casual local prestige badges.
- OHCHR / United Nations. *Code of Conduct for Law Enforcement Officials* — especially the treatment of force and firearms as extreme measures subject to necessity and reporting duties, which supports tightening review and record discipline as coercive intensity rises.
- United Nations. *United Nations Charter (full text)* and *Chapter VII* — especially the chartered and exceptional basis for sanctions and enforcement action at upper tiers, which supports upper-tier coercion remaining treaty-bound, typed, and reviewable rather than drifting into ambient world-police sovereignty.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic coercion-defaults note derived from them and from the archive’s existing work on local democracy, decentralization, metropolitan governance, emergency powers, regulatory boundaries, oversight, and treaty enforcement, translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0701

Current notes:

- `832-oversight-defaults-for-ideal-governments-of-each-scope-local-witness-municipal-scrutiny-metro-audit-national-watchdogs-and-treaty-review.md`

These sources informed the new oversight-defaults note in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Ideal governments by scope should use different oversight defaults: micro-local witness and escalation, municipal scrutiny and audit, metropolitan and regional correction chains that live at their own level, differentiated national watchdogs, and treaty-bound upper-tier review

- International IDEA. *Local Democracy* — especially the treatment of local democratic institutions as accountable self-government rather than merely symbolic participation, which supports distinguishing micro-local witness bodies from municipalities that need a full correction chain.
- Council of Europe. *12 Principles of Good Democratic Governance* — especially the insistence that local authorities operate inside clear accountability, transparency, and remedy frameworks, which supports treating municipal and regional oversight as constitutional infrastructure rather than an optional add-on.
- International IDEA. *Independent Institutions: Enhancing Democratic Integrity and Accountability through Constitutional Design* — especially the treatment of fourth-branch institutions such as auditors-general, ombudspersons, and other watchdogs as distinct integrity devices with specific lanes, which supports differentiated national and upper-tier oversight rather than one mascot super-agency.
- INTOSAI. *ISSAI 100 – Fundamental Principles of Public-Sector Auditing* — especially the argument that public-sector auditing provides legislative and oversight bodies and the general public with independent and objective assessments, which supports independent external audit as part of the ordinary correction chain rather than managerial self-report.
- OECD. *Supreme Audit Institutions and Good Governance* — especially the framing of audit as oversight, insight, and foresight for complex government systems, which supports giving metropolitan, regional, and national governments real external audit rather than decorative compliance rituals.
- OECD. *Governing the City* — especially the evidence that fragmented metropolitan systems fail when institutions do not match functional urban reality, which supports matching metropolitan scrutiny and complaint routes to metropolitan power rather than borrowing them permanently from a core city.
- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2022)2 on democratic accountability of elected representatives and elected bodies at local and regional level* — especially the emphasis on visible procedures, transparency, and accountability for elected local and regional bodies, which supports explicit scrutiny and review lanes at those scales.
- OECD. *Government at a Glance 2025 — Accountable law making* — especially the stress on legislative scrutiny as a core accountability function, which supports keeping chamber oversight visible rather than assuming elections alone do the checking work.
- United Nations. *Office of Internal Oversight Services* and *Coordination with other UN entities* — especially the internal-audit, investigation, inspection, and evaluation functions of OIOS and its coordination with the Board of Auditors and the Joint Inspection Unit, which supports treaty-bound upper-tier review through several distinct lanes rather than secretariat self-marking.
- United Nations. *United Nations Charter (full text)* — especially the treaty-based and delegated character of the Organization, which supports upper-tier oversight remaining bounded, published, and chartered rather than pretending there is already a full sovereign world watchdog constitution.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic oversight-defaults note derived from them and from the archive’s existing work on local democracy, public integrity, cross-boundary oversight chains, complaint routes, audit, and treaty review, translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0699

Current notes:

- `830-decision-rule-defaults-for-ideal-governments-of-each-scope-local-majorities-municipal-ordinary-rules-federal-double-keys-and-no-fake-global-unanimity.md`

These sources informed the new decision-rule-defaults note in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Ideal governments by scope should use different internal vote grammars: cheap local consensus-seeking, ordinary-majority municipal and metropolitan government, reserved settlement-protecting supermajorities, genuine federal double keys, and no fake global unanimity

- International IDEA. *Local Democracy* — especially the treatment of local democratic institutions as varied and scale-sensitive, which supports keeping micro-local decision rules cheap and legible instead of importing full parliamentary theater into steward bodies.
- International IDEA. *Opposition and Legislative Minorities: Constitutional Roles, Rights and Recognition* — especially the insistence that democracy is not crude majority rule and that minorities need structured rights without making a country ungovernable, which supports separating minority-protection procedures from minority-veto constitutions.
- International IDEA. *Bicameralism* — especially the discussion of concurrent majorities and qualified-majority devices for protecting territorially or communally structured settlements, which supports using double keys only where a genuine shared-rule bargain exists.
- International IDEA. *Constitutional Amendment Procedures* — especially the treatment of supermajorities and double-decision rules as reserved devices for constitutional change, which supports keeping higher thresholds attached to settlement-level questions rather than ordinary governing.
- OECD. *Governing the City* — especially the evidence that metropolitan reforms fail when fragmented institutions cannot move regional systems, which supports refusing confederal veto habits once a metropolitan government has real binding work to do.
- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the distinction among regional governance models and institutional settings, which supports matching regional vote grammar to real platform governance and shared-rule design rather than to prestige symmetry.
- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2022)2 on democratic accountability of elected representatives and elected bodies at local and regional level* — especially the emphasis on accountability frameworks, procedures, and transparency for elected bodies, which supports publishing threshold classes and decision rules clearly.
- United Nations. *Chapter IV: The General Assembly (Articles 9–22)* — especially the distinction between ordinary questions and important questions decided by different thresholds, which supports reserved higher-threshold classes instead of one undifferentiated rule.
- United Nations. *How Decisions are Made at the UN* — especially the distinction between consensus adoption and formal voting, which supports treating consensus as a method of accommodation rather than as proof that disagreement vanished.
- United Nations. *Rules of Procedure* — especially the explicit quorum and voting rules for deliberative bodies, which supports public clarity about which threshold applied and why.

## Sources used for rev0698

Current notes:

- `829-selection-defaults-for-ideal-governments-of-each-scope-open-local-voice-proportional-councils-metro-wide-elections-federal-shared-rule-and-delegated-global-seats.md`

These sources informed the new selection-defaults note in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Ideal governments by scope should use different selection defaults: open local voice, proportional municipal councils, whole-metro elections, real territorial shared-rule keys, and delegated upper-tier seats rather than one ballot fetish

- International IDEA. *Local Democracy* — especially the treatment of local democracy as democratic self-government through elected councils, mayors, and other local institutions, which supports keeping selection rules scale-sensitive rather than copying one electoral grammar downwards.
- International IDEA. *Democracy at the Local Level* — especially the practical emphasis on participation, representation, and conflict management in local institutions, which supports low-barrier, legible local selection rather than overengineered miniature national politics.
- International IDEA. *Electoral System Design: The International IDEA Handbook* — especially the insistence that electoral-system choice is one of the most important institutional decisions in any democracy, which supports treating municipal, regional, and national selection rules as constitutional fit questions rather than defaults inherited by habit.
- International IDEA. *Electoral system design in the context of constitution-building* — especially the warning that electoral design choices sit inside broader political settlements, which supports aligning selection rules with regime form and mandate source rather than choosing them in isolation.
- ACE Electoral Knowledge Network. *Electoral Systems* — especially the account of electoral systems as translation devices between votes and seats, which supports differentiating proportional, mixed, and direct-voice lanes by scope instead of treating them as moral badges.
- ACE Electoral Knowledge Network. *Practical Advice for Electoral System Designers* — especially the preference for moderate and context-aware reform over wholesale symbolic redesign, which supports the archive’s scale-fit rather than prestige-fit approach to selection.
- OECD. *Governing the City* — especially the evidence that metropolitan governance must match the real functional urban system, which supports direct metro-wide selection when a metropolitan body holds substantive powers.
- OECD. *The OECD Metropolitan Governance Survey* — especially the observation that metropolitan governance bodies are common but often weak, which supports insisting on visible whole-territory legitimacy once metropolitan power becomes real.
- International IDEA. *Bicameralism* — especially the distinction between lower chambers representing population and upper chambers serving more bounded territorial or review roles, which supports refusing duplicated chamber selection.
- International IDEA. *Second Chambers in Federal Systems* — especially the treatment of upper chambers as devices for shared rule by constituent units, which supports territorial second keys only where the federal bargain is genuine.
- International IDEA. *Government Formation and Removal Mechanisms* — especially the emphasis on confidence relationships in parliamentary systems, which supports keeping executive selection aligned with chamber-centered government where that is the regime form.
- United Nations. *United Nations Charter (full text)* — especially the chartered and delegated basis of global institutions, which supports delegated and merit-based upper-tier selection rather than counterfeit world-state election design.

## Sources used for rev0697

These sources informed the new mandate-exit-defaults note in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Ideal governments by scope should use different mandate-exit defaults: simple local recall, municipal executive dismissal, metro and regional confidence-loss grammar, bounded national dissolution, and upper-tier treaty-lapse discipline

- International IDEA. *Local Democracy* — especially the treatment of local democracy as democratic self-government across village, municipal, city, town, district, and regional levels, which supports scale-sensitive exit rules instead of copying one removal grammar across the whole stack.
- International IDEA. *Direct Democracy* — especially the description of recall as a device through which voters may end an elected official’s term once signature and ballot thresholds are met, which supports treating recall as a distinct exit tool rather than as a synonym for ordinary elections.
- Venice Commission. *Report on the Recall of Mayors and Local Elected Representatives* — especially the distinction between representative free mandate and executive recall, and the warning that recall should remain strictly regulated and exceptional to protect stability.
- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the local-democracy baseline that councils and assemblies are freely elected and may possess executive organs responsible to them, which supports council-centered municipal exit logic and caution about casual imperative mandate.
- International IDEA. *Government Formation and Removal Mechanisms* — especially the emphasis on visible confidence chains and parliamentary removal design, which supports successor-first and confidence-loss defaults at heavier territorial scales.
- International IDEA. *Dissolution of Parliament* — especially the warning that broad discretionary dissolution can restrain parliament and distort no-confidence, which supports the archive’s snap-brake rather than private-weapon view of dissolution.
- Venice Commission. *Code of Good Practice in Electoral Matters* — especially the insistence on regular elections and stability of the rules, which supports bounded exit design instead of constant plebiscitary churn.
- OECD. *Governing the City* — especially the account of metropolitan governance as a response to fragmentation in transport and spatial planning, which supports the need for explicit confidence-loss and continuity grammar once metropolitan power is real.
- United Nations. *United Nations Charter (full text)* — especially the chartered, delegated nature of upper-tier global authority, which supports mandate-bound rather than ambient exit logic for global institutions.
- United Nations Security Council Secretariat Branch. *Security Council Reporting and mandate cycles* — especially the regular pattern of mandate renewal, review, and lapse management, which supports explicit reauthorization and non-drift clocks for upper-tier delegated bodies.

## Sources used for rev0696

- `827-mandate-duration-defaults-for-ideal-governments-of-each-scope-short-local-rotation-municipal-and-metro-stability-national-renewal-windows-and-treaty-review-clocks.md`

These sources informed the new mandate-duration-defaults note in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Ideal governments by scope should use different mandate-duration defaults: short and recallable micro-local windows, medium municipal and metropolitan terms, ordinary regional and national renewal bands, and upper-tier review clocks rather than ambient permanence

- International IDEA. *Local Democracy* — especially the treatment of local democracy as democratic self-government across village, municipal, city, town, district, and regional levels and the observation that constitutions may specify terms for local councils and officials, which supports treating micro-local and municipal mandate duration as a real design choice rather than an afterthought.
- Venice Commission. *Report on the Recall of Mayors and Local Elected Representatives* — especially the account of recall as a mechanism for removing locally elected officials before the end of term, which supports keeping the smallest and most personal local offices easy to correct.
- International IDEA. *Government Formation and Removal Mechanisms* — especially the emphasis on parliamentary governments being selected through parliament and removable by votes of no confidence, which supports tying mandate duration to governability and successor formation rather than to calendar fetish.
- International IDEA. *Dissolution of Parliament* — especially the treatment of dissolution as a power that ends the term of a legislature and triggers new elections, which supports distinguishing ordinary renewal windows from early-deadlock repair and caretaker misuse.
- Venice Commission. *Code of Good Practice in Electoral Matters* — especially the insistence that elections must occur at regular intervals and that a legislative assembly’s term should not exceed five years, which supports the archive’s ordinary outer renewal band for representative legislatures.
- Venice Commission. *Report on Term Limits Part II* — especially the comparative treatment of term limits for members of parliament, locally elected representatives, governors, and mayors, which supports separating term-length design from the distinct question of whether hard re-election limits are justified.
- OECD. *Governing the City* — especially the stress on metropolitan governance as a vehicle for transport, land-use, infrastructure, and other long-horizon urban systems, which supports refusing electoral calendars so short that real metropolitan implementation never stabilizes.
- United Nations Sustainable Development Group. *Universal Periodic Review* — especially the recurring peer-review model for all UN member states, which supports the archive’s insistence that upper-tier delegated authority should sit under named review clocks rather than ambient permanence.
- United Nations General Assembly. *A/79/336* — especially the discussion of a predictable schedule of treaty-body reviews, which supports the archive’s broader preference for explicit periodic reinspection of upper-tier delegated mandates.

### Editorial interpretation

These sources informed the new mandate-source-defaults note in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Ideal governments by scope should use different mandate sources: assembly voice for micro-local units, council-centered municipal authority, whole-territory metropolitan public mandates, regional investiture, national confidence government, and treaty- or charter-derived delegation above the state

- International IDEA. *Local Democracy* — especially the treatment of local democracy as decentralization of power and responsibility to democratic institutions at village, municipal, city, town, district, and regional levels, which supports treating micro-local and municipal authority as real democratic layers without inflating them into uniform plebiscitary executive systems.
- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the insistence that local authorities should elect their bodies and possess real self-government, which supports council-centered municipal democratic authority rather than purely decorative local elections.
- OECD. *Governing the City* — especially the treatment of metropolitan governance as a response to functional urban interdependence, which supports insisting on a whole-territory public mandate once metropolitan powers become materially binding.
- World Bank. *International Practices of Metropolitan Governance* — especially the comparative catalogue of metropolitan arrangements, which supports distinguishing light delegated co-ordination from metropolitan institutions that need their own public authorization.
- Council of Europe. *Regional democracy - Council of Europe Reference Framework* — especially the account of democratic regional structure, organization, competences, own resources, and relations within the state, which supports treating regions as democratic tiers where justified without assuming they must copy national presidential mandates.
- International IDEA. *Government Formation and Removal Mechanisms* — especially the focus on confidence, investiture, dismissal, and deadlock repair, which supports the archive’s default of assembly-invested regional government and parliamentary-confidence national government.
- International IDEA. *Federalism* — especially the distinction between shared rule and self-rule across levels, which supports treating some regional and federal mandate chains as mixed rather than as simple copies of one national template.
- United Nations. *United Nations Charter (full text)* — especially the Charter’s role as a bounded constitutional instrument grounded in member-state commitment rather than a world-state ballot, which supports the archive’s default of treaty- or charter-derived authority at global scale.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. These sources informed the new regime-form-defaults note in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Ideal governments by scope should use different regime-form defaults: steward assemblies for micro-local units, council-plus-administration municipalities, directly legitimated metros, assembly-confidence regions, parliamentary-default nations, bounded plurilateral unions, and global narrow-waist institutions

- International IDEA. *Local Democracy* — especially the treatment of local democracy as democratic self-government for cities, towns, villages, and districts, which supports a real municipal democratic tier while keeping the smallest units close to participatory stewardship rather than inflated sovereign form.
- OECD. *Governing the City* — especially the typology of metropolitan governance arrangements and the emphasis on co-ordination in transport and spatial planning, which supports directly legible metropolitan government when urban systems are functionally shared.
- World Bank. *International Practices of Metropolitan Governance* — especially the comparative catalogue of collaborative metropolitan arrangements, which supports the archive’s distinction between metropolitan bargaining, bounded authorities, and directly legitimated metro institutions.
- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the comparative view of regional governance reforms and the diversity of institutional and financing arrangements, which supports treating regions as conditional platform tiers rather than mandatory symmetry layers.
- International IDEA. *Government Formation and Removal Mechanisms* — especially the focus on constitutional design choices for investiture, confidence, and dismissal, which supports the archive’s parliamentary or assembly-confidence default for most large democratic tiers.
- International IDEA. *Bicameralism* — especially the account of second chambers as devices for subnational representation, scrutiny, and review rather than automatic democratic furniture, which supports a no-duplication baseline.
- International IDEA. *Second Chambers in Federal Systems* — especially the treatment of upper houses as shared-rule instruments in federations, which supports using bicameralism where territorial bargain and shared rule are real rather than by prestige.
- International IDEA. *Non-Executive Presidents in Parliamentary Democracies* — especially the account of symbolic heads of state with limited arbitral roles, which supports separating ceremonial continuity from ordinary executive government.
- International IDEA. *Constitutional Monarchs in Parliamentary Democracies* — especially the treatment of constitutional monarchs as representative and civic heads of state rather than policymakers, which supports the archive’s rule against duplicate democratic apexes.
- United Nations. *United Nations Charter (full text)* — especially the Charter’s role as a bounded institutional constitution for global co-ordination rather than a world-state blueprint, which supports the archive’s narrow-waist global default.

These sources informed the new regulatory-and-enforcement-boundary-chain-template note in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Cross-boundary forms should carry regulatory-and-enforcement-boundary chains proportionate to their gravity: signal routing for connectors, principal-owned coercive acts with pooled support for backbones, remit-bounded licensing and enforcement for functional authorities, and fuller coercive constitutions for real territorial tiers

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government (ETS No. 122)* — especially Article 8’s requirement that administrative supervision rest on law, normally target legality rather than expediency, and remain proportionate, which supports visible legal title and proportionate coercive design rather than host-badge improvisation.
- Council of Europe. *Explanatory Report to the European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the distinction between legality supervision, delegated-task expediency control, and proportionate oversight, which supports keeping cross-boundary coercive powers explicit and law-bound.
- OECD. *Regulatory Enforcement and Inspections* — especially the principles of evidence-based enforcement, selectivity, risk focus and proportionality, co-ordination and consolidation, information integration, transparent governance, and clear and fair process, which support risk-based inspections rather than indiscriminate permit and enforcement sprawl.
- OECD. *OECD Regulatory Enforcement and Inspections Toolkit* — especially the twelve-criterion checklist for assessing inspection and enforcement systems, which supports treating the coercive perimeter as an assessable governance object rather than a black box.
- OECD. *Best practice principles for licensing and permitting* — especially the insistence that licensing be used only where risks are truly high, irreversible, or hard to manage ex post, which supports refusing ambient permit growth as default administrative theater.
- World Bank. *International Practices of Metropolitan Governance* — especially the distinction between metropolitan-scale planning and local implementation, with building permits and much enforcement often remaining local even where structural land-use planning is shared, which supports honest non-centralization of proximity-sensitive coercive acts.
- World Bank Group. *Risk-Based Approaches to Business Regulation: A Note for Reformers* — especially the use of shared registries, integrated inspection management, and co-ordinated risk-based inspectorates, which supports pooled inspection support without assuming that every coercive act must move to one central body.

- Council of Europe / Congress of Local and Regional Authorities. *Recommendation 132 (2003) on municipal property in the light of the principles of the European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the treatment of municipal property as an intrinsic component of local self-government, the insistence that transferred powers should be accompanied by necessary assets, and the warning against burden transfers that cripple the receiving authority, which supports treating asset title and residue as constitutional design rather than bookkeeping.
- European Union / EUR-Lex. *Regulation (EC) No 1082/2006 on a European grouping of territorial cooperation (EGTC) — consolidated text* — especially the requirements to specify members’ financial contributions, liability arrangements, and dissolution conditions, plus the rule that members may remain liable when EGTC assets are insufficient, which supports explicit residue maps for cross-boundary bodies.
- OECD Legal Instruments. *Recommendation of the Council on the Governance of Infrastructure* — especially the life-cycle governance orientation toward how governments fund, budget, deliver, operate, and monitor infrastructure assets, which supports treating asset stewardship as a governance chain rather than a technical afterthought.
- OECD. *Management of asset performance throughout the life cycle* — especially the coupling of asset management with accountability and financial management, which supports ring-fenced lifecycle disciplines rather than host-owned drift.
- OECD. *Enabling Inter-Municipal Shared Service Provision in Lithuania* — especially the treatment of joint asset management, infrastructure ownership, use, accounting, responsibility division, and financing as real inter-municipal governance problems, which supports explicit principal-versus-common-vehicle design rather than improvised shared ownership.
- World Bank. *Municipal Finances: A Handbook for Local Governments* — especially the treatment of municipal asset management, liabilities, and capital planning as core local-governance disciplines, which supports visible asset registers, debt rules, reserve policy, and handback planning rather than orphaned balance sheets.

### Cross-boundary forms should carry administrative chains proportionate to their gravity: small host secretariats for connectors, principal-owned pooled machinery for backbones, chartered operators for narrow authorities, and full civil-service and records homes for territorial tiers

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government (ETS No. 122)* — especially Article 6’s requirements that local authorities be able to determine internal administrative structures adapted to local needs and that local-government employment conditions support merit and competence, which supports matching administrative thickness to real responsibility rather than ruling through borrowed host machinery.
- OECD. *Enabling Inter-Municipal Shared Service Provision in Lithuania* — especially the insistence on clear legal forms and agreements covering functions, responsibilities, structure, cost-sharing, duration, termination, and reporting, together with the specific host-municipality risks highlighted in the report, which supports principal-owned backbones rather than host drift.
- OECD. *The OECD Metropolitan Governance Survey* and *Governing the City* — especially the evidence that metropolitan governance bodies vary materially in legal status, powers, budgets, and staff, which supports treating operator design, staffing thickness, and records home as model-sensitive choices rather than as invisible back-office aftercare.
- World Bank. *International Practices of Metropolitan Governance* — especially the differentiation among collaborative arrangements, advisory bodies, and stronger metropolitan entities, which supports an administrative-chain ladder instead of assuming that all cross-boundary bodies should be run through the same borrowed bureaucracy.

### Cross-boundary forms should carry fiscal chains proportionate to their gravity: connector dues for connectors, principal-owned cost-sharing for backbones, assigned revenues and bounded capital lanes for narrow authorities, and visible budget constitutions with equalization for territorial tiers

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government (ETS No. 122)* — especially Article 9’s requirements that local resources be adequate, commensurate, partly own-source, sufficiently diversified and buoyant, and that borrowing be available mainly for capital purposes, which supports matching fiscal thickness to real responsibility rather than funding public power through improvised pleas or hidden debt.
- OECD Legal Instruments. *Recommendation of the Council on Effective Public Investment Across Levels of Government* — especially the insistence that coordination and capacity should match the relevant geographic scale, which supports treating revenue and capital design as part of institutional honesty rather than as aftercare.
- OECD. *Enabling Inter-Municipal Shared Service Provision in Lithuania* — especially the emphasis on clear legal, fiscal, reporting, and cost-sharing arrangements for pooled municipal services, which supports principal-owned backbones rather than host-treasury ambiguity.
- OECD. *OECD Fiscal Decentralisation Database* and *Going Granular with Regional and Municipal Fiscal Data* — especially the insistence on typed revenue, expenditure, and debt structures as the basis for judging real fiscal autonomy or dependence, which supports evidence-led fiscal-chain claims instead of rhetoric about decentralization.
- World Bank. *International Practices of Metropolitan Governance* — especially the distinction among own-source revenues, budget transfers, and member-government contributions across metropolitan arrangements, which supports linking funding mode to institutional form rather than assuming one metro finance model.
- World Bank. *Municipal Finances: A Handbook for Local Governments* — especially the treatment of own-source revenues, transfers, debt, and off-budget exposure as different fiscal tools with different governance implications, which supports a compact fiscal-chain ladder instead of one undifferentiated money story.

### Cross-boundary forms should carry legitimacy chains proportionate to their gravity: delegated councils for connectors, principal-owned governance for shared-service backbones, elected-anchor plus second-key discipline for bounded authorities, and directly elected chambers for general-purpose territorial tiers

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government (ETS No. 122)* — especially the requirement that local self-government be exercised by councils or assemblies composed of freely elected members and that executive organs be responsible to them, which supports a strong elected-anchor default whenever a body is exercising genuine public-government power.
- OECD. *Regions and Cities - Where Policies and People Meet: Policy Briefs* — especially the finding that most metropolitan governance bodies use indirect representation while direct election is rare and associated with the most formalised metropolitan authorities, together with the warning about democratic-deficit concerns as metropolitan powers grow, which supports treating composition as a live design question rather than as administrative aftercare.
- OECD. *Enabling Inter-Municipal Shared Service Provision in Lithuania* — especially the insistence that shared-service arrangements need a clear legal base, specified forms, comprehensive agreements covering functions, responsibilities, structure, composition, cost-sharing, duration, termination, and reporting to member municipalities, which supports principal-owned backbones rather than pooled-service ambiguity.
- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the treatment of directly elected deliberative bodies as a key characteristic of decentralised regional government, which supports directly elected chamber ownership once a body has matured into a general-purpose territorial tier.
- OECD. *Governing the City* — especially the persistent diversity of metropolitan arrangements in legal status, composition, power, budget, and staff, which supports a legitimacy-chain ladder that matches composition and second-key design to the actual gravity of the form rather than copying one metropolitan model everywhere.

## Sources used for rev0679

Current notes:

- `810-threshold-dossiers-for-ideal-governments-of-each-scope-metro-triggers-county-pooling-regional-platform-proof-fiscal-floors-legitimacy-thickness-and-no-tier-thickening-by-anecdote.md`

These sources informed the new threshold-dossier note in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Serious ideal-governments-by-scope frontier work should assemble threshold claims through typed dossiers rather than one trigger number, one admired case, or one crisis workaround

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the rule that public responsibilities should generally be exercised by authorities closest to citizens while upward allocation depends on the nature and scale of the task, together with the requirement that local financial resources be commensurate with responsibilities and that equalisation not erase local discretion, which supports threshold dossiers built around vocation, scale, and fiscal non-hollowness rather than around ideology alone.
- OECD. *Definition of Cities and Functional Urban Areas* — especially the treatment of functional urban areas as cities plus economically connected surrounding areas identified through density and travel-to-work flows, which supports requiring metro-threshold work to declare the functional territory under study rather than relying on inherited municipal shells.
- OECD. *Effective Public Investment Toolkit* — especially the insistence that multi-level investment and service performance depend on co-ordination and capacity at the relevant geographical scale, which supports threshold dossiers that test whether fragmentation, pooling, or upper-tier build-out is actually required by the operating footprint of the system.
- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the documented variety of regional arrangements, asymmetry, metropolitan specificity, and capacity-sensitive design, which supports treating regional-platform thresholds and legitimacy-thickness questions as comparator- and morphology-sensitive rather than as one fixed ladder.
- OECD. *Fiscal Decentralisation Database* — especially the availability of comparable indicators on revenue, expenditure, and fiscal structure across levels of government, which supports typed fiscal-floor testing rather than impressionistic claims that a lower tier is either autonomous or hollow.
- World Bank. *International Practices of Metropolitan Governance* — especially the treatment of metropolitan areas as functional economic areas and labor markets and the comparison of alternative metropolitan governance models, which supports separating proof that a metro threshold has been crossed from the later choice among alternative metro institutional designs.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic threshold-assembly note derived from them and from the archive’s existing answer-key, benchmark, comparator, assessment-mission, and research-frontier run, translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

### Serious scope reviews should screen reviewer interests, role limits, and funding pathways before judgment begins

- U.S. Government Accountability Office. *Government Auditing Standards (2024 Revision)* and longstanding GAGAS specialist-independence guidance — especially the treatment of independence as a threshold condition for government review and the rule that work from an impaired specialist should not simply be relied on because the person is technically strong, which supports role typing, recusal, and replacement discipline rather than prestige laundering.
- World Health Organization. *Declaration of interests for experts* — especially the insistence that expert contributions must be actually and ostensibly objective and independent, which supports public-facing role-and-interest discipline for review teams rather than declaration-only theater.
- OECD. *Guidelines for Managing Conflict of Interest in the Public Service* — especially the emphasis on disclosure, consistency, and an appropriate degree of openness in resolving conflicts of interest, which supports visible restriction logic and public-safe integrity notes rather than hidden bilateral vetting.
- Council of Europe / Congress of Local and Regional Authorities. *European Code of Conduct for all Persons Involved in Local and Regional Governance* — especially the insistence on standards of integrity, accountability, disclosure, and public-interest conduct in local and regional governance, which supports secondment firebreaks, host-expert role limits, and no constitutional judgment by interested mission.

### Serious scope reviews should keep calibration packs, moderation checkpoints, and precedent notes so similar cases are judged in a similar way across teams

- U.S. Government Accountability Office. *Government Auditing Standards (2024 Revision)* — especially the requirement that quality-management systems support personnel’s consistent understanding and the consistent implementation of responses to quality risks, which supports score anchors and moderation loops rather than reviewer-by-reviewer drift.
- OECD. *DAC Peer Review Methodology, Updated 2023* and the Global Forum *Handbook for Peer Reviews on Transparency and Exchange of Information on Request* plus *Methodology for Round 2 peer reviews and non-member reviews* — especially the insistence that assessors use common authoritative materials, cross-check against other assessments, and maintain the same approach in order to produce proper, fair, efficient, equitable, and consistent reviews, which supports precedent banks, comparator discipline, and documented departures.
- World Health Organization. *Joint External Evaluation* team-member orientation / roster materials and the *Interim manual for the performance evaluation of regulatory systems* — especially the use of orientation, rostering, and training to ensure consistent understanding of the tool and consistency in quality, which supports calibration packs, hard-case conferences, and explicit recalibration when tools change.

### Administrative procedure should let people bring counsel, nonlawyer help, or a support person rather than forcing fair process to depend on solo navigation

- Council of Europe. *The administration and you — a handbook* — especially Principle 11 on representation and assistance, which supports a typed distinction between being represented, otherwise assisted, and formally authorising someone to act on another’s behalf.
- Council of Europe. *Guidelines on the efficiency and the effectiveness of legal aid schemes in the areas of civil and administrative law* — especially the insistence that access to justice may require legal advice, assistance, and representation rather than bare formal rights.
- European Parliament / European Ombudsman good-administration materials — especially the proposed administrative-procedure text stating that parties should be able to express their views in writing or orally, if necessary, with the assistance of a person of their choice, which supports an explicit support-person and assistance lane.
- U.S. Administrative Procedure Act and related administrative-law practice — especially the classic guarantee that parties may be accompanied, represented, and advised by counsel or other qualified representative, which supports keeping accompaniment and representation visible as ordinary procedural rights rather than luxury add-ons.
- ACUS. *Recommendation 2024-7: Nonlawyer Assistance and Representation in Agency Adjudications* and related project materials — especially the practical focus on qualifying or accrediting nonlawyer representatives and increasing the availability of assistance where lawyer-only systems underserve the public.

### Administrative fairness should be built around impartial decision-makers, workable separation of functions, and strict discipline for off-record merits influence

- Council of Europe. *The administration and you — a handbook* — especially Principle 3 on objectivity and impartiality and its insistence that even the appearance of bias must be avoided and that officials should not decide matters implicating their own interests or appeals from their own decisions.
- OECD / SIGMA. *The Principles of Public Administration* — especially Principle 17’s framing that legality in administrative procedure includes equality, impartiality, right to be heard, notification, reasons, appeal, and effective judicial protection.
- U.S. Administrative Procedure Act — especially the hearing and appearance provisions plus the separation-of-functions logic built into formal adjudication, which support distinguishing case-building from deciding roles in serious contested matters.
- ACUS. *Statement of Principles for Administrative Adjudication* and related adjudication recommendations — especially the current emphasis on prohibiting ex parte merits communications, requiring policies for recusal or disqualification, and preserving separation between adjudicators and investigative or prosecutorial personnel.
- ACUS. *Best Practices for Adjudication Not Involving an Evidentiary Hearing* and related federal-adjudication work — especially the continued insistence that even non-court-style adjudication should discipline ex parte contact, preserve the record, and avoid backstage merits influence.

### Administrative evidence should be gathered and tested fairly rather than outsourced onto the least capable party

- Council of Europe. *The administration and you — a handbook* — especially the treatment of participation, good-time notice, disclosure of facts and arguments, the opportunity to make submissions, and access to documents and files, which supports full adverse-case visibility before decision.
- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2007)7 on good administration* — especially the connected requirements of participation, reasonable time, reasons, notification, remedies, and good-faith legality, which support separating fair investigation from document ambush.
- OECD / SIGMA. *Checklist for a General Law on Administrative Procedures* — especially the explicit prompts on the principle of investigation, evidence, hearing the interested parties, reasons for accepting or rejecting evidence, and the right to examine relevant files.
- OECD. *Procedural Fairness: Issues in Civil and Administrative Enforcement Proceedings* — especially the discussion of case-file access, exculpatory evidence, and opportunities to answer allegations before a decision is taken.
- OECD. *Toolkit for Access to Justice and People-Centred Justice Systems* — especially the emphasis on reducing complexity and administrative burden, which supports making authorities fetch what they already know and assist people through avoidable proof barriers.
- ReNEUAL. *Model Rules on EU Administrative Procedure* — especially the model treatment of investigation, evidence, hearings, and procedural fairness, which supports distinguishing burden of production from burden of official inquiry.

### Administrative guidance should help people comply and officials act consistently without turning manuals and FAQs into hidden law

- OECD. *Regulatory Enforcement and Inspections Toolkit* — especially the insistence that compliance promotion should use guidance, toolkits, and checklists and that inspectors should work with official guidelines to improve consistency, transparency, and fairness.
- OECD. *Best Practice Principles for Licensing and Permitting* (2025) — especially the push for risk-focused, faster, and fairer licensing systems, which supports treating guidance as a public legibility layer rather than a covert burden-shifting device.
- OECD. *Regulatory Policy Outlook 2025* — especially the emphasis on meaningful stakeholder engagement and communication about how regulatory decisions are shaped, which supports consulting or at least openly explaining consequential guidance updates.
- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2007)7 on good administration* — especially the broader good-administration commitments to legality, legal certainty, equality, transparency, reasons, and reviewability, which support keeping guidance within law and making practical expectations publicly legible.
- Council of Europe. *The administration and you — a handbook* — especially the stress that inaccessible non-binding guidance cannot substitute for a statutory system “in accordance with law”, which supports treating practically binding guidance as something that must be publicly visible and legally disciplined.

### Legitimate expectations should civilize state reversal without freezing fraud, obvious illegality, or overriding public-interest correction

- Council of Europe. *The administration and you — a handbook* — especially the treatment of revocation, legitimate expectation, elapsed time, knowledge of unlawfulness, and the need for internal procedures that foster legal certainty when authorities correct their own mistakes.
- ReNEUAL. *Model Rules on EU Administrative Procedure* — especially the preambular insistence on legal certainty, fairness, transparency, and protection of legitimate expectations, which supports treating reliance as a core administrative-law design variable rather than a judicial afterthought.
- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2007)7 on good administration* — especially the code’s general principles of legality, legal certainty, proportionality, and reviewability, which support reliance-sensitive reversal rather than abrupt bait-and-switch correction.
- OECD / SIGMA. *The Principles of Public Administration* — especially the framing of modern public administration around rule-of-law, transparency, accountability, and citizen-trust expectations, which supports making reliance and fair frustration explicit rather than leaving them to administrative folklore.
- OECD. *Regulatory Policy Outlook 2025* — especially the emphasis that better regulation depends on predictable, proportionate, and communicative frameworks, which supports transition and fair-warning discipline when the practical meaning of rules changes.

## Sources used for rev0653

### Public redress should have a visible time constitution rather than leaving rights to backlog, dead time, and queue folklore

- OECD. *Toolkit for Access to Justice and People-Centred Justice Systems* — especially the 2025 implementation emphasis on accessible, affordable, high-quality legal and justice services plus governance infrastructure and evidence-based management, which supports treating timeliness as designed public infrastructure rather than a court-internal statistic.
- OECD. *Making Justice Systems More Effective and People Centred* — especially the framing of responsive justice as resolving problems early and fairly and of administrative justice as the interface between people and institutions, which supports taking delay as a rule-of-law failure rather than mere inconvenience.
- CEPEJ. *SATURN Guidelines for Judicial Time Management* (2021 revision) — especially the insistence on active case management, realistic time standards, rapid response to departures from targets, and transparent management of judicial time.
- CEPEJ. *Time Management Checklist* (2023) and *Implementing the SATURN Time Management Tools in Courts* — especially the practical stress on diagnosing duration by stage, identifying causes of delay, and using management tools before unreasonable duration becomes normal.
- Council of Europe. *The right to trial within reasonable time* and comparative materials on effective remedies against excessive duration — especially the point that states need effective acceleratory or compensatory remedies when proceedings last too long.

### Review should stay capable of protecting rights before irreversible public action turns the merits into theater

- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2007)7 on good administration* — especially the code’s treatment of appeals, appropriate remedies, and prejudice-free access to challenge, which supports taking interim protection seriously where delay would empty the remedy.
- Council of Europe. *The administration and you — a handbook* — especially its synthesis of review, appeals, suspensive effect, and effective remedies in relations between individuals and public authorities.
- Council of Europe / ECHR. *Guide to good practice in respect of domestic remedies* — especially the insistence, in relevant high-risk contexts, that remedies need suspensive effect or equivalent urgent protection to remain effective.
- ECHR. *Interim measures* fact sheet and related effective-remedy materials — especially the emphasis that interim measures are urgent protections used where there is a real risk of irreversible harm.
- OECD. *Toolkit for Access to Justice and People-Centred Justice Systems* — especially the 2025 people-centred emphasis that justice pathways should deliver fair outcomes in practice, which supports fast, usable urgent-protection lanes instead of nominal emergency powers no ordinary person can reach.

## Sources used for rev0652
