- `777-execution-of-administrative-and-judicial-decisions-against-public-authorities-necessity-tests-for-ideal-governments-compliance-clocks-budget-home-escalation-and-no-judgment-by-non-execution.md`

These sources informed the new pattern packs in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Access to public redress should not depend on surviving fees, costs, and pre-merits financial terror

- OECD. *Toolkit for Access to Justice and People-Centred Justice Systems* — especially the 2025 guidance that legal and justice services should be proportionate and affordable, with affordability metrics, tiered pathways, and cost-conscious design rather than implicit rationing by price.
- Council of Europe / CDCJ. *Guidelines on the efficiency and the effectiveness of legal aid schemes in the areas of civil and administrative law* (adopted 31 March 2021) — especially the insistence that legal aid should cover legal advice, assistance, and representation and be designed for accessibility, quality, and vulnerability-aware entry.
- CEPEJ. *Checklist for promoting access to justice* (2025) — especially the emphasis that access to justice requires affordability, free and early legal advice, and state-funded assistance where otherwise a fair hearing would be denied.
- Council of Europe. *Recommendation No. R (93) 1 on effective access to the law and to justice for the very poor* — especially the long-standing support for legal advice services, supported advice centres, and practical measures that reduce cost barriers for the poor.
- UNECE / Aarhus Convention. *Access to justice* materials and associated good-practice work on prohibitive costs — especially the insistence that procedures to challenge acts and omissions in relevant public-law fields should not be prohibitively expensive.

### Winning against the state should still lead to execution rather than operational defeat by delay or budget excuse

- OECD. *Toolkit for Access to Justice and People-Centred Justice Systems* — especially the 2025 guidance that enforcement and respect for outcomes across dispute-resolution mechanisms require affordable, accountable enforcement design rather than leaving parties alone after decision.
- Council of Europe. *Recommendation Rec(2003)16 on the execution of administrative and judicial decisions in the field of administrative law* — especially the principles requiring effective execution, liability where authorities refuse or neglect implementation, and the prohibition on lack-of-funds excuses defeating money obligations.
- CEPEJ. *Guidelines for a better implementation of the existing Council of Europe’s recommendation on enforcement* (2009) — especially the emphasis that enforcement quality, responsibility, and user-facing effectiveness are part of the justice system rather than an afterthought.
- CEPEJ. *Good practice guide on enforcement of judicial decisions* (2015) — especially the practical guidance on transparent, efficient, quality-controlled enforcement and the special importance of execution against public entities.
- Council of Europe / ECHR Knowledge Sharing. *Guide on Article 46 of the Convention – Binding force and execution of judgments* (2025 update) and related Council of Europe materials — especially the rule-of-law principle that failure to implement a final, binding judicial decision is incompatible with the state’s obligations and cannot be normalized as administrative discretion.

## Sources used for rev0651

Current notes:

These sources informed the new pattern packs in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Systemic illegality should not survive because no one claimant can efficiently carry a diffuse or mass wrong alone

- OECD. *Recommendation of the Council on Access to Justice and People-Centred Justice Systems* — especially the insistence that access to justice be designed around people’s real ability to vindicate rights, which supports treating representative and collective routes as ordinary justice infrastructure where one-by-one enforcement would predictably fail.
- OECD. *Toolkit for Access to Justice and People-Centred Justice Systems* — especially the 2025 implementation guidance on design, delivery, and empowerment, which supports fit-for-purpose standing, notice, and support architectures rather than formal availability alone.
- EUR-Lex. *Consumer protection – Representative actions* — especially the official summary of the Representative Actions Directive’s use of qualified entities, court or administrative approval of settlements, and controls on third-party funding conflicts, which supports a disciplined collective-redress lane rather than free-form mass-litigation improvisation.
- UNECE. *Access to Justice* under the Aarhus Convention — especially the requirement that members of the public have access to administrative or judicial procedures to challenge acts and omissions by public authorities and private persons in relevant fields, which supports broader standing where public-interest harms are real.
- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2007)14 on the legal status of non-governmental organisations in Europe* — especially the insistence that acts or omissions by public authorities affecting NGOs should be open to challenge before an independent and impartial tribunal.
- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2024)2 on countering the use of strategic lawsuits against public participation (SLAPPs)* — especially the warning against legal process being used to chill participation on matters of public interest, which supports calibrated cost and anti-abuse rules rather than deterrence by ruinous exposure.

### Winning legality should still lead to repair rather than stopping at quash-only rights

- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2007)7 on good administration* — especially the code’s treatment of remedies, compensation, and execution of court orders against public authorities, which supports pairing review with a real repair lane.
- Council of Europe. *Recommendation Rec(2003)16 on the execution of administrative and judicial decisions in the field of administrative law* — especially the insistence that administrative authorities should be liable where they refuse or neglect to implement judicial decisions and that lack of funds should not defeat obligations in public liability.
- Council of Europe. *Recommendation No. R (84) 15 relating to public liability* — especially the core idea that public authorities should make good damage caused by their acts by compensation or other appropriate reparation, and that concentrated public burdens should not simply be left on unlucky individuals.
- Council of Europe. *The administration and you — a handbook* — especially its synthesis of good-administration and public-liability standards around compensation, access to remedies, and full redress.
- OECD. *Recommendation of the Council on Access to Justice and People-Centred Justice Systems* — especially the people-centred emphasis that remedies must be usable and effective in practice, not merely doctrinally available.
- OECD. *Recommendation of the Council on Human-Centred Public Administrative Services* — especially the insistence that public administrative services should be designed around people’s rights, needs, and practical ability to navigate consequences, which supports low-friction claims and payment lanes rather than repair by bureaucratic endurance contest.

- `773-administrative-appeal-and-tribunal-necessity-tests-for-ideal-governments-reasons-file-access-internal-review-suspensive-relief-merits-vs-legality-review-and-no-rights-by-one-shot-decision.md`

These sources informed the new pattern packs in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Ombuds should exist as an independent low-friction maladministration and public-redress lane rather than as helpdesk theater

- Venice Commission / Council of Europe. *Principles on the Protection and Promotion of the Ombudsman Institution (the “Venice Principles”)* — especially the insistence on a strong legal foundation, independence, broad accessibility, own-motion work, and protection against retaliation or budgetary strangulation, which supports treating ombuds architecture as constitutional redress infrastructure rather than administrative décor.
- OECD. *The Role of Ombudsman Institutions in Open Government* — especially the framing of ombuds institutions as mediators and rights-guarding interfaces between people and administration, which supports designing them as complaint-handling, learning, and trust-repair institutions rather than narrow scandal offices.
- OECD. *Making Justice Systems More Effective and People Centred* (chapter on administrative justice as the interface between people and institutions) — especially the view that administrative justice includes ombuds institutes, tribunals, complaints systems, and frontline officials, which supports locating the ombuds inside one wider people-centred redress ecology.
- Council of Europe. *Guide to Good Administrative Practices for Public Authorities* — especially the treatment of administrative appeals, internal reviews, and objective appeal bodies, which supports clear onward-routing and complementarity between ombuds work and harder review lanes.
- Parliamentary Assembly of the Council of Europe. *Ombudsman institutions in Europe – the need for a set of common standards* — especially the emphasis on threats to ombuds independence and effectiveness, which supports guarding against quiet capture by underfunding, appointment games, or mandate shrinkage.

### Administrative appeals and tribunals should form the state’s ordinary second-look constitution rather than leaving rights to one-shot decisions

- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2007)7 on good administration* — especially the insistence on appeals, compensation, and prejudice-free use of remedies, which supports treating review rights as part of ordinary good administration rather than exceptional litigation add-ons.
- Council of Europe. *Guide to Good Administrative Practices for Public Authorities* — especially the operational emphasis on objective and impartial appeal authorities, appeal on merits or legality, and independent bodies where feasible, which supports typing review forms explicitly.
- OECD. *Recommendation of the Council on Access to Justice and People-Centred Justice Systems* — especially the wider call to build justice pathways around people’s needs and usable governance mechanisms, which supports designing administrative second-look routes as ordinary public infrastructure.
- OECD. *Toolkit for Access to Justice and People-Centred Justice Systems* — especially the practical guidance for implementing the access-to-justice recommendation, which supports readability, accessibility, and referral clarity in review systems.
- OECD. *Making Justice Systems More Effective and People Centred* (chapter on administrative justice as the interface between people and institutions) — especially the framing of administrative justice as the frontline of day-to-day rule-of-law delivery, which supports taking reasons, review, and remedy seriously below the constitutional-court level.
- OECD / SIGMA. *Implementation of laws on general administrative procedure in the Western Balkans* — especially the stress on reason-giving, file access, reviewability, and the practical implementation gap between legal promise and administrative reality, which supports making the appeal lane operational rather than merely statutory.

- `771-public-policy-evaluation-and-management-response-necessity-tests-for-ideal-governments-workplans-independence-publication-response-ledgers-reopen-triggers-and-no-learning-by-unanswered-recommendation.md`

These sources informed the new pattern packs in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Inside assurance should sit between line management and external scandal without becoming a shadow executive

- SIGMA. *The Principles of Public Administration* — especially Principle 27 on consistent internal audit, central harmonisation, direct reporting, risk-based planning, systematic follow-up, and the insistence that financial inspection, where it exists, should complement rather than swallow internal audit.
- OECD. *Internal control and audit in the public sector* — especially the current evidence that internal audit is only effective when it reaches enough of the risk-bearing public budget, which supports coverage realism instead of symbolic unit existence.
- OECD. *Enhancing co-operation between internal and external auditors* — especially the distinction between ongoing internal assurance and independent external oversight, plus the warning that co-operation needs safeguards against blurred responsibilities and threats to independence.
- CIGIE / IGnet. *Quality Standards for Inspection and Evaluation* and *Frequently Asked Questions* — especially the emphasis on independence, impartiality, hotline/reporting functions, and fact-based reports grounded in professional standards, which supports treating inspection and inspector-general work as method-disciplined rather than prestige branding.
- United States Code. *Inspector General Act of 1978* — especially the direct-reporting and non-interference provisions and the prohibition on program-operating responsibilities, which support the archive’s rule against self-inspection by line command.

### Evaluation should have a management-response lane and reopen logic rather than ending as shelf literature

- OECD. *Recommendation of the Council on Public Policy Evaluation* and *Implementation Toolkit for the OECD Recommendation on Public Policy Evaluation* — especially the whole-of-government framing, the three pillars of institutionalisation, quality, and impact, and the insistence that systematic follow-up mechanisms such as management responses and follow-up committees are what turn findings into action.
- OECD. *Government at a Glance 2025 — Public policy evaluation* — especially the current evidence that many governments still lack consistent follow-up mechanisms, which supports treating response and tracking design as core architecture rather than optional good practice.
- UNDP. *Evaluation Policy, 2025–2030* — especially the requirement that management respond to evaluation recommendations with specific, time-bound actions, assigned responsibilities, and public visibility through an online resource centre, which supports strong response ownership and public discoverability.
- World Bank Group / Independent Evaluation Group. *Management Action Record 2024* materials — especially the emphasis on implementation follow-through, validation, change of direction, and accumulated recommendations, which supports ledger-based tracking rather than one-shot acceptance theater.
- OECD / SIGMA. *Toolkit for the preparation, implementation, monitoring, reporting and evaluation of public administration reform and sector strategies* — especially the practical insistence that evaluation be connected to strategy cycles, reporting, and implementation rather than treated as detached commentary.

### Lobbying should remain legitimate participation without disappearing into invisible influence

- OECD. *Recommendation on Transparency and Integrity in Lobbying* — especially the revised 2024 framing that lobbying and influence are legitimate parts of policy making only when transparency and integrity rules make interactions between public decision-makers and influence actors visible and governable.
- OECD. *Lobbying in the 21st Century* — especially the warning that many systems reveal too little about targets, objectives, and channels of lobbying, which supports combining actor-side registers with official-side meeting disclosure, subject-matter visibility, and broader definitions of influence activity.
- OECD. *Strengthening the Framework on Pre- and Post-Public Employment in Romania* — especially the practical treatment of at-risk officials, switch-side representation, monitoring, advice, and sanctions, which supports typing revolving-door rules as a live integrity system rather than vague loyalty language.
- OECD. *Conflict of interest* — especially the emphasis that revolving-door risks, declaration verification, and conflict-resolution processes need data, monitoring, and real implementation rather than policy aspiration alone.
- Council of Europe / GRECO. *Stricter regulation needed to prevent corruption in top executive functions of central governments, says GRECO* — especially the insistence that lobbying contacts, revolving doors, asset transparency, and access to information remain active integrity risks for top executive functions, which supports placing lobbying visibility inside the constitutional operating system rather than at its edge.

### Conflict management and asset-interest disclosure should be a live decision-integrity system, not annual paperwork theatre

- OECD. *Conflict of interest* — especially the recent evidence that too few systems verify declarations at scale or resolve detected conflicts promptly, which supports making verification rates, resolution lanes, and sanction practice part of the design rather than afterthoughts.
- OECD. *OECD Guidelines for Managing Conflict of Interest in the Public Service* — especially the core idea that policy should identify, disclose, manage, and where needed prohibit unacceptable private-interest overlaps rather than rely on trust or post hoc scandal.
- World Bank / StAR. *Asset and Interest Disclosure: A Technical Guide* — especially the distinction between data collection, risk analysis, verification, publication choices, and enforcement, which supports treating declarations as a managed system with role-typed coverage and audit logic.
- Open Government Partnership. *Asset and Interest Disclosure* — especially the emphasis that publication quality, open formats, and usable disclosure data help journalists, watchdogs, and the public test whether officials are serving constituents or private interests.
- Council of Europe / GRECO. *Stricter regulation needed to prevent corruption in top executive functions of central governments, says GRECO* — especially the insistence on stronger monitoring of asset declarations, conflict management, and revolving-door practices for top executive roles, which supports connecting disclosure, recusal, and sanctions into one enforceable integrity architecture.

- OECD / SIGMA. *Study on the Political Involvement in Senior Staffing and on the Delineation of Responsibilities between Ministers and Senior Civil Servants* — especially the distinction between legitimate political direction and professional senior staffing, which supports keeping the political-trust layer bounded instead of letting it swallow the public service.

### Vacancy and acting service should preserve continuity without becoming a shadow appointments constitution

- OECD / SIGMA. *Public Administration in the Western Balkans 2024* — especially the warning that acting appointments exacerbate instability and bypass competitive recruitment, which supports treating serial provisionality as a design failure rather than a normal staffing method.
- OECD. *Public Employment and Management 2021* — especially the emphasis on transparency around senior public-service vacancies and recruitment processes, which supports making vacancy and acting status visible constitutional information rather than internal staffing trivia.
- International IDEA. *Government Formation and Removal Mechanisms* — especially the stress on continuity, caretaker discipline, and visible formation repair paths, which supports a narrow continuity bridge rather than open-ended interim government.
- Venice Commission / Council of Europe. *Opinion on the Proposed Amendments to the Constitution of Finland* — especially the caution that fixed-term members should be appointed only as acting justices and only on the initiative of the court, which supports high friction and narrow use of temporary occupancy in referee institutions.
- Venice Commission / Council of Europe. *Opinion on Certain Draft Amendments to the Law on the Constitutional Court of Bosnia and Herzegovina* — especially the caution around allowing acting or ad hoc judges to reconstitute a constitutional court too freely, which supports preserving composition, legality, and anti-capture discipline when vacancies threaten quorum.

## Sources used for rev0643

Current notes:

- `758-delegated-legislation-and-executive-rulemaking-necessity-tests-for-ideal-governments-framework-statutes-purposes-scope-time-limits-scrutiny-publication-and-no-government-by-decree-laundering.md`
- `759-independent-institutions-necessity-tests-for-ideal-governments-referee-functions-bounded-mandates-appointment-discipline-budget-protection-review-lanes-and-no-fourth-branch-sprawl.md`

These sources informed the new pattern packs in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Delegated legislation should stay a bounded implementation lane rather than becoming a hidden substitute legislature

- International IDEA. *The Role of Parliament in Delegated Legislation: Principles for Safeguarding Legislative Transparency and Democratic Accountability* — especially the framing of delegated legislation as practically necessary yet dangerous when poorly bounded, which supports keeping major political choices in primary law and treating legislative scrutiny as a continuing duty rather than a one-time delegation act.
- OECD. *Recommendation of the Council on Regulatory Policy and Governance* — especially the whole-of-government framing of regulatory policy and governance, which supports treating delegated rulemaking as part of a legible regulatory constitution rather than an administrative afterthought.
- OECD. *OECD Best Practice Principles for Regulatory Policy* — especially the focus on policies, practices, and institutions needed for better regulatory governance, which supports visible publication, consultation, and review disciplines for secondary legislation.
- Venice Commission / Council of Europe. *The Updated Rule of Law Checklist* — especially the insistence that societally important issues should normally be handled by statute and that delegated lawmaking must have explicit objectives, contents, scope, and when needed time limits, which supports a high burden of proof against vague enabling clauses.
- International IDEA. *Presidential Legislative Powers* — especially the warning that concentrated executive agenda and lawmaking powers can undermine constitutional balance, which supports skepticism toward decree-heavy systems and emergency-style delegation becoming normal governance.

### Independent institutions should exist only where a function genuinely needs credible distance from day-to-day political command

- International IDEA. *Independent Regulatory and Oversight (Fourth-Branch) Institutions* — especially the framing of such institutions as politically neutral bodies outside the three main branches whose purpose is to protect integrity and resilience of democratic governance, which supports a functional rather than romantic case for independence.
- International IDEA. *Independent Institutions: Enhancing Democratic Integrity and Accountability through Constitutional Design* — especially the distinction between guardian and watchdog bodies, which supports typing independent institutions by role instead of treating every commission as the same constitutional species.
- OECD. *Recommendation of the Council on Regulatory Policy and Governance* — especially the recommendation that regulatory agencies should support objective, impartial, and consistent decisions free from conflict of interest and improper influence, which supports independence only where neutrality is part of the function.
- OECD. *The Governance of Regulators* — especially the wider governance architecture linking regulators to legislatures, ministers, and accountability frameworks, which supports designing independence inside a visible constitutional order rather than as untouchable technocracy.
- OECD. *Being an Independent Regulator* — especially the practical emphasis on preventing undue influence while preserving trust and accountability, which supports pairing independence with transparent governance, budget, and review design.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive documents in this revision are not summaries of the sources. They are synthetic design notes derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0642

Current notes:

- `756-constitutional-review-necessity-tests-for-ideal-governments-integrated-apex-vs-specialized-court-ordinary-court-referral-abstract-review-remedial-bounds-and-no-guardian-without-docket-discipline.md`
- `757-emergency-powers-necessity-tests-for-ideal-governments-ordinary-law-first-temporal-limits-legislative-renewal-judicial-review-and-no-shadow-constitution-by-crisis.md`

These sources informed the new pattern packs in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Constitutional review should fit the polity’s legal tradition, settlement burden, and court ecology rather than being copied as prestige furniture

- International IDEA. *The Fundamentals of Constitutional Courts* — especially the distinction between centralized and diffused systems, the special case for constitutional courts in enforcing new constitutional settlements, and the importance of finality, abstract review design, referrals, and remedial flexibility.
- International IDEA. *Judicial Appointments* — especially the framing of appointment design as a balance among independence, representativeness, and competence, which supports treating constitutional-review staffing as anti-capture architecture rather than ordinary patronage.
- International IDEA. *Judicial Tenure, Removal, Immunity and Accountability* — especially the emphasis on balancing judicial independence with accountability, which supports treating constitutional guardianship as neither winner-takes-all spoils nor untouchable judicial enclosure.
- Venice Commission / Council of Europe. *Compilation of Venice Commission opinions, reports and studies on constitutional justice* — especially the comparative doctrine on relations with ordinary courts, standing, effects of decisions, and execution of constitutional judgments, which supports insisting on explicit referral lanes, typed remedies, and compliance design.
- Venice Commission / Council of Europe. *The Updated Rule of Law Checklist* — especially the elevation of effective checks and balances and effective constitutional review to explicit rule-of-law benchmarks, which supports treating constitutional review as core constitutional infrastructure rather than optional constitutional ornament.

### Emergency powers should stay exceptional, temporary, reviewable, and visibly separable from ordinary crisis administration

- Council of Europe / Venice Commission. *Emergency powers — what standards?* — especially the synthesis that emergency powers must remain compatible with democracy, human rights, and the rule of law, which supports routing emergency design through necessity, legality, and review rather than rhetorical exception.
- Venice Commission / Council of Europe. *Compilation of Venice Commission opinions, reports and studies on emergency situations* — especially the insistence that emergency powers be limited in time and space, subject to parliamentary scrutiny, and tied to restoration of the democratic legal order, which supports strong renewal discipline and skepticism toward permanent emergency.
- Venice Commission / Council of Europe. *The Updated Rule of Law Checklist* — especially the stronger emphasis on checks and balances, judicial independence, and constitutional review, which supports keeping courts and legality alive during crisis instead of accepting review blackout as a normal emergency cost.
- International IDEA. *Emergency law responses to the Covid-19 pandemic* — especially the observation that the pandemic exposed gaps in many emergency-law frameworks, which supports keeping ordinary law, typed emergency classes, and post-emergency cleanup visibly designed rather than improvised.

### Editorial interpretation

## Sources used for rev0641

Current notes:

- `754-head-of-state-necessity-tests-for-ideal-governments-no-separate-crown-below-national-scale-ceremonial-arbiters-collective-headship-reserve-powers-on-rails-and-no-duplicate-democratic-apex.md`
- `755-constitutional-referendum-necessity-tests-for-ideal-governments-amendment-classes-double-majorities-question-clarity-balanced-information-and-no-plebiscitary-shortcuts.md`

These sources informed the new pattern packs in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Separate heads of state should exist only where a symbolic or arbitral apex performs real constitutional work

- International IDEA. *Non-Executive Presidents in Parliamentary Democracies* — especially the framing of a non-executive president as a symbolic leader who may nevertheless act as a constitutional arbiter or guarantor, which supports treating ceremonial continuity and bounded reserve powers as a specific design choice rather than generic state prestige.
- International IDEA. *Constitutional Monarchs in Parliamentary Democracies* — especially the wider point that parliamentary systems can separate symbolic headship from day-to-day government without making the head of state the governing executive, which supports analyzing function rather than inherited grandeur.
- International IDEA. *Electing Presidents in Presidential and Semi-Presidential Democracies* — especially the distinction between presidents with substantial governing authority and those who are merely formal, which supports a higher burden of proof before directly electing a mostly ceremonial office.
- International IDEA. *Government Formation and Removal Mechanisms* — especially the parliamentary emphasis on government formation through legislative confidence, which supports keeping ceremonial or arbitral headship from mutating into a second governing chain.
- Swiss Confederation / Presence Switzerland. *The Federal Council* — especially the description of Switzerland’s seven-member collegial government and rotating presidency, which supports keeping collective headship available as a rare but honest pattern where plural or consociational settlement logic really justifies it.
- Swiss Confederation. *Federal Council* — especially the official presentation of the Federal Council as a jointly deciding seven-member government, which supports using collective headship only with explicit collegial rules rather than as symbolic vagueness.

### Constitutional referendums should be used for real constituent ratification, not as plebiscitary shortcuts

- International IDEA. *Direct Democracy* — especially the framing of referendums and initiatives as mechanisms that enable the public to vote directly on a constitutional amendment, law, treaty, or policy decision, which supports distinguishing true direct-democratic channels from symbolic consultation rhetoric.
- International IDEA. *Constitutional Amendment Procedures* — especially the discussion of referendums as one possible route for involving the people in constitutional amendment rather than as a universal requirement, which supports classifying amendment types before assuming a popular vote is necessary.
- Venice Commission / Council of Europe. *Revised Code of Good Practice on Referendums* — especially the insistence on question clarity, unity, prior scrutiny, balanced information, explicit legal effects, and caution about turnout and approval quorums, which supports treating referendum quality rules as constitutional substance rather than technical aftercare.
- Venice Commission / Council of Europe. *Report on Constitutional Amendment* — especially the comparative framing that there is no single best amendment model but that amendment procedures must fit the constitutional order they protect, which supports routing referendums through amendment class and territorial bargain rather than through one-size-fits-all plebiscitary romance.

## Sources used for rev0640

Current notes:

- `752-government-formation-and-removal-by-scope-micro-local-recall-municipal-manager-confidence-metro-investiture-parliamentary-default-constructive-no-confidence-fixed-terms-and-no-dual-democratic-command.md`
- `753-upper-chamber-necessity-tests-for-ideal-governments-territorial-shared-rule-bounded-veto-domains-asymmetric-bargains-suspensive-delay-and-no-duplicate-bicameralism.md`

These sources informed the new pattern packs in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Governments should be formed and removed through one legible confidence chain at each scale rather than through constitutional blank space

- International IDEA. *Government Formation and Removal Mechanisms* — especially the framing of parliamentary governments as indirectly selected through parliament and removable by parliamentary no-confidence, which supports making formation, replacement, and deadlock-repair grammar explicit rather than treating post-election bargaining as constitutional folklore.
- International IDEA. *Electing Presidents in Presidential and Semi-Presidential Democracies* — especially the distinction between presidents who govern directly and presidents who share power with a prime minister yet still hold substantial political authority, which supports treating dual-mandate systems as a deliberate constitutional exception rather than an uninspected default.
- International IDEA. *Presidential Legislative Powers* — especially the warning that strong popularly elected presidents can accumulate substantial agenda and legislative influence, which supports a higher burden of proof before adding a direct executive mandate where parliamentary repair may work better.
- International IDEA. *Non-Executive Presidents in Parliamentary Democracies* — especially the distinction between ceremonial or arbitral headship and operational government, which supports separating symbolic continuity from day-to-day governing command when that reduces conflict.
- OECD. *The OECD Metropolitan Governance Survey* — especially the evidence that many metropolitan bodies exist but few are directly elected and that directly elected ones are likelier to hold formal powers, which supports requiring explicit investiture grammar once metro institutions become real governments rather than loose coordination clubs.
- ICMA. *Council-Manager Form of Government Resources* — especially the description of council-manager government as elected policy leadership plus appointed professional administration serving at the council’s pleasure, which supports keeping municipal democratic direction and daily management visibly distinct.
- National League of Cities. *Cities 101 — Forms of Local Government* — especially the basic contrast among council-manager, mayor-council, commission, and town-meeting forms, which supports treating municipal formation design as a real choice rather than a single inherited template.
- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the wider protection of local democratic self-government and internal administrative structures under law, which supports keeping local formation rules lawful, legible, and non-arbitrary.

### Upper chambers should survive only where territorial shared rule, asymmetric bargains, or a bounded revising role are real

- International IDEA. *Bicameralism* — especially the constitution-building framing that bicameralism is a design choice to be justified rather than a ritual badge of seriousness, which supports explicit necessity tests before preserving or creating an upper chamber.
- Venice Commission / Council of Europe. *Report on Bicameralism* — especially the argument that participation of federated states, provinces, regions, or communities in central decision-making is an essential justification for bicameralism, which supports treating territorial shared rule as the strongest pathway to an honest upper chamber.
- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the wide variation in regional arrangements and reform paths, which supports refusing one inherited senate model for all regionalized or devolved states.
- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the wider commitment to territorial self-government under law, which supports tying any upper-chamber protection logic to real territorial autonomy rather than prestige duplication.

## Sources used for rev0639

Current notes:

- `750-polity-morphology-archetypes-for-ideal-government-stacks-dense-metro-polycentric-federal-sparse-archipelagic-and-cross-border-overlay-with-no-one-world-default.md`
- `751-regional-tier-necessity-tests-territorial-platforms-shared-rule-asymmetry-collapse-and-no-map-inheritance-as-constitution.md`

These sources informed the new pattern packs in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Whole-polity stack design should be morphology-aware rather than built from one universal territorial silhouette

- OECD. *Assigning responsibilities across levels of government* — especially the insistence that responsibility allocation needs to match actual capacity and coordination realities rather than abstract symmetry, which supports treating morphology as prior design information instead of assuming one stack everywhere.
- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the finding that regional reforms follow non-linear paths and yield a broad spectrum of governance models, which supports recurrent morphology archetypes instead of one normal regional template.
- OECD. *The OECD Metropolitan Governance Survey* — especially the finding that metropolitan governance bodies are common but often underpowered, which supports distinguishing metro-dominant morphologies from more distributed country-forms.
- OECD. *OECD Principles on Urban Policy* — especially the emphasis on effective urban policy and tools that work across cities, regions, and countries, which supports designing around the scale where urban systems are actually real.
- OECD. *Building More Resilient Cross-border Regions* — especially the treatment of border regions as places with recurring structural barriers that often need dedicated multi-level and cross-border governance overlays, which supports treating border-interlocked territories as a recurring morphology rather than a one-off anomaly.
- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the guarantee of political, administrative, and financial independence for elected local authorities, which supports keeping real local democratic homes visible even in fused, sparse, or archipelagic stacks.
- International IDEA. *Local Democracy* — especially the framing of local democracy as the self-government of cities, towns, villages, and districts by democratic means, which supports preserving local voice across very different whole-polity morphologies.
- World Bank. *From Crisis to Resilience: World Bank Support to Small States* — especially the emphasis that small states face distinctive capacity constraints and shocks, which supports a separate archetype for small and dispersed polities rather than treating them as failed large states.

### Regional tiers should exist by necessity test rather than by inherited map alone

- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the evidence that regional governance reforms differ widely and often rely on asymmetric arrangements, which supports requiring a visible necessity test before treating regions as indispensable.
- OECD. *Assigning responsibilities across levels of government* — especially the stress on clarity of assignment and the costs of overlap fog, which supports forcing regions to justify what work they uniquely hold.
- OECD. *The OECD Metropolitan Governance Survey* — especially the warning that metro bodies often exist with too little power, which supports checking whether a supposed regional problem is really a metropolitan one.
- OECD. *OECD Principles on Urban Policy* — especially the focus on targeting action to the effective scale of real urban systems, which supports refusing decorative regions where the real missing tier is metropolitan.
- OECD. *Building More Resilient Cross-border Regions* — especially the demonstration that some territorial problems are border-shaped rather than domestically regional, which supports preferring bounded overlays over fake regions where the footprint is cross-border.
- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the protection of elected local self-government, which supports rejecting regional expansion that hollows municipalities without proving necessity.
- International IDEA. *Local Democracy* — especially the emphasis on democratic self-government at local levels, which supports asking whether a region adds a necessary democratic home or only another layer of distance.
- World Bank. *From Crisis to Resilience: World Bank Support to Small States* — especially the small-state capacity framing, which supports honest collapse or pooling where regions cannot be sustained as real governments.

### Once the archive can absorb, move, and restore retired memorial objects, it should also keep one compact packet for repairing the memorial object boundary itself when one entry really covered several objects or several entries really described one

- OECD. *Assigning responsibilities across levels of government* — especially the emphasis on clear responsibility assignment and the costs of ambiguity, which supports treating memorial boundary repair as an explicit governance act rather than an editorial preference.
- The National Archives. *Guidance on Providing Information About Your Records* — especially the guidance that existing catalogue entries can often be amended to include predecessor and successor bodies, that name changes do not always require a new top-level entry, and that new higher-level groupings should only be created when records really do not fit the existing structure, which supports disciplined tests for when a dead authority is the same object with corrected context versus a genuinely distinct memorial object.
- The National Archives. *Basic Web Archiving Guidance* — especially the guidance to preserve historical access and use redirects or archive continuity when pages disappear, which supports keeping mistaken-boundary pages, aliases, and superseded lookup routes discoverable after repair rather than silently deleting them.
- UK Government. *Content design: planning, writing and managing content* — especially the rule that withdrawn or historical content should stay understandable and should not be duplicated unless there is a genuinely distinct user need, which supports separating name-only relabeling from real object splits or merges.
- UK Government. *Unpublishing and withdrawing ('archiving')* — especially the requirement for visible explanatory treatment of withdrawn content and explicit redirect behavior when routes change, which supports keeping prior memorial identifiers usable as superseded lookup surfaces after a boundary repair.
- U.S. National Archives and Records Administration. *NARA 1301, Lifecycle Data Standards and Lifecycle Authority Control* — especially the treatment of authority control as maintaining consistent access points, relationships among names and sources, and documented modification of authority sources, which supports explicit split/merge/relink packets instead of silent identifier drift.
- U.S. National Archives and Records Administration. *Archival Materials and Related Elements* — especially the requirement to link descriptions to creators and maintain hierarchy and provenance, which supports keeping object-boundary repair distinct from ordinary routing edits.
- U.S. National Archives and Records Administration. *National Archives Catalog Search Tips* — especially the visibility of authority records and their related archival descriptions, which supports preserving superseded names and duplicate entries as discoverable lookup aids even after one canonical memorial object is chosen.
- U.S. National Archives and Records Administration. *AC 34.2025* — especially the reminder to identify successor entities and ongoing records-management responsibility during reorganisation, which supports recalculating successor, records-home, and legacy-question routes object by object after a boundary repair.

- EUR-Lex. *The principle of subsidiarity* — especially the rule that decisions should be taken as closely as possible to citizens consistent with effective action, which supports burden-of-proof discipline for moving powers upward.
- United Nations. *United Nations Charter* — especially the treaty-bound basis for peace, security, sovereign equality, and co-operation, which supports keeping the global layer thin, real, and typed rather than imagining one general world state.
- World Health Organization. *International Health Regulations (2005)* — especially the legally binding alerting and coordination framework for international disease spread, which supports a narrow but real global layer for shared public-health risk.

### Protected self-government should remain lawful in supervision, adequately resourced in practice, and operationally capable at go-live rather than being hollowed out during implementation

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Articles 4, 6, 8, 9, and 11 on legal basis, administrative structures, proportional supervision, commensurate resources, and judicial remedy.
- Council of Europe. *Explanatory report to the European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the explanation that supervision by higher levels should remain tied to law and that local self-government needs practical institutional protection rather than formal titles alone.
- Council of Europe. *Adequate financial resources for local authorities* — especially the insistence that legal authority and responsibility are meaningless if local and regional authorities are deprived of the resources needed to carry them out.
- Council of Europe. *Regional democracy — Council of Europe Reference Framework* — especially the line that regional competences should be recognised by law and that regions be involved when their competences, essential interests, or scope of self-government are affected.

- Council of Europe. *Territorial reforms in Europe: Does size matter?* — especially the toolkit treatment of boundary and structural reform as a governed process requiring clear objectives, institutional dialogue, consultation, implementation planning, and evaluation rather than map surgery by fiat.

### Metropolitan or regional territorial design should be explicit about competences, membership, and multilevel fit

- Council of Europe. *Regional democracy — Council of Europe Reference Framework* — especially the framing of regional authorities as territorial authorities between the state and local authorities, and the broader implication that territorial tiers need explicit standing in the multilevel order.

## Sources used for rev0531

Current note: `623-redress-homes-for-shared-territorial-power-complaints-hearings-appeals-ombuds-and-no-government-by-jurisdiction-hunt.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Shared territorial government needs its own redress constitution rather than host-city complaint residue or operator maze

- OECD Legal Instruments. *Recommendation of the Council on Access to Justice and People-Centred Justice Systems* — especially the whole-of-state and people-centred frame for making justice usable in practice rather than treating redress as a courthouse tail only.
- OECD. *Making Justice Systems More Effective and People Centred* — especially the treatment of administrative justice as the interface between people and institutions, and the insistence that everyday disputes with public administration are central to trust and legitimacy.
- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 11’s baseline that local authorities need recourse to a judicial remedy to secure the free exercise of their powers and the principles of self-government.

### A real redress home needs ombuds, review, and co-ordinated non-judicial routes rather than one foggy promise of accountability

- OECD. *The role of Ombudsman Institutions in Open Government* — especially the description of ombuds institutions as guardians of citizens’ rights and mediators with public administration, and the emphasis on their role in surfacing systemic problems in service delivery and administration.
- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2019)6 on the development of the Ombudsman institution* — especially the insistence on independence, adequate resources, and co-ordination where several national, regional, local, or specialised ombuds institutions coexist.
- Council of Europe Congress. *The office of Ombudsman and local and regional authorities* — especially the line that ombuds services should be easy to access, properly staffed, independent, and able to handle complaints concerning all authorities providing public services at local and regional level.

### Review routes should be explicit, impartial, understandable, and challengeable

- OECD. *The Governance of Regulators* — especially the insistence that regulated actors should have an impartial and clearly explained route of appeal, timely written explanation of appeal rights, and clear information on how to make a complaint.
- Venice Commission / Council of Europe. *Principles on the Protection and Promotion of the Ombudsman Institution (the Venice Principles)* — especially the framing of ombuds institutions as part of democracy, rule of law, good administration, and human-rights protection.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0530

Current note: `622-emergency-homes-for-shared-territorial-power-emergency-operations-continuity-mutual-aid-recovery-and-no-government-by-crisis-summit.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Shared territorial government needs its own emergency and continuity constitution rather than crisis summit improvisation

- OECD/UN-Habitat. *Building resilience through disaster risk management in intermediary cities* — especially the insistence that resilience starts with capable institutions, empowered local governments, effective co-ordination across levels of government, updated legal mandates that clarify responsibilities in prevention, reaction, and rebuilding, and stronger local capacity for risk assessment, land-use planning, and emergency preparedness.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Resolution 500 (2024) — Local and regional responses to natural disasters and climate hazards: from risk preparedness to resilience* — especially the call for stronger solidarity across levels of governance, professionalised crisis management, adequate human and financial means for devolved missions, transparent and consistent decision-making, regular updating of response plans, effective vertical and horizontal co-ordination, regular exercises, and the combination of short-term action with long-term resilience.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Local authorities confronting natural disasters and emergencies* — especially the baseline that local authorities have roles in prevention, information, contingency planning, co-ordination of emergency services, reconstruction, and lessons learnt, rather than being merely passive recipients of command.

### An honest emergency home needs all-hazards planning, continuity discipline, and exercised leadership

- OECD. *Towards an All-Hazards Approach to Emergency Preparedness and Response* — especially the view that governments need crisis-management capacities for complexity and novelty, that emergency planning should encompass preparedness, response, and recovery, that continuity and emergency plans can be built for organisations, locations, hazards, and administrative units, and that exercises are essential for leaders as well as responders.
- UNDRR. *Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015-2030* — especially the priorities on understanding risk, strengthening disaster-risk governance, investing in resilience, and enhancing preparedness for effective response together with build-back-better recovery.
- UNDRR. *Words into Action guidelines: Implementation guide for local disaster risk reduction and resilience strategies* — especially the treatment of local strategies as context-specific, implementation-oriented, and explicit about assigning roles and responsibilities at the subnational level while remaining aligned with national strategy.

### Territorial emergency design must include infrastructure interdependence, information sharing, and service continuity

- OECD. *Good Governance for Critical Infrastructure Resilience* — especially the call for a system-based, all-hazards approach, governance structures that span sectors, secure information sharing with operators, and policy tools that sustain essential services and improve the capacity to rebound after shocks.
- OECD. *Ensuring the resilience of critical infrastructure: Government at a Glance 2025* — especially the emphasis on service continuity, whole-of-government systems thinking, prioritisation of critical vulnerabilities, information-sharing platforms, partnerships with operators, joint exercises, mutual assistance, and the need to address infrastructure resilience across the full life cycle.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

- OECD. *Digital public infrastructure: Government at a Glance 2025* — especially the treatment of data sharing, digital identity, base registries, digital post, payments, notifications, interoperability frameworks, API standards, and metadata standards as shared public enablers rather than app garnish.

## Sources used for rev0527

These sources informed the new pattern pack in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Shared territorial government needs its own buyer-or-commissioner architecture rather than inherited procurement residue

- OECD. *Recommendation of the Council on Public Procurement* — especially the treatment of public procurement as an integrated governance system rather than a clerical tail, with transparency, integrity, access, participation, efficiency, capacity, evaluation, risk management, accountability, and integration held together across the whole cycle.
- OECD. *Implementing the OECD Recommendation on Public Procurement in OECD and Partner Countries: 2020-2024 Report* — especially the reminder that the procurement life runs from defining public needs through contract execution, and that capacity, competition, transparency, integrity, and evaluation have to be implemented as a coherent system rather than in fragments.

### Delivery form, concessions, and PPPs should be chosen explicitly and docked to budget and service reality

- OECD. *Recommendation of the Council on Principles for Public Governance of Public-Private Partnerships* — especially the warning that PPPs can obscure real spending and risk if governments are not equipped to manage them, and the insistence that they be compared with traditional procurement and budget disciplines rather than treated as free money.

### Local and regional contract life needs transparency, expertise, and outsourced-service accountability

- Council of Europe Congress. *Making public procurement transparent at local and regional levels* — especially the warning that weak contracting expertise, lack of transparency, and outsourced-service accountability gaps create corruption and governance risk, together with the call to publish procurement details at all stages and strengthen internal controls.

## Sources used for rev0526

Current note: `618-regulatory-homes-for-shared-territorial-power-licensing-permits-standards-inspection-enforcement-and-no-government-by-operator-rulebook.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Real shared territorial government needs its own regulatory architecture rather than operator custom or host-government residue

- OECD. *The Governance of Regulators* — especially the treatment of regulatory powers, role clarity, objectives, accountability, and institutional relationships as a single governance architecture rather than an afterthought.
- OECD. *Governance of Regulators' Practices* — especially the practical emphasis on how regulators use powers, interact with other institutions, and preserve accountability while exercising binding authority over operators and markets.
- OECD. *Governing the City* — especially the metropolitan-governance concern that transport, land use, and other strategic sectors often sit in fragmented institutional arrangements that fail to coordinate effectively at urban scale.

### A regulatory home should use permissions, compliance, and enforcement proportionately and visibly

- OECD. *Licensing and Permitting* — especially the insistence that licensing authorities should use permissions only where risks justify them, keep requirements proportionate and understandable, and support compliance rather than governing through ambient bureaucracy.
- OECD/ITF. *Shaping the Relationship Between Public Transport and Innovative Mobility* — especially the observation that governance models which unify regulatory oversight and planning functions, define quality outcomes, and set contractual relationships materially shape mobility outcomes at territorial scale.
- OECD/ITF. *Competition and Regulation in the Provision of Local Transportation Services* — especially the point that regulators and public transport authorities must manage barriers to entry, tender design, and incumbent advantage rather than leaving competition and continuity to operator structure alone.

### Territorial regulation should stay lawful, proportionate, and challengeable rather than becoming a waiver constitution

- Council of Europe. *Explanatory report to the European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the explanation of Article 8 that administrative supervision must be lawful, proportionate, and normally confined to legality rather than expediency for own competences.
- Council of Europe / Venice Commission. *Updated Rule of Law Checklist* — especially the reminder that rule-of-law principles bind national, regional, and local authorities alike, sharpening the archive’s insistence that territorial regulation needs reasons, predictability, and review rather than side-letter discretion.

These sources informed the new pattern pack in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Shared territorial government needs a visible multi-year capital programme rather than disconnected project rounds

- OECD. *Effective Public Investment Toolkit* — especially the treatment of effective public investment as a multi-level governance problem organised around coordination, capacity, and framework conditions across the whole investment cycle.
- OECD. *Recommendation on the Governance of Infrastructure* — especially the insistence that infrastructure governance should cover how governments plan, prioritise, fund, budget, deliver, operate, and monitor assets across the full life cycle, with a long-term strategic vision linked to multi-year resource availability.

### Capital choices should distinguish funding from financing and stay tied to operations, maintenance, and repayment reality

- OECD. *Financing Cities of Tomorrow* — especially the distinction between funding and financing; the point that funding is what pays operations, maintenance, and financing repayment over time; and the wider emphasis that city and subnational investment needs reliable and predictable funding sources.
- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 9’s insistence that resources should be commensurate with responsibilities and that access to borrowing for capital investment should exist within the law.

### A capital home should manage assets over their whole life, not only at project launch

- OECD. *Management of assets throughout their life cycle* — especially the governance emphasis on asset condition, performance, accountability, professionalisation, and long-term costs across planning, construction, operation, maintenance, and end-of-life.
- OECD. *Management of asset performance throughout the life cycle: Government at a Glance 2025* — especially the evidence that many countries still do not systematically factor full life cycle costs, including operation, maintenance, and possible decommissioning, into project appraisal.

## Sources used for rev0524

Current note: `616-planning-homes-for-shared-territorial-power-territorial-plans-plan-hierarchies-conformity-and-no-government-by-vision-deck.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Shared territorial government needs a real planning spine, not just strategic rhetoric

- OECD. *Governing the City* — especially the treatment of transport and spatial planning as strategic sectors for metropolitan growth and the wider concern that fragmented metropolitan institutions often fail to coordinate them effectively.
- OECD. *The OECD Metropolitan Governance Survey* — especially the finding that organisations dedicated to metropolitan governance are common but often have little power, sharpening the archive’s question of when a shared territorial body needs a harder planning constitution.

### Spatial planning needs declared responsibilities, hierarchies, and implementation pathways across levels

- OECD. *Land-use Planning Systems in the OECD* — especially the cross-country emphasis on responsibilities of each level of government for spatial and land-use planning and on the hierarchical relations among plans.
- OECD. *The theory and practice of spatial planning and development* — especially the framing of spatial planning as long-term, publicly available planning by public authorities; the warning that overlapping plans can create incoherence and inefficiency; and the emphasis on planning hierarchies, inter-municipal co-ordination, and stakeholder consultation.
- OECD. *Governance of Land Use* — especially the insistence that land-use planning should be political and democratic, reflecting a shared community vision through engagement and communication.

### Territorial planning should be democratic, comprehensive, functional, long-term, and coordinated across authorities

- Council of Europe. *European Regional/Spatial Planning Charter (Torremolinos Charter)* — especially the classic insistence that spatial planning should be democratic, comprehensive, functional, and oriented to the longer term.
- Council of Europe. *12th European Conference of Ministers responsible for Regional Planning (Hanover, 2000)* — especially the treatment of sustainable spatial development as an integrated and regionally balanced strategy based on subsidiarity and reciprocity, and the insistence that national, regional, and local authorities all need active involvement in implementation.

### Real shared territorial government needs its own territorial evidence spine

- OECD. *Implementation Toolkit: OECD Recommendation on Regional Development Policy* — especially Pillar 5’s insistence on internationally comparable data and indicators at different territorial scales, the need for granular subnational indicators, harmonised methods, geospatial data, digital dissemination, and the warning against defining regional policy from national-level statistics alone.

- OECD. *OECD Local Data Portal* — especially the demonstration that subnational decisions require data-driven tools spanning demography, climate, transport, housing, services, finance, territorial organisation, and other place-based domains, with methods and sources made inspectable.

## Sources used for rev0521

These sources informed the new pattern pack in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Real shared territorial government needs direct civic-entry routes of its own

- Council of Europe Congress. *Additional Protocol on the right to participate in the affairs of a local authority* — especially the framing of participation as a right that authorities must facilitate through the measures needed to give it effect.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Citizen participation at local and regional level in Europe* — especially the insistence that local and regional authorities need dynamic communication policies, easy access to relevant information, and mechanisms to facilitate and evaluate participation.

### Deliberative and participatory methods should complement, not replace, representative institutions

- Council of Europe Congress. *Deliberative democracy* — especially the treatment of participation beyond elections as important but supplementary, and the endorsement of deliberative processes as complements to representative institutions at local and regional level.
- OECD. *OECD Guidelines for Citizen Participation Processes* — especially the ten-step design path, the distinction among several participation methods, and the warning that participation should be organised only where there is room for meaningful influence on decision-making.
- OECD. *Innovative public participation* — especially the framing of deliberative processes as ways to involve everyday people in solving complex issues while complementing representative democratic institutions.

- OECD. *Open government and citizen participation* — especially the current emphasis on inclusion, quality, and impact of participatory processes, and the connection between people feeling they have a say and higher trust in government.
- OECD. *Citizen participation and deliberation: Government at a Glance 2025* — especially the findings that participation at subnational level often offers the greatest return due to proximity; that cities and regions often show more innovation and more sustained participation over time; and that 103 of the 148 deliberative processes recorded between 2021 and 2023 were local or regional.

### Real shared territorial government needs a standing place in state decision-making and in relations with other tiers

- Council of Europe. *A contemporary commentary by the Congress on the Explanatory Report to the European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 4(6)’s insistence that local authorities should be consulted, insofar as possible, in due time and in an appropriate way on matters that concern them directly, and the commentary’s treatment of consultation as a key instrument of protection and promotion for local government.

## Sources used for rev0519

Current note: `611-public-law-homes-for-shared-territorial-power-legal-personality-named-competences-rulemaking-and-no-government-by-interlocal-contract.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Shared territorial power needs a public-law basis once it becomes real government

- Council of Europe. *The European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the insistence that local self-government and the basic powers and responsibilities of local authorities should be grounded in domestic law, and that authorities should have their own competences, structures, financial resources, and judicial recourse rather than existing only by administrative tolerance.
- Council of Europe. *Regional democracy — Council of Europe Reference Framework* — especially the treatment of regional authorities as bodies that may be provided for by the constitution or established by law, with judicial remedy for the free exercise of their powers, their own assets, administration, and staff, and foreseeable resources commensurate with competences.

### Metropolitan and regional legal form should match whether the body is still co-operation or has become a real territorial government

- OECD. *Approaches to Metropolitan Area Governance* — especially the distinction between policy-exchange forums, co-operation agreements, and bodies with the status of subnational government, together with the observation that some metropolitan bodies created for governance have the status of a full local government and therefore represent the most integrated approach.
- OECD. *The OECD Metropolitan Governance Survey* — especially the survey’s treatment of metropolitan bodies as varying in jurisdictional status, legal or regulatory powers, composition, and purpose, which sharpens the archive’s question of when shared territorial power needs a harder legal basis.
- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the distinction between co-operative regional bodies and stronger regional governments that are legal entities with their own budget, assets, administration, and decision-making power, together with the clarification that not every regional body has normative power.

## Sources used for rev0518

These sources informed the new pattern pack in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Real shared territorial government needs its own accountability framework rather than borrowed oversight

- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2022)2 on democratic accountability of elected representatives and elected bodies at local and regional level* — especially the insistence that accountability frameworks should comprise legislation, institutions, procedures, practices, and norms of conduct; that decisions should be reported on, explained, examined, questioned or sanctioned where appropriate; and that effective, proportionate remedies should exist for inappropriate decisions, omissions, and resulting actions or inactions.
- OECD. *Public integrity* — especially the Public Integrity Indicators framing that integrity systems need clear responsibilities, resources, monitoring, oversight, and sanctions, and that large gaps often remain between formal regulation and practical implementation.
- OECD. *Internal control and audit* — especially the argument that internal control, risk management, and internal audit are key to reducing fraud and corruption risk, supporting compliance, and using public resources responsibly.

### Openness, records, and complaint routes should follow the public function wherever the shared body acts

- Council of Europe. *Access to official documents – Guide* — especially the definition of public authorities as including national, regional, and local administration as well as private persons or bodies performing public functions or exercising administrative authority under national law; and the insistence on prompt handling, reasons for refusal, efficient records management, and active communication.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Transparency and open government* — especially the framing of open government around transparency, participation, and accountability, and the local-government relevance of publishing spending, contracts, and decision processes in intelligible form.

### Regional and metropolitan institutions vary widely in status and power, so their accountability stack should be matched to their real role

- OECD. *The OECD Metropolitan Governance Survey* — especially the finding that metropolitan bodies vary in legal status, composition, powers, budgets, and staffing, which sharpens the archive’s question of when derivative accountability is no longer enough.
- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the argument that stronger regional governance can enhance transparency and accountability, and the distinction between co-operative regional bodies and stronger self-governing regional authorities.

These sources informed the new pattern pack in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Real regional or metropolitan government needs its own administration, staff, and operating core

- Council of Europe. *Regional democracy — Council of Europe Reference Framework* — especially the principles that regional authorities should have their own assets, their own administration and staff, freedom to determine internal administrative structures, merit-based recruitment, and conditions of service that support capable public administration.
- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the distinction between co-operative regional bodies and stronger regional governments that are legal entities with their own autonomous budget, assets, administration, and decision-making power.
- OECD. *Making Decentralisation Work* — especially the definition of subnational administrative capacity as the ability to perform mandatory functions effectively, efficiently, and sustainably, plus the warning that capacity building must be systemic rather than reduced to occasional training.

### Metropolitan implementation quality depends on whether powers, budgets, and staff are actually matched to the work

- OECD. *Governing the City* — especially the argument that institutional fragmentation slows integration of transport and spatial planning, and that one authority or genuinely integrated arrangements can reduce co-ordination failure where metropolitan system choices are real.
- OECD. *Effective Public Investment Toolkit* — especially the framing that effective multilevel investment depends not only on co-ordination but also on capacity and sound framework conditions across levels of government.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0516

Current note: `608-fiscal-homes-for-shared-territorial-power-own-source-revenue-predictable-equalization-capital-discipline-and-no-metropolitan-government-by-begging-bowl.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Real subnational and territorial government needs a financial base proportionate to its work

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 9’s insistence on resources of local authorities’ own, commensurability between responsibilities and resources, diversified and buoyant finance, equalisation for weaker authorities, consultation on redistributed resources, non-earmarked grants as the general preference, and access to capital markets for investment within the law.
- OECD. *Subnational finance and investment* — especially the current framing that subnational governments carry large shares of public expenditure and investment and depend on a mix of taxes, grants, charges, and debt rather than one single funding lane.
- OECD. *OECD Fiscal Decentralisation Database* — especially the comparative tax-autonomy and transfer-revenue indicators that make it possible to distinguish own or rule-based fiscal capacity from dependence on intergovernmental transfers alone.

### Metropolitan and regional fiscal design should fit real functional territories rather than administrative leftovers

- OECD. *OECD Definition of Cities and Functional Urban Areas* — especially the insistence that metropolitan territories often exceed single municipal borders because commuting, labour-market, and economic linkages are real.
- OECD. *Approaches to Metropolitan Area Governance* — especially the comparative overview of the organisational forms through which metropolitan areas are governed, which sharpens the archive’s question of what fiscal constitution should follow when these bodies become real governments rather than mere co-ordination tables.
- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the distinction between co-operative regional bodies and stronger regional governments with their own budgets, wider funding mixes, and in many cases directly elected deliberative structures.
