## Sources used for rev0515

Current note: `607-democratic-homes-for-shared-territorial-power-indirect-councils-double-legitimacy-direct-election-and-no-metropolitan-rule-by-proxy.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Metropolitan democratic legitimacy is a live design problem, not a decorative afterthought

- OECD. *The OECD Metropolitan Governance Survey* — especially the finding that most metropolitan governance bodies rely on indirect representation, only a small minority are directly elected, and democratic-deficit and trust concerns remain central as these bodies take on wider territorial roles.
- OECD. *Governing the City* — especially the argument that choosing a metropolitan governance arrangement requires trade-offs across policy co-ordination, institutional/financial capacity, and perceived legitimacy and trust.

### New metropolitan structures should be democratically accountable without eroding local democracy

- Council of Europe Congress. *Good governance of large metropolises* — especially the insistence that metropolitan governance structures should not be a pretext for recentralisation, should remain democratically accountable, should preserve a clear division of competences, and may secure legitimacy either through direct elections or through appointment of elected representatives from component local governments.
- Council of Europe. *Regional democracy — Council of Europe Reference Framework* — especially the use of the framework as a reference code when countries establish or reform regional authorities and need to specify democratic structure, competences, resources, and relations with other authorities.

### Comparative regional practice supports a ladder from co-operative bodies toward directly elected regional government as powers deepen

- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the distinction between co-operative regional bodies with limited tasks and stronger regional governments with elected bodies, together with the argument that directly elected regional levels can strengthen democracy, transparency, and accountability when powers and responsibilities justify them.

## Sources used for rev0514

Current note: `606-when-co-operation-becomes-government-thresholds-for-constitutionalizing-shared-territorial-power-and-no-metropolitan-rule-by-consortium.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Co-operation is a legitimate multilevel tool, but it sits inside a broader decentralisation and regionalisation design problem

- OECD. *Decentralisation* — especially the current framing that decentralisation is political, administrative, and fiscal at once; that sequencing matters; and that upscaling of subnational governance commonly occurs through inter-municipal co-operation, metropolitan governance, and regionalisation.
- OECD. *Making Decentralisation Work* — especially the treatment of municipal co-operation, metropolitan governance, and stronger regional tiers as linked responses to fragmentation rather than as interchangeable labels.

### Metropolitan and regional upgrade should be keyed to real functional territories and the institutional forms used to govern them

- OECD. *OECD Definition of Cities and Functional Urban Areas* — especially the insistence that functional urban areas reflect meaningful economic and commuting linkages beyond single municipal borders.
- OECD. *Approaches to Metropolitan Area Governance* — especially the comparative overview of the different organisational forms used to govern metropolitan areas.
- OECD. *The OECD Metropolitan Governance Survey* — especially the finding that dedicated metropolitan governance organisations are common but often weak, which sharpens the archive’s concern with bodies that already govern shared territories without a commensurate democratic home.
- Council of Europe. *Regional democracy — Council of Europe Reference Framework* — especially the use of the framework as a reference code when countries establish or reform regional authorities and need to specify democratic structure, competences, resources, and relations with other authorities.

### Intermunicipal co-operation should stay available, but not as a cover for hidden territorial government

- Council of Europe. *Improving the quality of the services to citizens through inter-municipal co-operation (IMC)* — especially the treatment of IMC as inherent in decentralised administration and as a way to counter excessive fragmentation for service delivery.
- Council of Europe. *The Charter: how it works* / *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the Charter’s protection of local self-government, elected local bodies, own structures and resources, and the broader expectation that local authorities be treated as real constitutional actors rather than as disposable administrative fragments.

## Sources used for rev0513

These sources informed the new pattern pack in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Multi-level reform should be treated as a menu of institutional instruments rather than a one-tool ideology

- OECD. *Multi-level Governance Reforms* — especially the framing of multilevel reform as institutional, territorial, and public-management change together; the insistence that there is no one-size-fits-all formula; and the emphasis on co-ordination, dialogue, and evaluation rather than one-shot structural fixes.
- OECD. *Effective Public Investment Toolkit* — especially the practical three-pillar focus on coordination, capacity, and framework conditions, together with the current guidance on diverse forms of inter-municipal cooperation and pooled investment machinery.

### Cooperation is often a legitimate intermediate repair between atomized local incapacity and forced merger

- OECD. *Inter-municipal co-operation in the Western Balkans* — especially the treatment of IMC as a tool for economies of scale, local capacity, and co-ordination in fragmented systems, rather than merely as a politically softer synonym for amalgamation.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Tools on Good Democratic Governance* / Territorial Amalgamation Tool — especially the insistence that amalgamation should be approached as one possible remedy to territorial fragmentation rather than as the automatic answer.

### Territorial surgery should come late, with alternatives tested, consultation visible, and continuity risks named

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 5’s rule that changes in local authority boundaries should not be made without prior consultation of the local communities concerned.
- Council of Europe. *Territorial reforms in Europe: Does size matter?* — especially the toolkit’s treatment of alternative scenarios, inter-municipal cooperation, implementation steps, and the need to think through unwanted side effects and continuity questions before and during amalgamation reform.

### Metropolitan repair should become an honest democratic upgrade when shared politics and systems are already real

- OECD. *Governing the City* — especially the typology of metropolitan governance arrangements and the guidance for more effective co-ordination in transport and spatial planning.
- World Bank. *International Practices of Metropolitan Governance* — especially the comparative focus on collaborative arrangements in metropolitan areas and the reminder that metropolitan governance can take several forms, from cooperative arrangements to stronger institutional consolidation.

## Sources used for rev0512

Current note: `604-diagnosing-scope-misfit-overbuild-underbuild-duplicate-chains-orphan-functions-and-no-government-by-institutional-furniture.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Local and regional self-government need a real institutional, legal, and fiscal basis before lower tiers can be judged honestly

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the Charter’s insistence that local self-government rests on political, administrative, and financial independence and on elected local authorities rather than decorative decentralisation.
- Council of Europe. *Regional democracy — Council of Europe Reference Framework* — especially the use of the framework as a reference code when countries establish or reform regional authorities and need to specify competences, resources, and relations with other authorities.

### Responsibility assignment should be clear enough to reduce duplication, yet realistic enough to acknowledge shared fields

- OECD. *Assigning responsibilities across levels of government* — especially the argument that decentralisation outcomes depend on design, that responsibility assignment is central to effectiveness, and that clearer assignment is needed to reduce duplication and overlap without pretending all shared competences can disappear.
- OECD. *Making Decentralisation Work* — especially the warning that decentralisation outcomes depend greatly on design and implementation, and that overlapping assignments can produce duplication, waste, and accountability problems when functions are not fitted to the right tier.

### Real multilevel systems should be checked for capacity, co-ordination, and fit to functional scale, not only for constitutional elegance

- OECD. *Effective Public Investment Toolkit* — especially the current three-pillar structure around coordination, capacity, and framework conditions, plus its use of self-assessment and fit-to-scale public investment governance.
- OECD. *The governance of public service delivery across territories* — especially the view that central government typically owns policy design, standards, and oversight while regional and local tiers focus on provision and administration, with the precise split depending on economies of scale, distribution of benefits, and externalities.
- OECD. *The OECD Metropolitan Governance Survey* — especially the finding that metropolitan governance organisations are common but often weak, and that their existence is associated with better outcomes despite fragmentation pressures in large urban systems.

### Polycentric analysis is a better diagnostic lens than monocentric map tidiness

- Nobel Prize Outreach. *Elinor Ostrom – Prize Lecture* (*Beyond Markets and States: Polycentric Governance of Complex Economic Systems*) — especially the enduring argument that complex public goods are often governed through problem-fitted, overlapping, polycentric arrangements rather than one neat monocentric hierarchy.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

- Nobel Prize Outreach. *Elinor Ostrom – Prize Lecture* (*Beyond Markets and States: Polycentric Governance of Complex Economic Systems*) — especially the argument that complex public goods and public industries are often governed through diverse, overlapping, problem-fitted arrangements rather than one monocentric hierarchy.

Current note: `602-ideal-governments-of-each-scope-steward-cells-municipal-general-government-metropolitan-systems-democracy-regional-territorial-platforms-national-guarantor-states-continental-compacts-and-global-treaty-narrow-waists.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Local and regional self-government need political, administrative, and financial independence, not just ceremonial elections

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the statement that the Charter guarantees the political, administrative, and financial independence of local authorities, that local self-government should be recognised in domestic law and, where practicable, in the constitution, and that local authorities are to be elected through universal suffrage.
- Council of Europe. *Regional democracy — Council of Europe Reference Framework* — especially the use of the framework as a reference code of rights and duties for regional entities when countries establish or reform regional authorities.

### Metropolitan and regional layers exist because real functional systems and territorial public goods live above the municipality

- OECD. *The OECD Metropolitan Governance Survey* — especially the finding that metropolitan governance organisations are common but often weak, and that their existence is associated with better outcomes on transport, environmental issues, and urban sprawl.
- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the evidence that regional governance reforms have deepened in many countries, that regions occupy a privileged co-ordination position for place-based policy, and that governance models differ because territorial functions and capacities differ.

### Complex public goods are usually governed best through differentiated and polycentric arrangements rather than one single monocentric hierarchy

- Nobel Prize Outreach. *Elinor Ostrom – Prize Lecture* (*Beyond Markets and States: Polycentric Governance of Complex Economic Systems*) — especially the enduring polycentric argument against one-size monocentric governance for complex multi-scale public goods.

### The global layer should stay treaty-bound and problem-specific: peace, sovereign equality, and cross-border health alerting are real global functions, but still narrow ones

- United Nations. *UN Charter* / *Purposes and Principles of the UN* — especially the Charter’s framing of the United Nations around peace, sovereign equality, and peaceful settlement rather than general domestic government.
- World Health Organization. *International Health Regulations* — especially the description of the IHR as a legally binding international-law framework defining states’ rights and obligations for cross-border public-health risks, with WHO responsible for global early warning and coordination rather than routine domestic administration.

## Sources used for rev0509

Current note: `601-publicity-of-local-deliberation-open-meetings-agenda-notice-closed-session-limits-and-no-government-by-chamber-obscurity.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Public local deliberation requires more than elections: meetings must be open, noticed, and connected to citizens and records

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 3.2’s rule that local self-government is exercised by councils or assemblies composed of freely elected members, which implies a real public representative forum rather than hidden executive substitution.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Relations between the public, the local assembly and the executive in local democracy* — especially the insistence that public meetings of the local assembly should be the rule and closed sessions the exception, that authorities should inform the public in advance of overall policies and publicise decisions and debates widely, and that the public should be able to put questions to local officials.

### Participation and access-to-information standards sharpen the difference between publicity, inclusion, and secrecy

- Council of Europe. *Explanatory Report to the Additional Protocol to the European Charter of Local Self-Government on the right to participate in the affairs of a local authority* — especially the rule that Parties must maintain a legislative framework facilitating participation in local affairs and the explanation that access to official information is structurally linked to that right.
- Council of Europe. *The Council of Europe Convention on Access to Official Documents (Tromsø Convention)* — especially the current statement that everyone has a right to access official documents held by public authorities, that documents are in principle public subject only to listed exceptions, and that minimum standards include prompt and fair handling plus review and appeal routes.

### Open meetings are one method inside a larger participation constitution, not a substitute for it

- OECD. *OECD Guidelines for Citizen Participation Processes* — especially the treatment of open meetings/town hall meetings as one participation method among several and the insistence that participation processes should be designed only where there is room for meaningful influence.
- OECD. *Open government and citizen participation* — especially the current emphasis that transparency and access to information are core principles of open government, that people who feel they have a say report higher trust, and that stronger citizen voice and participatory design are needed alongside transparency.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0508

Current note: `600-organizational-autonomy-internal-administrative-structures-standing-orders-and-no-self-government-by-template-lock.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Organizational autonomy is part of local self-government, not a discretionary courtesy

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 6.1’s rule that local authorities shall be able to determine their own internal administrative structures, within a general statutory framework, so they can adapt them to local needs and ensure effective management.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Contemporary commentary of the Congress on the Explanatory Report of the European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the interpretation that the power to organise internal affairs is part of local autonomy, should remain broad, may vary with executive form, and may include subordinate units, local companies or agencies, and agreements with other local authorities.

### Current monitoring practice still treats internal organizational discretion as a live design issue

- Council of Europe Congress. *Monitoring of the application of the European Charter of Local Self-Government in Slovenia* — especially the finding that Slovenian law leaves considerable room for municipal by-laws and statutes to determine internal organisation and the formation and functioning of municipal bodies.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Monitoring of the application of the European Charter of Local Self-Government in Poland* — especially the restatement that Article 6.1 safeguards local autonomy by allowing authorities to establish internal administrative structures and that local governments should retain room to create independent bodies to deliver local services.

### Comparative practice shows that there is no one dominant organizational model even within decentralised regional government

- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the evidence that decentralised regions share broad democratic features but still use different executive arrangements and governance structures, confirming that lawful self-government does not require one fixed internal template.
- Council of Europe. *Regional Democracy Reference Framework* — especially the rule that regional authorities shall freely determine the internal structures of their administrative system and their bodies within the framework defined by law.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0507

Current note: `599-local-and-regional-assembly-scrutiny-rights-agenda-power-questions-committee-capacity-and-no-majoritarian-blackout.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Local self-government exercised by assemblies requires real supervisory capacity

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 3.2’s rule that the right of local self-government is exercised by councils or assemblies composed of members freely elected by secret ballot, and that those bodies may possess executive organs responsible to them.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Relations between the public, the local assembly and the executive in local democracy (Recommendation 113 (2002))* — especially the insistence that the representative assembly must deal with matters of greatest importance to the local community, that executive “responsibility” requires effective supervision by the assembly, and that members need essential rights to request meetings, place items on the agenda, and put written or oral questions to the executive.

### Scrutiny must be resourced, politically fair, and protected against information asymmetry

- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2022)2 on democratic accountability of elected representatives and elected bodies at local and regional level* — especially the guidance that scrutiny should be supported by adequate rules, timely and meaningful information, sufficient time and expertise, equal information to all members irrespective of political considerations, and fair access of political groups to specific scrutiny functions.

### Current monitoring practice still treats assembly responsibility and executive accountability as live design issues

- Council of Europe Congress. *Monitoring of the application of the European Charter of Local Self-Government in Iceland* (2024) — especially the current restatement that representative assemblies are the bodies mandated to deal with matters of utmost local importance such as budgetary or tax issues, and that the executive’s responsibility to the elected council is a primary form of political accountability under Article 3.2.

## Sources used for rev0506

Current note: `598-conditions-of-office-for-local-elected-representatives-free-mandate-fair-compensation-lawful-removal-and-no-self-government-by-attrition.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Free exercise of local elected office requires more than elections alone

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 7’s rule that the conditions of office of local elected representatives must provide for free exercise of their functions, appropriate financial compensation, and law-set incompatibilities.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Contemporary commentary of the Congress on the Explanatory Report of the European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the interpretation that free exercise covers not only pay, but also facilities, ICT, training, flexible meeting planning, protection against threats and privacy abuse, and safeguards against arbitrary prosecution or overbroad incompatibility rules.

### Compensation and operating support are democratic access rules, not luxuries

- Council of Europe Congress. *The Congress calls for proper compensation for local and regional elected representatives* — especially the insistence that material concerns should not prevent citizens from standing for office, that compensation should be commensurate with responsibilities, and that published salary scales and transparency help resist both exclusion and corruption risk.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Adopted Texts* — especially the 2019 Congress package on financial compensation of local and regional elected representatives, which frames adequate, transparent compensation as part of effective democratic local governance.

### Arbitrary dismissal, trustee substitution, and deprivation of mandate still threaten local democracy in live systems

- Council of Europe Congress. *Dismissal of Mayor of Van: Statement by Congress co-Rapporteurs on local democracy in Türkiye* — especially the warning that replacing democratically elected opposition mayors with trustees undermines the very nature of democracy.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Council of Europe Congress completes second fact-finding visit to Türkiye* — especially the 2025 evidence that dismissed or suspended local elected representatives and trustee replacement remain live practical threats to the exercise of local democratic mandates.

### Distinguishing general-purpose metropolitan governance from single-purpose authorities

- OECD. *The OECD Metropolitan Governance Survey* — especially the distinction between governance bodies and single-issue sectoral authorities, the insistence on thematic width as a separator between general metropolitan governance and single-purpose bodies, and the evidence that transport authorities are widespread and often high-budget special-purpose institutions.

- World Bank. *International Practices of Metropolitan Governance: A Compendium of Collaborative Arrangements in Metropolitan Areas* — especially the discussion of special-purpose districts as a common response in fragmented systems and the practical risk that proliferating independent special-purpose bodies makes coordination across interrelated urban functions more difficult.
- World Bank. *Strengthening Institutions for Urban and Metropolitan Governance* — especially the description of metropolitan-wide or special-purpose districts for urban services and the warning that politics rather than efficiency or equity can dominate when fragmented autonomous agencies proliferate.

## Sources used for rev0503

Current note: `595-territorial-reorganization-incorporation-mergers-splits-status-change-and-no-map-surgery-by-managerial-fiat.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Territorial reorganization is lawful only if consultation is real and timely

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 5’s rule that changes in local authority boundaries shall not be made without prior consultation of the local communities concerned, possibly by means of a referendum where statute permits.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Contemporary commentary on the Explanatory Report of the European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the clarification that Article 5 does not forbid mergers or impose one territorial model, but it does make timely consultation mandatory, interprets “local communities” broadly enough to include residents as well as authorities, and treats Article 5 logic as relevant not only to line changes but also to wider restructuring of local-government systems.
- Council of Europe Congress. *The right of local authorities to be consulted by other levels of government* — especially the insistence that consultation should be clearly defined, transparent, timely enough to influence outcomes, and treated as a required part of policy making and legislation rather than as symbolic aftercare.

### Current evidence that compulsory territorial reform still turns on consultation quality

- Council of Europe Congress. *Monitoring of the application of the European Charter of Local Self-Government in Finland* (2024) — especially the concern about unclear consultation procedures for obligatory merger or compulsory municipal boundary change in difficult financial situations, and the recommendation to establish such procedures.

### Merger is one tool, not the only tool

- OECD. *Multi-level Governance Reforms* — especially the comparative discussion of voluntary municipal amalgamations, the use of incentives, and the fact that merger negotiations and referendum-based consultation can shape or block reform outcomes.
- OECD. *Making multi-level governance work for Slovenia’s regions* (2026) — especially the suggestion that governments may need to consider alternatives to promoting mergers in order to strengthen municipal capacity, reinforcing the archive’s preference for an alternatives test before territorial extinction or forced amalgamation.

## Sources used for rev0502

Current note: `594-local-authority-associations-consortia-transfrontier-co-operation-and-no-self-government-in-isolation.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### The Charter’s three distinct associational rights

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 10’s distinct protection for local authorities to co-operate and form consortia with one another, to belong to associations for the protection and promotion of their common interests, and to co-operate with counterparts in other States under law.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Contemporary commentary on the Explanatory Report of the European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the insistence that each paragraph of Article 10 sets out a specific right, that intermunicipal co-operation is a fundamental tool for authorities that are too small or weak to deliver alone, that separate joint organisations may take different legal forms but should not dilute transparency or accountability, and that representative associations play a fundamental role in consultation, lobbying, training, and sometimes legal defence.

### Co-operative regions and regional associations as a real institutional form

- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the distinction between regional associations of municipalities, ordinary intermunicipal co-operation, and representative local-government associations; the requirement that co-operative regions have legal status and agreement from member municipalities; and the observation that such arrangements often preserve municipal authority while achieving economies of scale for region-wide tasks and shared services.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0501

Current note: `593-local-self-government-judicial-remedy-constitutional-standing-interim-relief-and-no-autonomy-without-recourse.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Legal protection of local self-government as a constitutional requirement

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 11’s rule that local authorities must have the right of recourse to a judicial remedy to secure the free exercise of their powers and respect for local self-government as recognized in domestic law.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Contemporary commentary on the Explanatory Report of the European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the explanation that Article 11 requires effective access by local authorities to an independent court or equivalent body so they can invoke and defend local self-government when their rights or powers are challenged or endangered by higher levels.

### Current evidence that institutional standing and constitutional-court access materially matter

- Council of Europe Congress. *Monitoring of the application of the European Charter of Local Self-Government in the Republic of Moldova* (2025) — especially the positive treatment of ordinary-court standing plus the direct complaint procedure allowing local authorities to appeal to the Constitutional Court where the principle of local autonomy is contravened.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Monitoring of the application of the European Charter of Local Self-Government in Poland* (2025) — especially the discussion of local authorities’ ability to submit complaints concerning constitutional infringements to the Constitutional Tribunal, reinforcing the value of a visible apex-review lane for institutional autonomy claims.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Compliance of Norwegian legislation with Article 11 of the European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the insistence that local authorities must enjoy the full exercise of judicial remedies against state-administration decisions in order for Article 11 protection to be real in practice.

### Comparative grounding on legal autonomy as part of local autonomy itself

- OECD. *Local autonomy, government quality and fragmentation* — especially the use of **legal autonomy** as a component of local autonomy, including constitutional clauses and recourse to the judicial system to settle disputes with higher authorities.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0500

Current note: `592-local-ordinance-power-bylaws-framework-laws-review-lanes-and-no-self-government-by-administration-only.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Local self-government requires more than administrative discretion

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 4’s rules that local authorities should have full discretion to exercise initiative within law, that public responsibilities should generally sit closest to the citizen, and that local powers should normally be full and exclusive.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Contemporary commentary on the Explanatory Report of the European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the treatment of Article 4 as protecting local initiative, full and exclusive powers, narrow interpretation of limitations, and meaningful local authority within the legal order rather than administrative dependency.

### Current evidence that weak local regulatory power hollows self-government

- Council of Europe Congress. *Monitoring of the application of the European Charter of Local Self-Government in Greece* (2025) — especially the finding that local authorities lack the power to approve binding local regulations except in limited cases, reducing their ability to regulate and manage a substantial share of public affairs under their own responsibility.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Monitoring of the application of the European Charter of Local Self-Government in Poland* (2025) — especially the current discussion of Article 94 of the Constitution recognizing the law-making power of local and regional authorities within limits specified by law, and the linked insistence on clearer distribution of competences and full/exclusive local powers.

### Multi-level regulatory quality and the value of capable local autonomy

- OECD. *Multi-Level Regulatory Governance* — especially the warning that regulatory quality at one level of government can be undermined by poor regulatory practice at another, which supports a clearer architecture for lawful local rulemaking rather than untyped overlap or central improvisation.
- OECD. *Local autonomy, government quality and fragmentation* — especially the argument that local autonomy interacts with government quality and fragmentation, reinforcing the need for local rulemaking power to be paired with capable institutions rather than treated as either magic or danger by itself.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0499

Current note: `591-concurrent-competences-lead-lane-typing-paramountcy-defaults-and-no-government-by-overlap-fog.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Overlap, clarity, and the need for clear legislative provisions in shared fields

- Council of Europe. *Explanatory Report to the European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the treatment of overlapping responsibilities: powers should normally be full and exclusive, but where complementary action by different levels is required, the intervention of central or regional authorities should rest on clear legislative provisions.
- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 4's rules on subsidiarity, full and exclusive powers, discretion in delegated tasks where possible, and consultation on matters directly affecting local authorities.

### Current OECD guidance on role clarity and concerted action across levels

- OECD. *Implementing regional development strategies: A practitioner's guide* (2025) — especially the insistence that implementation in multi-level governance systems requires sound governance arrangements, policy stewardship at all levels, and governance structures that support concerted action rather than fragmented execution.
- OECD. *Recommendations on Enhancing the Skills Governance System in Sweden* — especially the statement that effective decentralisation hinges on clear delineations of responsibilities ("who does what") across levels of government, alongside robust horizontal and vertical coordination mechanisms.

## Sources used for rev0498

Current note: `590-multilevel-impact-packets-subsidiarity-territorial-effects-rural-proofing-and-no-lawmaking-by-placeless-abstraction.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Consultation as a constitutional right and a required part of policymaking

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 4.6’s rule that local authorities should be consulted in due time and in an appropriate way on matters that concern them directly.
- Council of Europe Congress. *The right of local authorities to be consulted by other levels of government* — especially the insistence that consultation should be a required part of policy making and the legislative process, that implications for self-government and economic consequences should be analysed carefully, and that written reasons should be given when proposals from local or regional authorities are not retained.

### Current subsidiarity, proportionality, and impact-assessment practice

- European Commission. *Better regulation* and *Annual Report 2024 on the application of the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality and on relations with national Parliaments* — especially the current rules that subsidiarity and proportionality are analysed in impact assessments, that a subsidiarity grid is attached to politically sensitive and important initiatives accompanied by an impact assessment, and that these assessments are reviewed through an independent quality-control lane.

### Territorial effects, differentiated regional impacts, and rural proofing

- OECD. *Implementation Toolkit: OECD Recommendation on Regional Development Policy* — especially the guidance to engage subnational stakeholders early, review and adapt sectoral policies for differentiated regional impacts, and design sectoral policies with territorial diversity in mind.
- OECD. *Rural proofing: Lessons from OECD countries and potential application to health* and *Reinforcing Rural Resilience* — especially the current account of screening upcoming initiatives for differential territorial impacts, using a pre-territorial assessment necessity check, and escalating to full territorial impact assessment where impacts are likely to be substantial.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0497

Current note: `589-general-competence-home-rule-local-charters-municipal-initiative-and-no-government-by-ultra-vires-fragment.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### General competence, local initiative, and the legal envelope of local self-government

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 4.2's rule that local authorities should, within law, have full discretion to exercise initiative on matters not excluded from their competence nor assigned elsewhere, read together with Article 4.4 on full and exclusive powers and Article 4.6 on consultation.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Contemporary commentary on the Explanatory Report of the European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the treatment of Article 4.2 as a protection for local initiative, the comparison between **ultra vires** traditions and **general competence** clauses, and the warning that overregulation can hollow local autonomy even where formal status looks strong.

### Current comparative practice and examples of general local competence

- Council of Europe Congress. *Monitoring of the application of the European Charter of Local Self-Government in Poland* (2025) — especially the current restatement that Article 4.2 requires local governments to be able to initiate tasks at their own discretion when those tasks are not reserved to another authority, and the Polish example of a general local-task clause.
- European Committee of the Regions. *France Introduction* and *Germany Introduction* pages in the Division of Powers portal — especially the comparative examples that French local and regional authorities are described as having general competence, while German municipalities are constitutionally guaranteed the right to regulate all local affairs on their own responsibility within the limits of the laws.

### Autonomy versus agency in decentralised systems

- OECD. *Making Decentralisation Work* — especially the warning that fiscal or expenditure decentralisation can be misleading where subnational governments function mainly as agencies funded and regulated by the center rather than as independent policymakers with real room for manoeuvre.

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0496

Current note: `588-delegated-state-tasks-local-discretion-agent-status-funding-and-no-mixed-accountability-blur.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Own versus delegated local powers, local discretion, and consultation

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* and the Congress *Contemporary commentary on the Explanatory Report* — especially the distinction between full and exclusive own local powers and delegated powers, the insistence that delegated tasks should leave room for adaptation to local conditions where possible, and the broader consultation rule for matters that concern local authorities directly.

### Typed supervision, differentiated control, and support for delegated tasks

- Council of Europe Committee of Ministers. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2019)3 on supervision of local authorities’ activities* — especially the current guidance that supervision should distinguish own from delegated competences, define supervisory remits in law, reserve broader expediency control mainly for delegated tasks, limit prior approval to narrow cases, and complement supervision with advice and assistance.

### Delegation as a weaker form of decentralisation than devolution

- OECD. *The governance of public service delivery across territories* — especially the treatment of delegation as a transfer of limited decision-making and administrative authority for specific tasks, under which subnational governments act as agents of the central government and delegated tasks may be altered or withdrawn.

## Sources used for rev0495

Current note: `587-deconcentrated-state-administration-prefects-field-offices-and-no-shadow-local-government.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Territorial state presence versus democratic self-government

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* and the Congress *Contemporary commentary on the Explanatory Report* — especially the insistence that local self-government means democratically constituted authorities with real autonomy, and that administrative supervision by central or regional government must stay lawful, proportionate, and ordinarily legality-bound except for delegated tasks.

### Deconcentrated regions, their uses, and their limits

- OECD. *Regional Governance in OECD Countries* — especially the treatment of deconcentrated regions as a distinct institutional type, the Portugal example, and the warning that they can be easy to establish yet weak on autonomy and citizens' accountability.
- OECD. *Decentralisation and Regionalisation in Portugal* — especially the argument that deconcentrated central-government regional units may help coordinate and manage programs but lack the status and legitimacy of an elected regional government when real place-shaping discretion is needed.

### Clarifying roles, forums, and centralised mindsets

- UNDP. *Multi-Level Governance in Crisis-Affected Settings* — especially the current lesson that decentralisation requires clear delineation of responsibilities, involvement of each level of government in multilevel-governance forums, local capacity development, and a shift away from centralised mindsets if territorial governance is to become more than field administration.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

### Function transfer as transfer of operating means, not just legal authority

- OECD. *Decentralisation* — especially the current definition of decentralisation as the transfer of powers, responsibilities, and resources from central to elected subnational authorities.
- OECD. *Making Decentralisation Work* — especially the treatment of institutional reform as including the transfer of **tasks, assets, human and fiscal means** across levels of government, and the insistence that decentralisation outcomes depend on how reforms are designed and implemented rather than on legal headline alone.

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 4’s rules on consultation and delegated local discretion, Article 5’s rule on consultation for boundary changes, Article 6’s insistence on appropriate administrative structures and competent local staff, and Article 9’s broader resource-congruence logic.

### Clarity of responsibilities, capacity-building, and transition discipline

- UNDP. *Multi-Level Governance in Crisis-Affected Settings* — especially the current lesson that decentralisation must be accompanied by local-level capacity development, investment in clarifying and delineating responsibilities, formal multi-level forums, and fiscal arrangements that are not undermined by central control.

## Sources used for rev0493

Current note: `585-conditional-grants-matching-funds-competitive-programs-and-no-government-by-grant-maze.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Local fiscal discretion, consultation, and limits on earmarking

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 9’s insistence that local authorities have adequate resources of their own, that financially weaker authorities be protected through equalisation, that authorities be consulted on the allocation of redistributed resources, and that grants should as far as possible not be earmarked in ways that remove local policy discretion.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Monitoring of the application of the European Charter of Local Self-Government in Slovenia* — especially the current restatement of Article 9’s rules on equalisation, consultation, and the presumption against excessive earmarking of grants.

### Co-ordinated investment, capacity, and reducing administrative burden across grant systems

- OECD Legal Instruments. *Effective Public Investment Across Levels of Government* and OECD. *Effective Public Investment Toolkit* — especially the view that co-financing arrangements, contracts, consultation, and other co-ordination tools can be justified where investment coherence is genuinely needed, but should be paired with adequate resources, capacity, and minimisation of administrative barriers across levels of government.
- OECD. *Place-Based Policies for the Future* — especially the argument that place-based policy requires effective multi-level co-ordination, adequate subnational funding, stronger local capacity, and less one-size-fits-all control if diverse places are to deliver long-term development well.

### Conditional grant design, coherence, and avoiding proliferating compliance regimes

- World Bank. *Conditional Grants in Principle, in Practice and in Operations: A Primer* — especially the treatment of conditional grants as a diverse design family rather than one instrument, the warning that fragmented and ad hoc grant systems raise administrative burden and weaken local coherence, and the recommendation to use more integrated rules and procedures so local governments are not overloaded by incompatible grant-management systems.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Common floors, local discretion, and legality-bound override

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 4’s rules that public responsibilities should generally be exercised by the authorities closest to the citizen, that local authorities should have full and exclusive powers unless limited by law, that delegated tasks should leave room for local adaptation, and that local authorities should be consulted on matters that concern them directly.

- OECD. *Implementation Toolkit: OECD Recommendation on Regional Development Policy* — especially the insistence that regional development strategies recognise territorial diversity, engage subnational stakeholders early, align national objectives with subnational strategies, and avoid one-size-fits-all policy design that ignores place-specific context.

## Sources used for rev0491

Current note: `583-subnational-distress-administrative-supervision-special-measures-handback-clocks-and-no-silent-recentralization.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Lawful supervision, proportionality, and reviewable intervention

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 8’s rules that supervision must rest on law, should normally be confined to legality review, and must remain proportionate to the interests protected, together with Article 11’s insistence on legal protection of local self-government.
- Council of Europe Congress. *Contemporary commentary on the Explanatory Report of the European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the current interpretation that supervision is itself a form of intervention in local decision-making, that ad hoc supervisory powers are prohibited, and that proportionality should shape both the type and intensity of control.
- Council of Europe Committee of Ministers. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2019)3 on supervision of local authorities’ activities* — especially the guidance distinguishing administrative, financial, and democratic supervision; requiring legal clarity on supervisory authorities and remits; and tying supervision to legality, proportionality, competence, and capacity.

### Fiscal distress, insolvency, and service-protecting rescue lanes

- OECD. *Managing rising subnational fiscal risks* — especially the current argument that subnational governments need stronger risk-management capacity while national governments must mitigate risks created by national policy, minimise moral hazard in support, and use legislative powers to avert excessive risk-taking through effective intergovernmental co-operation.
- OECD. *Insolvency Frameworks for Sub-national Governments* and *Fiscal Federalism 2022* (Chapter 7) — especially the treatment of subnational insolvency frameworks as prompt and orderly debt-resolution lanes that can facilitate restructuring and fiscal recovery, complement ex ante budget rules, and help prevent service-threatening financial breakdown.
- IMF. *How to Manage Fiscal Risks from Subnational Governments* — especially the recommendation that central governments pair stronger regulatory frameworks, improved fiscal reporting, and enhanced central oversight with continued attention to public-service delivery and macro-financial spillovers from weak subnational finances.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0490

### Subsidiarity, local administrative capacity, and the right to co-operate

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 4’s rule that public responsibilities should generally be exercised by the authorities closest to the citizen unless the size or nature of the task, or efficiency and economy, require a larger territorial area; Article 6’s insistence on appropriate administrative structures and competent staff; and Article 10’s recognition that local authorities may co-operate and form consortia to carry out tasks of common interest.

### Mandate-finance congruence, local fiscal room, and subnational finance baskets

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially Article 9’s insistence that local authorities have adequate financial resources of their own, that those resources be commensurate with their legal responsibilities, and that at least part derive from local taxes and charges whose rates they can influence within the law.
- OECD. *Place-Based Policies for the Future* — especially the treatment of under- and unfunded mandates as a major constraint on subnational capacity; the insistence that expenditure needs and revenues be kept consistent; and the recommendation that subnational governments rely on a balanced basket of grants, shared and own-source taxes, charges, and property income rather than symbolic decentralization without finance.
- OECD. *Subnational finance and investment* — especially the high-level picture that subnational governments carry major spending responsibilities while relying on mixed revenue structures combining taxes, grants, and user charges, reinforcing the archive’s preference for explicit revenue constitutions rather than grant fog.
- OECD. *Fiscal federalism network* and *OECD Fiscal Decentralisation Database* — especially the current emphasis on tax autonomy as a live comparative dimension of local fiscal accountability, and the dataset’s treatment of recurrent immovable property taxation and government-level responsibility for it.
- OECD. *Intergovernmental fiscal transfers and fiscal equalisation in a time of consolidation* — especially the warning that fiscal equalisation design has to preserve equitable service delivery while maintaining efficiency and local discipline during tighter-budget periods.

- OECD. *Asymmetric decentralisation: Trends, challenges and policy implications* — especially the treatment of asymmetric decentralisation as a differentiated assignment of competencies across subnational governments at the same level, the warning that it should be implemented carefully, and the observation that contemporary asymmetry is increasingly urban as well as regional.
- OECD. *Decentralisation* — especially the treatment of asymmetric decentralisation as a practical reform path, alongside metropolitan governance and regionalisation, rather than as a constitutional anomaly.

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the principles that local powers should be protected by law, that responsibilities and resources should remain commensurate, and that decentralised diversity cannot honestly rest on arbitrary central withdrawal or resource starvation.

### Subsidiarity, local self-government, and allocation close to the citizen

- EUR-Lex / European Union. *The principle of subsidiarity* — especially the criteria asking whether a problem has transnational aspects, whether lower-level action would be insufficient, and whether action at the higher level has clear advantages.
- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government* — especially the treatment of local self-government as the right and ability to manage a substantial share of public affairs; the rule that public responsibilities should generally be exercised by authorities closest to the citizen; and the insistence that local functions require adequate resources and legal protection.

### Metropolitan, urban, and multi-level coordination

- OECD. *The OECD Metropolitan Governance Survey* — especially the finding that metropolitan-governance organizations are common but often weak, which reinforces the archive’s warning against binding metro systems governed without sufficient democratic or operational power.
- OECD. *Five years of the OECD Principles on Urban Policy* — especially the emphasis on coordinating responsibilities and resources across levels of government and the evidence that many national urban policies now include formal multi-level coordination mechanisms.
- OECD / UN-Habitat. *Global State of National Urban Policy 2024* — especially the treatment of national urban policy as a government-led process for rallying and coordinating diverse actors toward a common urban vision, and the archive-relevant argument that national frameworks can help manage urban spillovers without erasing local government.

### Crisis, adaptation, and power-sharing across levels

- UNDP. *Toolkit to Strengthen Multi-Level Governance in Crisis-Affected Settings* — especially the treatment of multi-level governance as requiring alignment across diverse levels, bottom-up participation, and power-sharing incentives rather than a simple center-periphery command chain.

## Sources used for rev0486

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging/lint hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Open government, proactive disclosure, and public-information reuse

- OECD. *Government at a Glance 2025 — Transparency of public information* — especially the treatment of transparency as a core democratic element; the emphasis on proactive disclosure of clear, complete, timely, reliable, and relevant public-sector data and information; and the distinction between regulatory frameworks and actual publication practice for standard datasets.
- OECD Legal Instruments. *Recommendation of the Council on Open Government* — especially the treatment of open government as a culture of governance rooted in transparency, integrity, accountability, and stakeholder participation across levels of government rather than as a portal-only exercise.
- OECD Legal Instruments. *Recommendation of the Council on Enhancing Access to and Sharing of Data* — especially the insistence on findability, accessibility, interoperability, and reusability across organisations while protecting rights and other legitimate interests.

### Reuse rules, high-value datasets, and machine-readable publication

- EUR-Lex / European Union. *Directive (EU) 2019/1024 on open data and the re-use of public sector information* — especially the treatment of open data as information in open format for free re-use; the expectation of open and machine-readable formats with metadata; the practical role of asset lists and portal sites; the free-of-charge default for reuse; and the stronger requirements for dynamic and high-value datasets, including APIs and bulk download where relevant.
- EUR-Lex / European Union. *Commission Implementing Regulation (EU) 2023/138* — especially the legal in-force list of high-value datasets and the arrangements for their publication and re-use, which reinforces the archive’s rule that some categories deserve stronger publication discipline than ordinary decorative disclosure.

### Catalogs, metadata, and portal discipline

- Data.gov. *Open Government* — especially the treatment of an open-data site as statutory catalog infrastructure tied to standardized, machine-readable publication with metadata included in the catalog, plus a repository of tools, best practices, and schema standards rather than a pile of agency-specific improvisations.
- data.europa.eu. *Introducing DCAT-AP for high-value datasets* — especially the treatment of DCAT-AP as a specification for describing public-sector datasets, making them easier to find across government portals, and supporting discoverability and usability across borders and sectors.

## Sources used for rev0485

Current note: `577-public-service-delivery-one-stop-shops-and-assisted-digital-access-by-scope-community-navigation-municipal-front-counters-regional-shared-service-centers-national-service-standards-and-no-government-by-channel-maze.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack and the small archive-tagging/lint hygiene improvement in this revision. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Human-centred, seamless, and cross-channel public services

- OECD Legal Instruments. *Recommendation of the Council on Human-Centred Public Administrative Services* — especially the treatment of public administrative services as needing a whole-of-government strategy; the definitions of accessibility, omni-channel delivery, once-only, and life events; the recommendation to coordinate within and across all levels of government; and the insistence on scalable interoperable digital public infrastructure that enables fair service design and delivery at local and national levels and across borders.
- OECD. *Government at a Glance 2025 — Seamless and accessible public administrative services* — especially the treatment of service standards as a way to create consistent experiences across sectors and institutions regardless of whether people access services online or offline, and the use of life-event approaches to align services with how people actually encounter the state.

### One-stop shops, integration, and the difference between co-location and real service design

- OECD. *One-Stop Shops for Citizens and Business* — especially the treatment of one-stop shops as part of broader administrative simplification; the warning that there is no universal model; and the emphasis on cooperation, governance, communication, interoperability, human capital, and monitoring rather than superficial rebranding.
- World Bank. *Achieving Integrated Government-to-Business Service Delivery: A Planning Guide for Reformers* — especially the distinction between one-roof and single-window models; the treatment of integrated service delivery as requiring information sharing, common identifiers, shared infrastructure, and service redesign; the warning that co-located services can still force repeat submissions and fragmented back-office handling; and the practical role of delegated client-contact organizations.

### Narrow cross-border service rails

- EUR-Lex / European Union. *Regulation (EU) 2018/1724 establishing a single digital gateway* — especially the treatment of a gateway as a single entry point for information, procedures, and assistance/problem-solving services, and the bounded use of once-only evidence exchange for specified cross-border procedures rather than a general world service bureaucracy.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

### Authoritative sources, base registries, and the once-only discipline

- EUR-Lex / European Commission. *European Interoperability Framework – Implementation Strategy* — especially the recommendations to make authoritative sources available to others with access controls, develop interfaces to base registries and authoritative sources, publish the semantic and technical means for reuse, and maintain metadata and data-quality plans for base registries.
- European Commission. *Good Practices on Building Successful Interconnections of Base Registries* — especially the treatment of base registries as trusted and authentic sources of basic information that form the cornerstone of public services; the linkage to the once-only principle; and the insistence that interoperability spans legal, organisational, semantic, and technical layers rather than only transport.
- European Commission. *Europe's Single Digital Gateway / Once-Only Technical System* — especially the treatment of once-only exchange as a bounded mechanism for secure automated cross-border exchange of official evidence and data between competent authorities at the request of citizens and businesses, rather than as a free-for-all circulation model.

### Shared platforms, identifiers, and governed reuse

- OECD. *The OECD Digital Government Policy Framework* — especially the treatment of data as a foundational enabler of digital government; the need to govern data as a strategic asset for planning, delivery, and monitoring; the removal of barriers to sharing and reuse; and the government-as-a-platform idea as an ecosystem of reusable components and data sources rather than a pile of disconnected services.
- UNECE. *Guidelines on the Use of Registers and Administrative Data for Population and Housing Censuses* — especially the insistence that linked register systems depend on unified or otherwise workable identifiers, that unique keys materially affect integrability and linkage quality, and that register-based systems require disciplined metadata and quality work rather than casual record fusion.
- World Bank. *Future Company Registers* — especially the treatment of a company register as part of a country base-register architecture and as the sole trusted source of information about companies, which reinforces the archive’s broader principle that downstream services should not quietly replace authoritative-source ownership.

### Good administration, personal notification, and legal effect

- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2007)7 on good administration* — especially the treatment of good administration standards as applicable across levels of government; the requirement that those concerned by individual decisions be personally notified except in exceptional circumstances; the indication of appeal procedures and time limits; and the rule that, except in urgent cases, decisions should not be operative until appropriately published.
- EUR-Lex / European Union. *Code of Good Administrative Behaviour for Staff of the European Commission in their relations with the public* (2024) — especially the reaffirmation that the right to good administration is a fundamental right and that public administration should be open, efficient, and independent.

### Physical and electronic delivery rails with proof models

- Universal Postal Union. *Universal Postal Convention* — especially the requirements that member countries or designated operators establish, publish, update, and measure delivery standards; that those standards be no less favourable than comparable domestic service; the availability of advice-of-delivery service for registered items; and the existence of secure electronic postal registered mail with proof of sending and proof of delivery.
- EUR-Lex / European Union. *Regulation (EU) No 910/2014 on electronic identification and trust services* — especially the treatment of electronic registered delivery services as evidence-bearing channels, the legal-effect rule for electronic registered delivery, and the stronger presumptions attached to qualified electronic registered delivery services.
- OECD. *Good Practice Principles for Public Service Design and Delivery in the Digital Age* — especially the insistence that public services should be accessible, ethical, equitable, accountable, and transparent rather than designed around administrative convenience alone.

### Cross-border service as a narrow treaty lane

- HCCH. *Convention of 15 November 1965 on the Service Abroad of Judicial and Extrajudicial Documents in Civil or Commercial Matters* — especially the treatment of service abroad as a treaty-bound mutual-assistance lane intended to bring documents to the addressee’s notice in sufficient time, and the explicit rule that the Convention does not apply where the address of the person to be served is not known.

## Sources used for rev0473

Current note: `565-ideal-constitutional-kits-by-scope-neighborhood-voice-municipal-councils-metropolitan-democracy-regional-federal-capacity-national-solidarity-continental-compacts-and-global-narrow-waists.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Ideal constitutional kits by scope: neighborhood voice, municipal councils, metropolitan democracy, regional federal capacity, national solidarity, continental compacts, and global narrow waists

- EUR-Lex / European Union, **The principle of subsidiarity** — core source on the requirement that action be taken at the closest possible level to the citizen unless a higher level is more effective, which the archive translates into a general routing rule for upward delegation.
- Council of Europe, **European Charter of Local Self-Government** — core source on local self-government as political, administrative, and financial independence under law, with local authorities elected through universal suffrage rather than treated as mere branch offices of the center.
- UN-Habitat, **International Guidelines on Decentralization and Access to Basic Services for All** — source on democratic, constitutional, legal, and administrative principles for decentralization, local governance, and service access strong enough to matter beyond symbolism.
- OECD, **OECD Principles on Urban Policy** — core source on targeting the effective scale of policy action, adapting policy to where people live and work, and treating metropolitan governance as a normal response to real functional geographies rather than an institutional afterthought.
- OECD, **What Makes Cities More Productive? Evidence on the Role of Urban Governance from Five OECD Countries** — source on the productivity penalty from fragmented metropolitan governance and on the mitigating value of metropolitan governance bodies where functional urban areas outrun municipal borders.
- OECD, **OECD Guidelines for Citizen Participation Processes** — operational source on meaningful participation design and on the range of methods governments can use, including participatory budgeting, civic monitoring, and representative deliberative processes.
- OECD, **Innovative Citizen Participation and New Democratic Institutions** — source on deliberative processes as complements to representative institutions rather than replacements for them, which supports the archive’s scope-matched participation doctrine.
- UNFCCC, **The Paris Agreement** — source on the global layer as a legally binding but treaty-bound climate architecture organized around common goals, national plans, review cycles, finance, and capacity support rather than a world-state executive.
- WHO, **International health regulations** — core source on the global public-health layer as an early-warning, notification, coordination, and capacity architecture with rights safeguards, not an ordinary domestic health ministry for the world.
- United Nations Treaty Collection, **BBNJ Agreement status** — current source showing the high-seas biodiversity treaty entered into force on 17 January 2026, underscoring that the global layer can be sparse yet real when commons governance is built through narrow treaty machinery.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

- UNODC, **Anti-corruption agencies and commissions** — core source on UNCAC articles 6 and 36, the requirement for mandate, independence, competent staff, and adequate resources, the absence of a one-size-fits-all model, and the distinction between preventive, investigative, prosecutorial, and mixed anti-corruption bodies.
- UNODC, **United Nations Convention against Corruption** — treaty source for preventive anti-corruption body or bodies, specialized authorities, public-sector integrity measures, interagency cooperation, international cooperation, and asset recovery architecture.
- UNODC / UNDP / anti-corruption practitioners, **Jakarta Statement on Principles for Anti-Corruption Agencies** plus UNODC’s **Colombo Commentary** — core source on legal basis, appointment, tenure, budget, staffing, accountability, and operational independence for anti-corruption agencies and commissions.
- OECD Legal Instruments, **Recommendation of the Council on Public Integrity** — core source on risk-based public-integrity systems, coherent strategy, clear institutional responsibilities, and implementation beyond ad hoc ethics policy.
- OECD, **Integrity and anti-corruption strategies** — source on whole-of-government strategic frameworks, central coordination, monitoring, and implementation structures rather than integrity-by-slogan.
- Council of Europe / GRECO, **About GRECO mutual evaluations** — source on the continuing importance of independence, specialization, and means available to national bodies engaged in preventing and fighting corruption.
- UNODC GlobE Network, **About the GlobE Network** — source on the narrow but real international layer for direct anti-corruption law-enforcement cooperation, practitioner contact, secure information exchange, and case advancement across borders.

Current note: `562-civil-registration-vital-statistics-and-legal-identity-institutions-by-scope-local-event-capture-municipal-service-access-regional-backstops-national-trust-frameworks-and-no-government-by-documentary-erasure.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Civil registration, vital statistics, and legal identity institutions by scope: local event capture, municipal service access, regional backstops, national trust frameworks, and no government by documentary erasure

- UN Statistics Division, **UN Legal Identity Agenda** — core source on the holistic approach linking civil registration, vital statistics production, and identity management, and on civil registration as the continuous, permanent, compulsory, and universal recording of vital events.
- UN Statistics Division, **Handbook on Civil Registration and Vital Statistics Systems: Management, Operation and Maintenance, Revision 1** — core source on centralized and decentralized structures, legal frameworks, operational design, and the interaction between civil registration, population registers, and identity management systems.
- UN Legal Identity Agenda, **The role of unique identifiers in civil registration & vital statistics and national identification systems** — core source on when unique identifiers can help, when they create risk, and why inclusive, secure, and interoperable design needs explicit governance rather than magical thinking.
- World Bank ID4D, **Principles on Identification for Sustainable Development** plus **Interoperability** materials — core source on universal coverage from birth to death, robust and responsive identity, open standards, privacy and user rights, and multi-layer interoperability instead of repeated re-enrolment.
- UNICEF Data, **Birth registration** and **The Right Start in Life: 2024 update** — source on birth registration as a prerequisite for legal identity and children’s rights, on the continuing scale of non-registration, and on the importance of lifecycle registration access rather than one-off campaigns.
