These sources informed the new pattern pack. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Districting and boundary commissions by scope: local polling areas, municipal wards, regional mapmaking, national equal-suffrage rules, and no government by incumbent cartography

- Venice Commission, **Report on Constituency Delineation and Seat Allocation** — core source on equal voting power, transparency, impartial boundary authorities, lawful criteria, and the prevention of malapportionment and gerrymandering.
- Venice Commission / Council of Europe, **Code of Good Practice in Electoral Matters** — core source on equal suffrage, limits on permissible deviation, periodic review outside election periods, impartial committee design, and protection against last-minute amendment of fundamental electoral rules including constituency boundaries.
- International IDEA, **Electoral Boundary Delimitation** — operational source on boundary delimitation as a fairness-critical part of representation, on the risks of malapportionment and gerrymandering, and on transparent, publicly testable districting tools and alternatives.
- ACE Electoral Knowledge Network, **Designation of a Boundary Authority** — comparative source on centralized versus regionalized districting authorities, independent commissions, the range of approval models, and why authority design matters as much as cartographic technique.
- OHCHR, **Human Rights and Elections** — source on the requirement that the legal framework and relevant institutions function independently and/or impartially within a wider rights-safe electoral environment.

## Sources used for rev0466

Current note: `558-public-defense-and-legal-aid-by-scope-local-entry-municipal-navigation-regional-provider-capacity-national-independence-and-no-justice-by-unaffordable-counsel.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Public defense and legal aid by scope: local entry, municipal navigation, regional provider capacity, national independence, and no justice by unaffordable counsel

- OHCHR, **Basic Principles on the Role of Lawyers** — core source on access to lawyers and legal services, sufficient public funding and resources, prompt access upon arrest or detention, confidentiality, and protection of lawyers from improper interference.
- UNODC / UN General Assembly, **United Nations Principles and Guidelines on Access to Legal Aid in Criminal Justice Systems** plus UNODC’s **Access to Legal Aid** materials — core source on legal aid as an accessible, effective, sustainable, and credible state obligation, including specialized services for children, women, and other groups with specific needs.
- UNODC Education for Justice, **Models for Governing, Administering and Funding Legal Aid** — core source on national legal-aid bodies or authorities, independence from undue political or judicial interference, sustainable funding, fair and proportional distribution of funds between prosecution and legal-aid agencies, and the variety of public-defender / board / mixed-provider models.
- UNODC Education for Justice, **Access to legal aid for those with specific needs** — source on special measures, dedicated services, age-appropriate and gender-sensitive provision, and the need for specialized training and delivery settings.
- Council of Europe, **Legal aid** and **Legal aid in civil and administrative law: new guidelines** — core source on accessible, effective, sustainable, and reliable civil and administrative legal-aid schemes, one-stop-shop and advice access, quality assurance, geographical distribution, diversity, data collection, and attention to vulnerable groups.
- UNDP / UNODC / Open Society Foundations, **Early Access to Legal Aid in Criminal Justice Processes Handbook** — source on why early access matters operationally and how policy design must begin before preventable coercive or procedural damage occurs.
- UNDP, **Legal Aid Service Provision: A Guide to Programming in Africa** — source on legal aid as effective defense, rights protection, legal empowerment, and state-led systems that can still work with grassroots and non-state providers rather than excluding them.

## Sources used for rev0465

Current note: `557-detention-corrections-and-reentry-by-scope-local-contact-and-release-links-regional-custody-probation-national-rights-floors-and-no-government-by-warehousing.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Detention, corrections, and reentry by scope: local contact and release links, regional custody and probation, national rights floors, and no government by warehousing

- OHCHR / UN General Assembly, **United Nations Standard Minimum Rules for the Treatment of Prisoners (the Nelson Mandela Rules)** — core source on dignity, separation and classification, healthcare, contact, activities, complaints, and limits on disciplinary abuse including prolonged solitary confinement.
- OHCHR, **Optional Protocol to the Convention against Torture (OPCAT)** plus **National Preventive Mechanisms** materials — core source on regular independent visits to places of detention and the requirement that detention not be monitored only by the authorities who run it.
- OHCHR / UN General Assembly, **United Nations Standard Minimum Rules for Non-custodial Measures (the Tokyo Rules)** — core source on non-custodial measures, community involvement, and basic safeguards for alternatives to imprisonment.
- OHCHR / UN General Assembly, **United Nations Rules for the Treatment of Women Prisoners and Non-custodial Measures for Women Offenders (the Bangkok Rules)** — source on gender-responsive detention design and the obligation to develop non-custodial options rather than forcing women through male-designed custody systems by default.
- Council of Europe, **European Prison Rules** — core source on humane prison management, inspection and independent monitoring, contact with the outside world, and the principle that conditions violating human rights cannot be justified by lack of resources.
- Council of Europe, **European Probation Rules** — core source on probation as supervision, guidance, assistance, and social inclusion rather than bare surveillance.
- WHO Europe, **Organizational models of prison health** plus the **WHO Prison Health Framework** — source on equivalence of care, clinical independence, prison-health governance, and the case for stronger connection between detention health services and the ordinary health system.
- UNODC, **The Prevention of Recidivism and the Social Reintegration of Offenders** — source on release planning, continuity of care, social reintegration, and why reentry should be built into the custody system rather than improvised at the gate.

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.


## Sources used for rev0464

Current note: `556-police-services-by-scope-neighborhood-legibility-municipal-or-county-operations-metropolitan-serious-crime-bureaus-national-standards-and-no-police-self-investigation.md`

These sources informed the new pattern pack. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Police services by scope: neighborhood legibility, municipal or county operations, metropolitan serious-crime bureaus, national standards, and no police self-investigation

- OHCHR. *Code of Conduct for Law Enforcement Officials*, especially the insistence that law-enforcement officials serve the community, protect human dignity and human rights, use force only when strictly necessary and proportionate, resist torture and corruption, and report violations rather than hide them.
- OHCHR. *Basic Principles on the Use of Force and Firearms by Law Enforcement Officials*, especially the emphasis on non-violent means first, differentiated tools, strict necessity, proportionality, preservation of life, reporting, review, and recurring training and authorization discipline for firearms.
- Council of Europe. *European Code of Police Ethics* and the Council of Europe materials on police oversight mechanisms, especially the insistence that police remain accountable to the state, citizens, and their representatives, that effective and impartial complaint procedures exist, and that external oversight complements internal command.
- UNODC. *Key mechanisms and actors in police accountability and oversight*, especially the requirement that complaint investigations be reasonably independent, rights-safe for complainants and accused officers, appealable, and systematically analysed for recurring causes of misconduct.
- United Nations Police. *Community-oriented policing* plus OHCHR and OSCE democratic-policing materials, especially the argument that trust, local legibility, civilian organization, and democratic control are design requirements rather than soft public-relations extras.

### Ombuds institutions by scope: frontline complaint entry, municipal redress, regional coordination, national independence, and no government by unanswered grievance

## Sources used for rev0461

These sources informed the new pattern pack. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Supreme audit institutions and external public audit by scope: local books, regional/state audit, national independence, and no government by self-audit

- INTOSAI. *Independence of Supreme Audit Institutions* plus the *Declaration of Lima*, especially the insistence that SAIs can only be objective and effective if they are independent of the audited entity, that their existence and independence should be secured in the constitution or strong law, and that access and protection against outside influence are preconditions rather than luxuries.
- PEFA. *PEFA's Seven Pillars* plus indicator materials for *PI-30 External Audit* and *PI-31 Legislative Scrutiny of Audit Reports*, especially the treatment of independent external review, broad audit coverage, standards-based work, public reporting, and legislative scrutiny with executive follow-up as one connected accountability chain.
- SIGMA / OECD. *Increasing the impact of supreme audit institutions through external engagement*, especially the framing of SAIs as critical public-accountability institutions whose findings matter more when they are published, understandable, and actively used by legislatures, civil society, media, and citizens.
- INTOSAI / ISSAI. *Fundamental Principles of Public-Sector Auditing (ISSAI 100)*, especially the treatment of financial, compliance, and performance audit as distinct but complementary modes of public-sector scrutiny rather than one generic audit blur.

## Sources used for rev0459

These sources informed the new pattern pack. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Public prosecution by scope: local diversion, regional ordinary charging, national integrity cases, and no government by prosecutor

- OHCHR. *Guidelines on the Role of Prosecutors*, especially the requirements that prosecutors act fairly and consistently, refuse or discontinue unfounded cases, give due attention to crimes by public officials, reject evidence obtained through grave human-rights abuse, and consider diversion or discontinuance where law permits.
- Council of Europe. *Recommendation Rec(2000)19 on the role of public prosecution in the criminal justice system*, especially the insistence that prosecutors respect judicial independence, act objectively and fairly in court, and scrutinize the lawfulness and human-rights compliance of police investigations when deciding whether prosecution should commence or continue.
- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2012)11 on the role of public prosecutors outside the criminal justice system*, especially the warning that any non-criminal powers must be clearly defined by law, exercised with legality, objectivity, fairness and impartiality, and not allowed to displace ordinary access to an independent and impartial tribunal.
- Consultative Council of European Prosecutors. *Independence, accountability and ethics of prosecutors*, especially the emphasis on reasoned transparent decisions, proportionality when supervising investigations, protection of defence rights, care for vulnerable people, and respect for judicial independence.
- Venice Commission. *Report on European Standards as regards the Independence of the Judicial System: Part II — the Prosecution Service*, especially the recognition that prosecutorial hierarchies can exist but case-selection and public-interest instructions require legal discipline, transparent reasons, and safeguards against political abuse.
- UNODC. *The Status and Role of Prosecutors*, especially the treatment of prosecutors as gatekeepers of criminal justice whose institutional design must balance independence, accountability, diversion capacity, ethics, and principled relations with police and courts.

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.


## Sources used for rev0458

These sources informed the new pattern pack. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Judiciaries by scope: neighborhood entry, regional trial courts, national apex review, and global rights backstops

- OHCHR. *Basic Principles on the Independence of the Judiciary*, especially the insistence that judicial independence must be guaranteed by the state, that everyone has the right to ordinary courts using established procedures, that exceptional tribunals should not displace ordinary courts, and that adequate resources are part of judicial independence.
- Council of Europe. *Judicial independence and impartiality*, especially the continuing Plan of Action framing judicial independence and impartiality as preconditions of the rule of law rather than optional institutional refinements.
- CEPEJ / Council of Europe. *Quality of justice* and CEPEJ materials, especially the treatment of court quality, time management, user expectations, workload, and court-organisation reform as matters of public-service design as well as legal doctrine.
- International IDEA. *Judicial Appointments* plus *Judicial Tenure, Removal, Immunity and Accountability*, especially the treatment of appointment models, tenure, removal, discipline, and accountability as design problems that must balance independence with public legitimacy and control of abuse.
- International IDEA. *Courts in Federal Countries* plus *The Fundamentals of Constitutional Courts*, especially the distinction between ordinary courts and constitutional review, and the design questions raised when federal or competence disputes need an apex judicial settlement.
- World Bank. *JUPITER / Data and Analytics*, especially the framing of judicial effectiveness around access to justice, efficiency, and quality rather than prestige or doctrinal elegance alone.

## Sources used for rev0457

These sources informed the new pattern pack. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Civil service and public workforce: merit, neutrality, capability, fair pay, and no government by patronage

- OECD. *Recommendation of the Council on Public Service Leadership and Capability*, especially the insistence that a professional, capable, and responsive public service is fundamental to trust, that public-service leaders need the conditions to provide impartial evidence-informed advice, and that responsibility for these conditions is shared across levels and institutions.
- SIGMA / OECD / European Union. *The Principles of Public Administration* (2023 edition), especially the insistence that public servants act with professionalism, integrity, and neutrality; are recruited and promoted on merit and equal opportunity; may reject unlawful instructions; and should be protected against wrongful dismissal and undue influence.
- World Bank. *Public Workforce Performance and Prosperity* (2026), especially the framing of the world’s roughly 400 million public sector workers as a central determinant of state capability, service delivery, fiscal sustainability, and citizen trust.
- OECD. *Workforce Insights from Central Governments* chapter on pay, working conditions, and remote working arrangements, especially the argument that fair pay, supportive working conditions, and workable flexibility are basic conditions for attracting, motivating, and retaining talent in the public sector.
- International Labour Organization. *Collective bargaining and labour relations* materials, especially the treatment of collective bargaining as a fundamental right and a basis for sound labour relations, including in public service contexts.

## Sources used for rev0456

These sources informed the new pattern pack. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Legislatures by scope: neighborhood assemblies, municipal councils, metro chambers, federal shared rule, and no government by decree

- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government*, especially the insistence that local self-government should be exercised by freely elected councils or assemblies, with real responsibilities, legal foundations, and consultation rights rather than decorative decentralization.
- OECD. *Quality Budget Institutions* (especially the section on effective budget oversight), especially the claim that parliamentary engagement across the budget cycle is fundamental to democratic governance and trust.
- International IDEA. *Bicameralism* plus *The Role of Parliament in Delegated Legislation*, especially the view that second chambers should justify themselves through shared-rule or review functions and that delegated rulemaking must remain bounded by parliamentary scrutiny rather than hollowing out the legislative role.
- OSCE/ODIHR. *Guidelines on Democratic Lawmaking for Better Laws*, especially the insistence that democratic lawmaking should be open, transparent, participatory, rights-compliant, and organized as a cycle rather than a backstage executive act.
- Commonwealth Parliamentary Association. *CPA Benchmarks for Democratic Legislatures* materials, especially the emphasis on committees, oversight and accountability, representation and engagement, parliamentary service, and standards and ethics as parts of a working legislature rather than decorative extras.

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.


## Sources used for rev0455

These sources informed the new pattern pack. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Budgeting, treasury, and public accounts: credible ceilings, legislative authority, one treasury, and no government by fund fog

- OECD. *Recommendation of the Council on Budgetary Governance* plus *Quality Budget Institutions*, especially the insistence on clear fiscal objectives, medium-term and top-down budgeting, budget transparency, effective parliamentary oversight, and a coherent set of budget institutions rather than disconnected reforms.
- OECD. *Best Practices for Parliaments in Budgeting*, especially the argument that legislatures should review fiscal frameworks and budget totals with enough time, committee scrutiny, hearing powers, and analytic support to make budget approval real rather than ceremonial.
- IMF. *Fiscal Transparency Code* / *Fiscal Transparency Evaluation* materials, especially the treatment of fiscal reporting, fiscal forecasting and budgeting, and fiscal risk analysis and management as one connected transparency standard rather than separate technical silos.
- IMF. *Treasury Single Account: Concept, Design and Implementation Issues*, especially the argument that fragmented government banking arrangements should give way to treasury-controlled cash consolidation, better visibility, and lower borrowing costs.
- PEFA. Framework and summary materials on the seven pillars of public financial management, especially budget reliability, predictability and control in budget execution, accounting and reporting, and external scrutiny and audit.

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

### Public enterprises and state-owned enterprises: explicit rationales, separated roles, hard budget constraints, and open books

- OECD. *Corporate governance of state-owned enterprises* and *Ownership and Governance of State-Owned Enterprises 2024*, especially the insistence on professional and active state ownership, published ownership policies, recurrent review of rationales for ownership, transparency, and accountability to the public.
- OECD. *Competitive Neutrality Toolkit*, especially the treatment of competitive neutrality, state support, and compensation for public-service obligations as matters that require explicit design rather than patriotic fog.

- World Bank. *Public Reporting on State-Owned Enterprises*, especially the argument that aggregate SOE reporting is a leading practice for ownership oversight, fiscal-risk management, and public accountability across the portfolio rather than enterprise-by-enterprise opacity.

### Delegated public functions: contractors, concessions, nonprofits, and platforms cannot become constitutional escape hatches

- OECD. *Recommendation of the Council on Principles for Public Governance of Public-Private Partnerships*, especially the insistence that PPPs need value-for-money discipline, a sound institutional framework, and honest alignment of public-sector responsibilities rather than off-book project theatre.
- World Bank. *PPP Online Reference Guide* plus *Public Financial Management Frameworks for PPPs*, especially the treatment of contingent liabilities, guarantees, disclosure, affordability, and long-tail fiscal risk as central governance questions rather than technical annexes.

## Sources used for rev0451

These sources informed the new pattern pack. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Special-purpose authorities and arm’s-length bodies: narrow remits, hard charters, open books, and sunset review

- OECD. *Organisation of public administration: agency governance, autonomy and accountability*, especially the insistence that form should follow function, that autonomy must be balanced by accountability, and that uncontrolled agencification creates governance vacuums rather than professional administration.
- OECD. *OECD Guidelines on Corporate Governance of State-Owned Enterprises 2024*, especially the insistence that public ownership should rest on disclosed rationales, recurrent review, and accountability to the general public rather than opaque permanent exception.
- OECD. *OECD Principles on Urban Policy* and the 2025 *Five years of the OECD Principles on Urban Policy* follow-up, especially the argument for functional urban-scale governance, metropolitan and regional co-operation, and scale-matched public-service design rather than administrative fragmentation.
- OECD. *Approaches to Metropolitan Area Governance*, especially the observation that metropolitan governance can take several legal forms, including full governments and authorities, but that form, powers, legal status, and funding all matter for whether the institution is merely consultative or actually governing.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0450

These sources informed the new pattern pack. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Intergovernmental machinery: lawful councils, compacts, formula finance, and no government by summit

- OECD. *Making Decentralisation Work*, especially the insistence that decentralisation depends on clear assignments, adequate capacities, and multi-level coordination rather than one-off devolution theatre.
- OECD. *Navigating conflict and fostering co-operation in fiscal federalism* (2024), especially the treatment of institutional forums, mediation, and conflict-resolution machinery as part of the constitutional operating system of fiscal federalism.
- Council of Europe. *The consultation of local authorities by higher levels of government*, especially the principle that consultation should rest on a clear legal framework and happen early enough to shape decisions rather than merely react to them.
- International IDEA. *Federalism* and *Federal Systems, Intergovernmental Relations and Federated Regions*, especially the framing of federal orders as combinations of self-rule and shared rule that need designed relations among levels rather than bare power lists.

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0449

These sources informed the new pattern pack. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Government by scope: micro-local stewardship, municipal professionalism, metropolitan democracy, regional capacity, national guarantees, and global narrow waists

- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2023)5 on the principles of good democratic governance*, especially the insistence that democratic governance should be lawful, rights-respecting, accountable, participatory, and institutionally competent across levels rather than reduced to one electoral ritual.
- Council of Europe. *European Charter of Local Self-Government*, especially the principle that local authorities need real responsibilities, legal protection, and resources proportionate to their functions rather than decorative decentralization.
- OECD. *OECD Principles on Urban Policy* and *Five years of the OECD Principles on Urban Policy*, especially the focus on effective scales of policy action, multi-level coordination, and functional urban governance rather than city romanticism or municipal fragmentation.
- OECD. *Navigating conflict and fostering co-operation in fiscal federalism* (2024), especially the treatment of intergovernmental institutions, dispute handling, and fiscal coordination as constitutional machinery rather than after-the-fact politics.
- International IDEA. *Federalism* and related division-of-powers materials, especially the framing of federal or regionalized systems as combinations of self-rule and shared rule rather than mere administrative delegation.
- United Nations. *UN Charter* plus World Health Organization materials on the *International Health Regulations*, especially the idea that the global layer is strongest where it is treaty-based, function-specific, and openly limited rather than pretending to be a full world sovereign.

The archive document in this revision is not a summary of the sources. It is a synthetic design note derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0448

These sources informed the new pattern packs. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Money and payments by scope: local cash access, municipal acceptance, national public money, and global interoperability

- World Bank Group. *Financial Infrastructure*, especially the treatment of payment and credit infrastructure as public-interest systems requiring law, regulation, supervision, inclusion, and cross-border connectivity rather than mere fintech convenience.
- World Bank Group. *Government-to-person (G2Px)*, especially the insistence that digitized public payments should improve inclusion, empowerment, and public-service delivery rather than just replacing cash with apps.
- Bank for International Settlements / CPMI. *Cross-border payments programme*, especially the concentration on interoperability, legal and supervisory frameworks, and cross-border data exchange rather than one universal platform fantasy.
- European Central Bank. *Access to and acceptance of cash*, especially the principle that cash access and effective acceptance remain part of practical payment citizenship.
- World Bank Group. *The Global Findex Database 2025*, especially the continued use of account, savings, borrowing, and digital-payments evidence as a way to test whether payment design is actually inclusive.

### Banking, credit, and financial stability by scope: local relationship lending, regional development finance, national prudential oversight, and global backstops

- Basel Committee on Banking Supervision. *Core Principles for effective banking supervision* (2024 consolidated version), especially the framing of prudential supervision as a national minimum architecture rather than an optional technical extra.
- Financial Stability Board. *Key Attributes of Effective Resolution Regimes for Financial Institutions* (revised version 2024), especially the insistence that authorities must be able to resolve failing institutions in an orderly way while maintaining continuity of vital functions and avoiding taxpayer loss from solvency support.
- World Bank Group. *Financial Infrastructure*, especially the treatment of payment, credit, and supervisory infrastructure as foundational to inclusion and stability.
- International Monetary Fund. *Financial Access Survey (FAS) 2025 Annual Report*, especially the continuing use of supply-side access metrics to reveal whether finance is widening real access or merely expanding digital rhetoric.
- World Bank Group. *The Global Findex Database 2025*, especially the demand-side view of how adults actually save, borrow, make payments, and manage risk.

### Trade, customs, and border procedures by scope: local port interfaces, regional gateways, national trade policy, and global rules

- World Trade Organization. *Agreement on Trade Facilitation*, especially the treatment of release and clearance of goods, publication duties, broker rules, common procedures, and the single-window concept as anti-friction governance rather than only customs modernization.
- World Customs Organization. *WCO Data Model*, especially the role of harmonized data definitions and messages in making single-window systems and cross-border interoperability possible.
- UN Trade and Development (UNCTAD). *Trade facilitation*, especially the emphasis on technical assistance, digitalization, capacity building, and resilience across trade systems rather than treaty abstraction alone.
- World Customs Organization. *SAFE Framework of Standards 2025* announcement and materials, especially the three-pillar framing that balances Customs-to-Customs, Customs-to-Business, and Customs-to-other-Government-Agencies cooperation.
- World Bank Group. *International LPI*, especially the continuing use of customs performance, infrastructure quality, logistics competence, and timeliness as real-world tests of whether border governance actually works.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive documents in this revision are not summaries of the sources. They are synthetic design notes derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0447

These sources informed the new pattern packs. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Transport and logistics by scope: local streets, metro transit, regional corridors, national networks, and global interoperability

- OECD/ITF. *ITF Transport Outlook 2023*, especially the treatment of passenger and freight movement across all modes, liveability, infrastructure decisions, and regional policy differences.
- OECD/ITF. *Urban Logistics Hubs* and *The Future of Public Transport Funding*, especially the insistence that urban freight and public transport funding are metropolitan and territorial-governance problems rather than single-municipality or mode-silo problems.
- ICAO. *Air Transport Policy and Regulation*, especially the treatment of connectivity, consumer protection, competition, essential services, and global regulatory cooperation in air transport.
- OECD. *Transport* topic page, especially the description of the ITF as the only global body covering all transport modes.
- IMO. *List of IMO Conventions*, especially the existence of narrow but real global rulebooks for maritime safety, pollution, ballast water, search and rescue, and ship-port interfaces.

### Oceans, coasts, and fisheries by scope: local stewardship, regional seascapes, national jurisdiction, and global high-seas guardrails

- United Nations. *Oceans and the Law of the Sea* and the UNCLOS overview materials, especially the treatment of the law of the sea as the overarching legal framework for activities in oceans and seas.
- United Nations. *BBNJ Agreement* materials, especially the current post-entry-into-force framework for biodiversity beyond national jurisdiction.
- FAO. *Small-scale fisheries governance* and the *SSF Guidelines*, especially the insistence on participatory governance, rights, sustainable resource management, and inclusion of fishers and fishworkers.
- IMO. *Pollution Prevention* and *MARPOL* materials, especially the treatment of ship-source pollution as a standing international-governance question rather than a purely domestic port problem.
- CBD. *Target 3: Conserve 30% of Land, Waters and Seas*, especially the emphasis on effectively conserved and equitably governed coastal and marine areas integrated into wider seascapes and the ocean.

### Outer space and orbital commons by scope: local hosting, regional spaceports, national authorization, and global sustainability guardrails

- UNOOSA. *The Outer Space Treaty* and *Space Law Treaties and Principles*, especially the non-appropriation and peaceful-use baseline for outer space.
- UNOOSA. *Long-term Sustainability of Outer Space Activities* materials and the *LTS Information Repository*, especially the focus on policy and regulatory frameworks, safety of operations, information-sharing, and equitable long-term access.
- UNOOSA. *The “Space2030” Agenda*, especially the treatment of space economy, society, accessibility, and diplomacy as public-governance rather than prestige-only questions.
- UNOOSA. *Working Group on the “Space2030” Agenda* materials, especially the continuing governance role of COPUOS and UNOOSA in coordinating shared space-policy architecture.
- United Nations. *Treaties and Principles on Outer Space* materials, especially the continuing treaty floor on registration, liability, rescue, and peaceful use.

## Sources used for rev0446

These sources informed the new pattern packs. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Water and sanitation by scope: local access, municipal stormwater, basin governance, national rights, and transboundary cooperation

- OECD. *The OECD Principles on Water Governance and implementation strategy* plus the 2024 indicator-framework materials, especially the insistence that water governance should be effective, efficient, and inclusive across levels of government and across uses rather than left to one ministry or one utility silo.
- UNECE. *The Water Convention and the Protocol on Water and Health*, especially the treatment of transboundary waters as a legal cooperation problem requiring agreements and joint bodies rather than ad hoc upstream power.
- OHCHR. *OHCHR and the rights to water and sanitation* plus the Special Rapporteur and handbook materials, especially the framing of drinking water and sanitation as human rights derived from dignity and adequate living standards rather than discretionary services.
- World Health Organization. *Water, sanitation and hygiene (WASH)* and related water-sanitation-health materials, especially the insistence that safe WASH is foundational to health, schooling, and resilience.
- UNDRR. *Water risks and resilience*, especially the framing of integrated and risk-informed water governance as a cross-sector resilience task rather than a narrow infrastructure problem.

### Waste and materials by scope: local collection, regional recovery, national producer responsibility, and global hazard controls

- OECD. *Circular economy in cities and regions*, especially the treatment of cities and regions as core actors in circular transitions because they govern land use, waste, mobility, procurement, and local economic development.
- OECD. *Extended producer responsibility and economic instruments* plus the 2024 EPR update, especially the insistence that responsibility for products should shift upstream to producers rather than remaining a permanent municipal cleanup obligation.
- UNEP. *Global Waste Management Outlook 2024* plus current waste-programme materials, especially the argument that waste governance must move upstream toward prevention, circularity, and inclusive system change rather than only downstream disposal.
- Basel Convention Secretariat. *Basel Convention Overview*, *Household Waste Partnership*, and *E-waste Overview*, especially the treatment of hazardous, household, plastic, and electronic waste as governance problems requiring environmentally sound management and controlled transboundary movement.

### Extractive resources by scope: local consent, regional cumulative impacts, national public rents, and global just-transition guardrails

- United Nations. *The UN Secretary-General's Initiative on Critical Energy Transition Minerals* and the Panel materials, especially the effort to articulate guiding principles so clean-energy demand does not reproduce injustice, corruption, and unequal value capture.
- UNEP. *Strengthening laws and governance* plus the wider extractives programme, especially the insistence that minerals and metals governance must integrate environmental sustainability, circularity, and stronger public law rather than pure concession acceleration.
- Extractive Industries Transparency Initiative. *EITI Standard 2023* and the 2024 progress materials, especially the use of disclosure, beneficial ownership, contract transparency, and multi-stakeholder oversight to reduce extractive corruption and opacity.
- UNCTAD. *Critical minerals* and the 2026 industrial-policy report, especially the warning that critical-mineral demand can reinforce commodity dependence unless countries capture more value and govern transition minerals strategically.
- World Bank Group. *Metals and Minerals*, especially the emphasis on turning mineral wealth into jobs, growth, and resilient supply chains through stronger institutions rather than raw extraction alone.

The archive documents in this revision are not summaries of the sources. They are synthetic design notes derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0445

These sources informed the new pattern packs. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Indigenous peoples and self-government by scope: community authority, municipal consent, regional co-governance, national plurinational constitutionalism, and global rights

- United Nations / OHCHR. *United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples*, *About Indigenous Peoples and human rights*, and the Expert Mechanism materials on self-determination, especially the insistence that Indigenous peoples have the right to self-determination and to determine their own identity, institutions, and political status rather than existing only as stakeholders inside dominant-state law.
- OHCHR. *Consultation and free, prior and informed consent (FPIC)*, especially the clarification that consent must be free, prior, informed, and sought through Indigenous peoples’ own representative institutions before legislative, administrative, extractive, relocation, or hazardous decisions that affect them.
- ILO. *Indigenous and Tribal Peoples Convention, 1989 (No. 169)* and related implementation materials, especially the insistence on consultation, institutions, land, and participation as binding governance commitments rather than discretionary reconciliation language.

### Language and linguistic pluralism by scope: local use, municipal service access, regional language ecologies, national rights, and global support

- UNESCO. *Multilingualism and Linguistic diversity* plus *What you need to know about multilingual education*, especially the insistence that multilingualism is central to inclusion, heritage, Indigenous empowerment, and learning in languages people understand rather than a symbolic afterthought.
- Council of Europe. *European Charter for Regional or Minority Languages* and *About the Charter*, especially the treatment of regional and minority languages as part of democratic cultural diversity and as languages to be protected and promoted across public life.
- OHCHR. *Language Rights of Linguistic Minorities: A Practical Guide for Implementation* plus the *Declaration on the Rights of Persons Belonging to National or Ethnic, Religious and Linguistic Minorities*, especially the balance between official-language settlements and the obligations to respect minority-language use, participation, and education.
- UNESCO. *Indigenous Languages Decade (2022-2032)*, especially the framing of preservation, revitalization, digital inclusion, and education support as a global but non-centralizing task.

### Rural, remote, and peripheral places by scope: local agency, intermunicipal service backbones, regional catchments, national territorial solidarity, and global learning

- OECD. *Implementation Toolkit: OECD Recommendation on Regional Development Policy*, especially the pillars on territorial scale, multi-level governance, capacities, resources, integrity, and performance as a practical framework for place-sensitive regional policy.
- OECD. *Rural Well-being: Geography of Opportunities* plus the OECD rural-development topic page, especially the argument that rural places are diverse, connected to cities, marked by distance penalties and demographic pressures, and in need of governance mechanisms centred on citizen well-being rather than crude economic residualism.
- European Commission. *Territorial cohesion* and *Urban-rural linkages*, especially the insistence on balanced territorial development, attention to areas facing severe natural or demographic handicaps, and stronger focus on integrated territorial strategies that reflect functional geographies rather than one administrative boundary.

## Sources used for rev0444

These sources informed the new pattern packs. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Older persons and ageing by scope: local belonging, municipal age-friendly design, regional supports, national income security, and global rights

- OHCHR. *Older persons*, *OHCHR and older persons*, and the *United Nations Principles for Older Persons*, especially the insistence that older persons are full rights-holders entitled to independence, participation, care, self-fulfilment, and dignity rather than passive objects of protection.
- World Health Organization. *Ageing and health*, *WHO’s work on the UN Decade of Healthy Ageing*, *Integrated care for older people (ICOPE)*, and the age-friendly-cities materials, especially the treatment of healthy ageing as a whole-of-society design question rather than only a hospital or pension problem.
- OECD. *Ageing and long-term care* and relevant 2025 long-term-care indicators, especially the evidence that access, workforce design, and care settings are systemic governance questions rather than private household burdens.

### End-of-life and death care by scope: local accompaniment, municipal death administration, regional palliative capacity, national rights, and global pain relief

- World Health Organization. *Palliative care* topic materials plus the integrated-clinical-services palliative-care page, especially the insistence that palliative care is a crucial part of integrated, people-centred health services and an ethical duty rather than an optional charity service.
- Council of Europe. *Guide on the decision-making process regarding medical treatment in end-of-life situations* materials, especially the focus on dignity, consent, decision process, and rights-bearing medical judgment rather than family improvisation or purely technical hospital discretion.
- OHCHR. *About the right to health and human rights* plus the UN experts’ statements on pain relief and end-of-life suffering, especially the argument that avoidable suffering and lack of palliative access are human-rights failures rather than unfortunate inevitabilities.

### Freedom of conscience and religion by scope: local coexistence, municipal neutrality, regional fairness, national rights, and global protection

- OHCHR. *Freedom of religion*, *Special Rapporteur on freedom of religion or belief*, and the *International standards* materials, especially the guarantee of freedom of thought, conscience, religion, and belief including the rights to change or refuse religion.
- OSCE/ODIHR and Venice Commission. *Guidelines for Review of Legislation Pertaining to Religion or Belief*, especially the insistence on legal personality, equal treatment, and scrutiny of laws that burden minority or dissenting communities.
- OHCHR. *The Rabat Plan of Action* and related freedom-of-expression materials, especially the distinction between lawful criticism of religion and incitement to discrimination, hostility, or violence.
- Council of Europe. *Strasbourg Principles for inter-religious dialogue within the Council of Europe* and thematic freedom-of-religion materials, especially the reminder that democratic pluralism requires both state neutrality and equal protection for minorities.

## Sources used for rev0441

These sources informed the new pattern packs. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Public revenue and taxation by scope: local own-source taxes, metropolitan base-sharing, national progressivity, and global anti-evasion floors

- OECD. *Fiscal Federalism 2022*, especially the treatment of tax and spending assignment, subnational tax autonomy, and the trade-offs of decentralisation in intergovernmental fiscal relations.
- OECD. *Synthesising good practices in fiscal federalism*, especially the distinction between less mobile subnational tax bases and tax instruments better suited to national level.
- OECD. *Global Minimum Tax* and the *Global Anti-Base Erosion Model Rules (Pillar Two)* materials, especially the argument that coordinated minimum-tax rules reduce profit shifting and put a floor under destructive tax competition.

### Public investment and procurement by scope: local maintenance, metro systems, national capital discipline, and narrow global standards

- OECD. *Recommendation of the Council on Effective Public Investment Across Levels of Government* and the *Effective Public Investment Toolkit*, especially the insistence that public investment is a shared responsibility across levels of government and should be coordinated at the appropriate territorial scale.
- OECD. *Recommendation of the Council on Public Procurement* and *Implementing the OECD Recommendation on Public Procurement in OECD and partner countries*, especially the treatment of procurement as a whole-cycle system requiring transparency, integrity, access, efficiency, capability, and accountability.
- OECD. *Strategic public procurement* and *Recommendation on Governance of Infrastructure*, especially the case for professionalised procurement, lifecycle discipline, infrastructure governance, and the link between procurement quality, resilience, and broader public value.

### Innovation and industrial policy by scope: local entrepreneurship, regional clusters, national missions, and open global coordination

- OECD. *Broad-based Innovation Policy for All Regions and Cities*, especially the argument that innovation policy must broaden beyond a few frontier science actors and adapt to the capacity of diverse regional innovation systems.
- OECD. *Place-based industrial policy*, the *OECD Recommendation on Regional Development Policy* toolkit, and the OECD industrial-policy materials, especially the case for tailoring industrial interventions to different places and coordinating them across levels of government.
- UNIDO. *Industrial Policy Advice and Capacity Development*, especially the framing of industrial policy as a governed toolkit for identifying sector strengths, weaknesses, and strategic interventions rather than an improvisational subsidy race.
- WTO. *Agreement on Subsidies and Countervailing Measures* and the WTO subsidies overview, especially the role of transparency and multilateral disciplines in constraining subsidy escalation without pretending trade law should replace domestic development strategy.

### Climate by scope: local adaptation, metropolitan form, regional resilience, national transition, and global carbon limits

### Education and learning by scope: local belonging, metropolitan desegregation, regional systems, national rights, and global knowledge

- UNESCO. *Right to education*, especially the treatment of education as a legal right with state obligations, monitoring, and enforcement rather than a discretionary service.
- OECD. *Education Governance in Action*, especially the insistence that modern education governance in complex systems depends on accountability, capacity building, strategic thinking, and flexible steering rather than one command line.
- UNESCO-IIEP. *Cities and Education 2030: Local challenges, global imperatives*, especially the reminder that cities face distinct governance challenges in implementing education policy and therefore need a real institutional role rather than being treated as mere delivery outposts.
- UNESCO. *Inclusion in education*, especially the treatment of access, equity, disability inclusion, and belonging as constitutive design questions rather than remedial exceptions.

### Work and income security by scope: local access, regional labour markets, national social insurance, and transnational portability

- ILO. *Labour administration*, especially the definition of labour administration as public administration in the field of national labour policy and the insistence that states maintain viable systems to formulate and implement that policy.
- ILO. *Public employment services and active labour market policies for transitions*, especially the case for public employment services as key labour-market institutions that help workers and firms navigate disruption and transition.
- ILO. *Social Protection Floors Recommendation, 2012 (No. 202)*, especially the treatment of nationally defined floors of basic social-security guarantees as a state responsibility rather than household luck.
- ILO. *Social dialogue and tripartism*, especially the claim that labour governance requires structured voice for workers and employers and that social dialogue is both instrumentally useful and intrinsically democratic.

### Networked utilities by scope: local access, metropolitan mobility, basin-and-grid coordination, national universal service, and cross-border interoperability

- OECD. *The OECD Principles on Water Governance and implementation strategy*, especially the treatment of water governance as a multi-level system spanning municipal, regional, and basin scales with explicit local-implementation guidance.
- IEA. *Electricity Grids and Secure Energy Transitions*, especially the warning that grids can become a major bottleneck for clean transitions and electricity security if governance and investment do not keep pace.
- OECD. *Policy Directions for Establishing a Metropolitan Transport Authority for Korea's Capital Region*, especially the practical assignment of strategic planning, investment, data management, public transport services, and multimodal transfer-centre management to a metropolitan authority where the urban system requires it.
- ITU. *Global Connectivity Report 2025*, especially the framing of universal and meaningful connectivity as a policy imperative spanning quality, availability, affordability, devices, skills, and security rather than raw coverage alone.

The archive documents in this revision are not summaries of the sources. They are synthetic design notes derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.


## Sources used for rev0437

These sources informed the new pattern packs. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Land and housing by scope: secure tenure, municipal place-making, metro housing markets, regional landscapes, and national fairness

- OECD. *Urban housing*, especially the insistence that improving access to affordable, quality, and sustainable housing in cities requires coordination by the relevant actors at the right scale, alongside supply, land-use, and renovation tools.
- OHCHR. *OHCHR and the right to adequate housing* and the housing rights materials of the Special Rapporteur, especially the treatment of adequate housing as a right to live somewhere in security, peace, and dignity rather than a mere commodity.
- OHCHR. *A/HRC/49/48: Spatial segregation and the right to adequate housing*, especially the analysis of discriminatory zoning, forced eviction, resettlement, and territorial planning as drivers of segregation that require mapping, planning, and policy correction.
- UN-Habitat. *Steering the Metropolis: Metropolitan Governance for Sustainable Urban Development* and *Secure Land Rights for All*, especially the case for metropolitan coordination plus secure and plural land-rights arrangements rather than parcel-level fragmentation alone.

### Health by scope: primary care, regional capacity, national risk pools, and global alerts

- World Health Organization. *Primary health care*, especially the definition of PHC as a whole-of-society approach combining integrated services, multisectoral action, and empowered communities as close as feasible to everyday life.
- World Health Organization. *Health Systems Governance* and *Universal health coverage (UHC)*, especially the treatment of governance, equity, quality, and affordable access as system-wide public responsibilities rather than local accidents.
- World Health Organization. *International Health Regulations*, especially the layered obligations on states to build surveillance and response capacity, notify WHO of international public-health risks, and coordinate internationally while protecting individual rights.

### Membership and movement by scope: local inclusion, national citizenship, regional mobility, and global protection

- OHCHR. *International standards governing migration policy*, especially the reminder that all migrants, regardless of status, are entitled to the same human rights as everyone else and must have participation, accountability, and remedy.
- United Nations. *Global Compact for Migration*, especially the framing of migration governance as a cooperative task across local, national, regional, and global levels rather than a purely national border monopoly.
- IOM. *Migration Governance Indicators*, especially the adaptation of migration-governance assessment to the local level so municipal and regional roles are treated as governance domains rather than implementation afterthoughts.
- UNHCR. *Ending statelessness* and *About statelessness*, especially the treatment of nationality as protection against rightlessness and of statelessness prevention and reduction as an active public duty.

The archive documents in this revision are not summaries of the sources. They are synthetic design notes derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.


## Sources used for rev0436

These sources informed the new pattern packs. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Public safety by scope: prevention first, local legitimacy, metro serious-crime coordination, and rights-locked cooperation

- OHCHR. *Code of Conduct for Law Enforcement Officials*, especially the treatment of law enforcement as community service bound to human dignity, confidentiality discipline, and strictly necessary force.
- OHCHR. *Basic Principles on the Use of Force and Firearms by Law Enforcement Officials*, especially the insistence on non-violent means first, differentiated force options, training, reporting, and strict limits on firearms use.
- Council of Europe. *Police oversight mechanisms in the Council of Europe member states*, especially the reminder that police must be accountable to the state, citizens, and their representatives through efficient external control.
- UNODC. *Crime Prevention* and the *Handbook on the Crime Prevention Guidelines*, especially the case for evidence-based prevention, multisector collaboration, local adaptation, and movement away from purely punitive public-safety thinking.
- INTERPOL. *Legal documents*, *Data protection*, and *Compliance and review*, especially the constitutional commitments to human rights, political neutrality, data-protection rules, and review of notices against Articles 2 and 3.

### Intelligence by scope: no local secret police, judicial authorization, minimization, and multi-key oversight

- Venice Commission. *Report on the Democratic oversight of the Security Services* and the 2015 update, especially the insistence on statutory basis, democratic control, and strong oversight of security services.
- Venice Commission. *Report on the Democratic Oversight of Signals Intelligence Agencies*, especially the need for strong legal framing and oversight where technical collection powers are unusually intrusive.
- Parliamentary Assembly of the Council of Europe. *Control of internal security services in council of Europe member states*, especially the warnings against letting internal security services run ordinary criminal investigations, arrest, or detention, and the preference for prior judicial authorization of intrusive measures.
- OHCHR. *Special Rapporteur on the right to privacy* and *Digital space and human rights*, especially the emphasis on reviewing interception and personal-data practices, identifying unjustified intrusions, and bringing surveillance under the rule of law.

### Defense by scope: civilian control, civil defense, no subnational war machines, and narrow-waist collective security

- OSCE. *Code of Conduct on Politico-Military Aspects of Security*, especially the requirement of democratic political control of military, paramilitary, and internal security forces together with respect for human and civil rights.
- United Nations. *UN Charter* and Chapter VII materials, especially the narrow global role of collective-security action in relation to threats to peace, breaches of the peace, and acts of aggression.
- OSCE. *Reform and co-operation in the security sector* materials, especially the connection between democratic civilian control, rule of law, and security-sector legitimacy.

The archive documents in this revision are not summaries of the sources. They are synthetic design notes derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

## Sources used for rev0435

These sources informed the new pattern packs. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Public integrity by scope: local disclosure, conflict checks, lobbying transparency, asset declarations, and anti-capture routing

- OECD. *Recommendation of the Council on Public Integrity*, especially the treatment of integrity as a whole public system with clear responsibilities, risk management, accountability, and a culture of integrity rather than a narrow criminal-law afterthought.
- OECD. *Recommendation on Transparency and Integrity in Lobbying and Influence*, especially the insistence that lobbying can contribute to better policymaking only when influence activity is transparent, fairly accessible, and governed against opaque or monopolized influence.
- UNODC. *UNCAC Chapter II: Prevention*, especially the treatment of preventive anti-corruption policy, dedicated bodies, public-sector integrity, procurement discipline, and public trust as prior governance architecture rather than cleanup after scandal.

- Venice Commission and OSCE/ODIHR. *Preventing and responding to the misuse of administrative resources during electoral processes*, especially the insistence that incumbency must not be allowed to convert state resources into campaign advantage.

### Public sphere by scope: community media, public-service independence, ownership transparency, and civic information commons

- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2018)1 on media pluralism and transparency of media ownership*, especially the treatment of pluralism and ownership transparency as structural democratic safeguards rather than optional market niceties.
- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2012)1 on public service media governance*, especially the insistence that public service media need independence, accountable governance, and protection against political interference.
- Council of Europe. *Recommendation CM/Rec(2022)4 on promoting a favourable environment for quality journalism in the digital age*, especially the recognition that quality journalism requires conditions of sustainability, safety, and democratic relevance.
- European Union. *European Media Freedom Act*, especially the effort to protect media freedom, pluralism, and transparency in an increasingly digital and cross-border information market.
- UNESCO. *Community Media*, especially the argument that independent, community-owned media are necessary for a plural media ecology and should be clearly recognized in law rather than squeezed between state and commercial models.

## Sources used for rev0432

## Sources used for rev0431

These sources informed the new pattern packs. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Subsidiarity by default, burden of proof for upward scope, and recallable delegation

- EUR-Lex. *The principle of subsidiarity*, especially the rule that action should remain as close to the citizen as possible and only move upward when the higher level is demonstrably more effective.
- OECD. *Multi-level governance*, especially the emphasis on designing and implementing public investment and regional development across levels of government rather than pretending one tier can do it alone.
- Nobel Prize Outreach. *The Prize in Economic Sciences 2009 - Popular information*, especially the recognition that associations of users can govern common-pool resources with real rules and enforcement rather than defaulting every problem to either market exit or central command.

### Scope-typed governance stack: block, municipality, metro, region, nation, and planet

- OECD. *OECD Principles on Urban Policy* and related metropolitan-governance work, especially the insistence on targeting the effective scale of policy action and the evidence that fragmentation across metropolitan municipalities carries real productivity penalties.
- World Health Organization. *International health regulations*, especially the legally binding international coordination frame for disease notification and response that individual municipalities or nations cannot substitute for alone.
- UN Climate Change. *The Paris Agreement*, especially the treatment of climate as a problem requiring global treaty baselines combined with national implementation.
- United Nations. *UN Charter*, especially the continued assignment of primary peace-and-security machinery to an explicitly international layer.
- ICANN. *About the ICANN Community*, especially the example of a narrow-waist global institution that coordinates one shared technical layer without trying to administer the whole internet.

### Fiscal equalization, universal floors, and no postcode lottery

- OECD. *Making Decentralisation Work*, especially the warning that decentralization outcomes depend on institutional design rather than on devolution alone.
- OECD. *Fiscal Federalism 2022*, especially the trade-off that decentralization can improve fit and accountability while also risking stronger territorial inequality if intergovernmental finance is badly designed.
- OECD. *Fiscal Equalisation in OECD Countries* and *Fiscal equalisation and regional development policies*, especially the principle that equalization aims to let subnational governments provide similar public services at a similar tax burden despite uneven fiscal capacity and spending need.

### Editorial interpretation

The archive documents in this revision are not summaries of the sources. They are synthetic design notes derived from them and translated into Radical-Governance pattern language.

- Government of Canada. *Guideline on Service and Digital*, especially the emphasis on trustworthy, human-centered public-service operations with explicit review and accountability paths.

## Sources used for rev0423


These sources informed the new pattern packs. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Bounded automation levels, meaningful human intervention, and stop conditions

- NIST. *AI Risk Management Framework (AI RMF 1.0)*, especially the requirement to clearly define and differentiate human roles, responsibilities, and human-AI configurations ranging from fully autonomous to fully manual.
- Government of Canada. *Algorithmic Impact Assessment* tool and guidance, especially the requirements to document the degree of human involvement in decisions, identify override or intervention points, and update the assessment when scope or functionality changes.
- UK Government. *AI Playbook for the UK Government*, especially the emphasis on meaningful human control at the right stages, human validation and intervention strategies, and continuous evaluation as autonomy and operational risk increase.

### Coupled change follow-through, matrices, partial-completion warnings, and closure

- Government of Canada. *Algorithmic Impact Assessment* tool and guidance, especially the requirements to review and update assessments when functionality, scope, or context of use materially changes.
- UK Government. *AI Playbook for the UK Government*, especially lifecycle governance duties around monitoring, user-issue reporting, retraining or model update planning, and maintaining accountability beyond initial release.
- NIST AI Resource Center. *AI RMF Playbook*, especially the treatment of roles, oversight, change management, complaints, adjudication, and post-deployment documentation as linked governance responsibilities rather than isolated artifacts.

### Confusability budgets, disambiguation floors, and alias-collision registers

- U.S. Web Design System. *Typography* and plain-language guidance, especially the expectation that public-facing language be concrete, scannable, and hard to misunderstand in consequential services.
- NIST. *AI Risk Management Framework (AI RMF 1.0)*, especially the requirement that information provided to affected people and operators be meaningful, interpretable, and appropriately differentiated by context and role.
- GOV.UK. *Functional Standards / Govspeak and service writing guidance*, especially the emphasis on clear labels, explicit wording, and avoiding avoidable ambiguity in public-service communication.

- U.S. National Archives and Records Administration. *Guidance on Managing Web Records* (current), especially the records-management focus on reliability, authenticity, integrity, and usability as characteristics of trustworthy records.

- NIST. *Guide for Cybersecurity Event Recovery (SP 800-184)*, especially the use of staging systems to validate the integrity of recovered data and restore trust in the infrastructure.

## Sources used for rev0414

These sources informed the new pattern packs. They are included as compact grounding rather than exhaustive literature review.

### Redaction gateways, protected-attribute filters, and exception registers

- Canada.ca Design. *Privacy and security for AI help applications* (updated 2026), especially the requirements to minimise personal information collection, avoid retention for secondary purposes, redact personal information before sending queries to the AI service, and apply redaction patterns for threats, profanity, and manipulation.
- National Archives and Records Administration. *NARA 2025 AI Compliance Plan* (2025), especially internal safeguards prohibiting classified or sensitive data including PII and CUI in the approved assistant and forbidding high-impact AI use cases without CAIO approval.
- EUR-Lex. *Regulation (EU) 2024/1689* (AI Act), especially the prohibitions on biometric categorisation used to infer race, political opinions, trade union membership, religious or philosophical beliefs, sex life, or sexual orientation, and the prohibition on emotion-recognition uses in workplace and education contexts except for narrow lawful carve-outs.

### Subgroup floor metrics, public harm registries, and thresholds

- NIST. *Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework (AI RMF 1.0)*, especially the warning that measurement approaches can fail when they do not account for differences in affected groups and contexts and that harms may affect varied groups or sub-groups differently.
- GOV.UK. *Algorithmic Transparency Recording Standard guidance for public sector bodies* (updated 2025), especially the instruction to document bias and fairness evaluation over subgroups within the dataset and the handling of sensitive attributes and proxies.
- Accessibility Standards Canada. *CAN-ASC-6.2:2025 Accessible and Equitable Artificial Intelligence Systems*, especially the requirements for ongoing monitoring for bias or discrimination toward people with disabilities, public registries of harms and contested decisions, and thresholds for unacceptable risk and harm.
- Canada.ca. *Artificial intelligence in the hiring process* (2026), especially the accountability for decisions, the need to validate AI-generated content, remove biases and barriers, and maintain official-language parity so candidates are not disadvantaged.

### Citation-gated public chatbots, approved source hierarchies, and abstention

- Canada.ca Design. *Content guidance for AI help applications* (updated 2026), especially the requirements that AI help applications always include a citation link, that all in-scope answers include at least one authoritative citation, that responses be generated only from trusted sources such as Canada.ca, and that teams prefer routing to existing wizards over AI improvisation.
- NIST. *Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework: Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile* (2024), especially the guidance to review and verify sources and citations in outputs during testing and ongoing monitoring and to verify that retrieval-augmented-generation data is grounded.
- Canada Revenue Agency. *CRA Chatbot* (current public page), especially the framing of the tool as helping Canadians find trusted information on CRA programs and services.
- Canada.ca. *Guide on the use of generative artificial intelligence* (updated 2025), especially the risk framing for public-facing chatbots and the expectations for transparency, oversight, and alternative non-automated communication routes.
